 MAXWELL DREWITT.

 CHAPTER I.
 Brian’s Difficulties.


Mr. Gyton returned to London with his uncle, greatly to his disgust, for
he was really enamoured of Geraldine Drewitt; “But the idea of staying
on with a blue-book and a rouge-pot without you!” he remarked to Mr.
Pryor.

“Cannot you ride?” asked the barrister.

“Over those winding roads for ever? No. Human nature demands a change.”

“Cannot you fish?”

“No. I cannot live on fried trout, and ham and eggs, and whiskey, in
those districts where fish is plentiful: add to which, that I agree with
Dr. Johnson—was not it?—who said a fishing-rod had a worm at one end and
a fool at the other.”

“Can you not superintend matters at Durrow?”

“Durrow be hanged! We will come over with a party, but till then, Durrow
sees nothing of your humble servant.”

“Can you not cultivate a friendship with Harold Drewitt?”

“Much obliged, but Mr. Harold Drewitt is a young gentleman whom I admire
much more than I like. No, uncle, I return with you to London by the
Kingston and Holyhead packet, if you please.” And Mr. Francis Gyton
locked his portmanteau determinedly.

“When shall we see you in Connemara again?” asked Lady Emmeline, with
her sweetest smile.

“Not until next summer, I fear,” answered Mr. Pryor, and his hostess
looked inconsolable.

“I wish we were going to spend the winter in London,” she said, with a
sigh; and Mrs. Gyton heard of that sigh, and asked her Ladyship to visit
London after Christmas; a visit which Lady Emmeline enjoyed greatly, and
which was most probably the happiest time she ever spent during all the
years that had passed since her marriage with Maxwell Drewitt.

As for the owner of the Headlands, he felt rejoiced exceedingly when Mr.
Pryor took his leave. The visit had not turned out according to his
anticipations.

There were several matters in which he had desired Mr. Pryor’s
co-operation and assistance, but he decided that it would be more
prudent to let those matters wait.

“Twelve months can make no material difference,” argued Mr. Maxwell
Drewitt; “and when he returns, those young people at Kincorth will be
singing to a different tune. And that reminds me I may as well call
round with Ryan, and give him instructions how to proceed.”

But on the road Maxwell met Mr. Ryan coming post haste to him.

“Look here,” said the attorney—by this time a shabby, shuffling man of
close on fifty, who smelt even at the earliest hours in the morning of
whiskey, and who would now as soon have thought of calling his employer
Max, as he would of addressing Lord Marsden as “Bob.”

There had been a time when the pair were very intimate; when they stood
very close together on Fortune’s wheel; but the wheel had gone round
since that, and carried Drewitt up and Ryan down. They were very far
apart now—far as master and man can stand—and though the attorney said
“Look here,” it was not said familiarly, but rather out of the excess of
his own astonishment, with a great anxiety to know how Mr. Drewitt would
receive the contents of the letter he held out for him to read.

Maxwell glanced over it—then he looked at Ryan.

“What the devil is the meaning of this?” he said.

“That there is a fresh hand at the helm,” was the reply. “It is that
young Brian.”

“Damn him!” said Maxwell heartily. “Had not they some clause by which,
if the interest were not paid, they could sell without power of
redemption?”

“Yes, if the interest were demanded; but the interest has not been
demanded lately.”

“And curse you for a fool; why has it not been demanded?”

“By your own order, sir; by your own order, I assure you,” said Ryan.
“You were afraid of exciting Mr. Drewitt’s apprehensions, and
consequently——”

“Mr. Archibald, you mean. I am Mr. Drewitt. I am the eldest son of the
eldest son, remember that, if you please.”

“Mr. Archibald’s apprehensions,” corrected Ryan, “and——”

“You must get to know what this means,” interrupted Maxwell. “You must
go to old Neill, and ascertain all about it.”

“I have been, sir, and Neill is quite in the dark himself. This is the
last transaction he is ever to be engaged in for them. Master Brian
requested him to write to us. Had he seen him first he says he would not
have done it. He refused to give him any further information, and begged
him to send all his father’s papers back to Kincorth.”

“What will be the next move do you expect?” asked Maxwell.

“To pay off the principal.”

“They have got a backer,” muttered Maxwell Drewitt, with a shocking
oath, “and that backer is Geoffry Pryor. Are all the papers likely to go
back, Ryan?” he asked, with a certain significance, which made the other
turn a little pale.

“Neill will not give them up till his costs are paid, and he has a
pretty tidy bill against Mr. Drewitt. He thinks also if he retains the
papers he may get back the business.”

“You keep him up to that idea,” said Maxwell; “and as for this interest,
let them pay it if they can; and the principal too, for that matter,
curse them! That young fool thinks to get the better of me. So much the
worse for him, that is all.”

And Maxwell Drewitt turned on his heel, and retraced his steps towards
the Headlands, swearing to himself as he walked along. It was a new
thing for Mr. Maxwell Drewitt to find himself worsted, and he had been
worsted here.

He thought he had but to step up to Kincorth and take possession, and
behold Kincorth was as far from him as ever.

Farther perhaps—for there was, as Mr. Ryan had said, a fresh hand at the
helm, who was evidently about to follow his cousin’s example and make
the old property valuable.

“He is ploughing, he is digging, he is sowing,” Maxwell remarked to Ryan
about three months afterwards; “where does he get the money?” and Ryan’s
thoughts ran back to a time when he had put the same question to himself
with regard to Maxwell, and wondered if this younger and better Drewitt
would be equally successful with his cousin.

“It is not likely, I suppose,” remarked Mr. Ryan, “that he will do as
well as you have done.”

“No, faith,” was the reply, “he is working in chains. If the young
beggar did not richly deserve all he gets, I should pity any one who was
trying to work under my uncle. I would rather work for the devil. He
would know his own mind; a thing Archibald Drewitt never did.”

And what Maxwell Drewitt said was true.

“But for Kincorth,” wrote Brian to Mr. Pryor, “but for the sake of the
dear old place whose roof I want to keep over the heads of my father and
mother if I am able, I could sometimes almost wish I had rented a
portion of Durrow from you, and tried my luck there. No person can
imagine how hard I find it to carry any point, or to maintain it when
carried. My father thinks I am taking too much on me, and latterly even
Harold has gone over to the enemy. I have no one with me except my
mother and Geraldine. Even my mother thought I ought to defer more to my
father’s opinions, and so I had to tell her about the mortgage. Since
then she has been with me, heart and soul, and indeed but for her I
should often have felt inclined to give up in despair. But I am in the
river now, and I do not mean to sink if I can help it. We have never got
those papers back yet from Mr. Neill. He is keeping them against his
costs, and as the papers cannot be of much importance, I think I shall
not speak again to my father on the subject till you come over. Connor
is doing wonders with Durrow. If the place were his own he could not be
working harder to increase its value. My father’s health does not
improve; on the contrary, those attacks are more frequent. I have been
urging him to go to Dublin, but he seems to think I want him out of the
way. Perhaps when you are next here you may be able to persuade him to
get some first-rate advice. My speaking seems to do more harm than
good.”

And Brian was quite right. The fact of his proposing anything made Mr.
Drewitt object to it.

“It is hard upon you Brinbaldy,” said Wilhelmina, who had married years
and years before Mr. Goldich, a man far below herself in birth and
social position. “It is hard upon you. I can remember the time when my
uncle would not cut down a tree because you might not like it; when he
was going to build a house for you at Analore; when we were not allowed
to have a door open for fear the draught might kill you, precious baby
that you were; and now all the county is talking about how badly you and
he hit it off, and how sorry he is Harold is not the eldest son.”

“If Harold could keep the property together, I am sure I wish he were
the eldest,” sighed Brian.

“Dear, dear! to think about the fuss they made over you once, and to see
how that young jack-a-napes has cut you out now. My husband says, Brian,
that if he were you, he would not let Maxwell and Harold be so much
together. He says Harold will go to the dogs entirely if he is not got
out of this neighbourhood, and that you ought to see to him.”

“Can I do everything?” asked Brian. “Am I Harold’s father? am I the
owner of Kincorth? How is it possible for me to do more than I am doing?
Do you think I am master here?”

“Well, you need not be angry at me for repeating what he says; you need
not snap me up like that for giving you good advice. Go on as you
choose, only don’t blame us if any harm happens to Harold.”

“There is no harm likely to happen to Harold,” Brian would answer; but
spite of this, as often as not he went straight off to his brother, and
entreated him to stay more at home, not to drink so much, not to mix so
freely with the lower orders, but to remember he was a Drewitt, and
being such, to keep himself to himself.

“You want to make me a sneak like yourself,” the boy retorted. “You take
too much on you; you set yourself up a great deal too high; you are not
a bit like what you used to be; you are too much of the master before
your time, as Maxwell says.”

Then calmly, and without a change of colour, Brian damned Maxwell.

“We will be quits some day,” he remarked.

“When you have made your fortune out of mangold-wurzel and your fine new
English sheep, I suppose,” taunted Harold, for the English sheep had
proved failures, and the success of the mangold was problematical.

“I would rather lose money on mangolds than take it out of my wife’s
pocket,” returned Brian; and thus they wrangled on, till oftentimes the
elder brother was sick and tired of his thankless labour—of the work
which was never done, and which, in the opinion of many people, had
better never have been attempted.

It was hard; and this lad of twenty felt the difficulty of his position
keenly. He rose early, he went to bed late, he fought hard for what he
believed to be right. He did his best to fulfil his promise to Mr.
Pryor, and yet the consequence was that, when Mr. Pryor returned to
Connemara, he found Mr. Drewitt dreadfully dissatisfied with his
son—full of complaints, full of grievances.

“It is very hard for a man to be so treated by his own child, Mr.
Pryor,” sobbed the invalid. “There is no use in my making any
suggestions. He is only twenty, and I am sixty-four; yet you would
think, to see the way he gets on, he was sixty-four instead of me. I am
sure I managed my own property for many a year before he was born, but
he ignores me completely—completely, sir.”

“I hope Brian is never disrespectful,” observed the Englishman.

“No; I cannot make any complaint on that score; he only takes his own
way; he will not be guided by me. Now there’s flax. I wanted him to sow
flax; but do you think he would do it? and I ordered the men to plant
some young trees round that piece of land he is reclaiming over by the
Point. You would hardly credit it, I suppose, but he told them to go on
ploughing, and leave the trees till another season. And those trees,
sir! when he comes to lay that down in grass he will have no shelter for
his cattle; not as much as would shade a sheep.”

Mr. Pryor took the old man’s hand kindly in his own—

“My dear Mr. Drewitt,” he began, and his heart was grieved, as he spoke
both for the father and the son. “Will you go back in your own mind
twelve months, and think of your position then—of your position now? I
know, I have good reason for knowing, that had I not advanced money—had
I not, in fact, taken the mortgages on Kincorth myself—the former
mortgagee would have foreclosed ere this; and Brian knew this as well,
and the lad is now working hard to free your property from its
incumbrances. If in all cases he does not follow your advice, I am
confident it is only because he feels that whatever returns money in the
present is of more importance than any improvements calculated to
beautify the property in the future.”

“But trees, Mr. Pryor—trees are always valuable,” persisted Mr. Drewitt.

“Not until they grow,” answered Mr. Pryor, with a smile, and that smile
weakened the force of all he had said previously.

“Of course, I know nothing,” said the old man; “of course, my opinion is
of no weight. I am your debtor, sir, and Brian sees with your eyes—and
I—I might as well be in my coffin.” Having arrived at which sensible
conclusion, Mr. Drewitt burst into tears.

All in vain, Mr. Pryor begged him to consider how hard Brian was
working, how his endeavours had caused several of Mr. Drewitt’s most
troublesome creditors to cease their persecutions, how there was
comparative comfort at Kincorth.

“Good Heavens! Mr. Drewitt, what would you have?” went on the
Englishman. “How can Brian possibly do more for you than he is doing?”

“For himself, Mr. Pryor, you mean,” answered Brian’s parent. “He thinks
the estate will come to him absolutely, when I am dead and gone. It is
not right for a son to act as if his father were already dead and
buried; and I must look out for Harold’s interests—I must see to them.”

“If you would attend to Harold’s interests in the present,” returned Mr.
Pryor, whose patience was by this time exhausted, “and insist that he
should spend his days in some profitable manner, instead of drinking,
hunting, and consorting with all kinds of bad characters, it would be
more to the purpose, I think, than exalting him over the son who is
trying his best to do his duty.”

“There you are,” interrupted Mr. Drewitt, “taking Brian’s side, of
course: it only shows me more and more that I must see to Harold—poor
lad—for who would be on his side if I and his mother were gone?”

“You are right, Mr. Drewitt,” answered Mr. Pryor; “it will be better for
you to see to Harold; it will save much heartburning on the boy’s part
and much misconception on your own. I am sorry you are so ill-advised as
to believe the interests of your two sons can for the present be
separated; but I have done: every man must manage his family and his
affairs for himself, and strangers only do more harm than good by
meddling. I am sure I have,” muttered the lawyer to himself, as he
returned slowly and thoughtfully towards Durrow.

He had not, however, gained the top of the hill above Kincorth before he
heard some one calling—

“Oh! Mr. Pryor, do stop, pray, pray do!” and stopping and looking back
he saw Geraldine flying after him.

“I have run so fast,” she panted. “I want to speak to you. I want to
talk with you so much: may I walk part of the way to Durrow?”

Might she?—part of the way?—when he would have given all Durrow to have
heard her promise to walk with him all through life.

At what age does a man’s heart cease to beat faster, I wonder, when a
pretty girl—that is, the pretty girl of his especial fancy—speaks to
him?

There was a dreadful disparity between them—a quarter of a century, or
thereabouts—which made Geoffry Pryor feel occasionally that he was mad
for thinking of such a thing.

And perhaps he was mad; but he thought about Geraldine, from morning
till night, for all that.

“I do so want to talk to you about Brian,” she began. “Home is so
wretched for him, you can have no idea, Mr. Pryor—none;” and Geraldine
lifted her soft dark eyes to the Englishman’s face to see if he were
attending to what she said. “I sometimes think he would be better away,
for poor papa seems to fancy he can do nothing right—nothing.”

“Your papa is not in a fit state of health to judge accurately on any
subject,” was the reply.

“But it is so miserable for Brian.”

“Brian is doing his duty.”

“It is so hard for him to have to be doing his duty so young,” she
answered.

“He will find it all the easier when he is old,” replied Mr. Pryor; but
there was nothing unkind in his tone, harsh though the truth might be.

“Could he not do his duty any place else?” she asked. “He tells me he
could not; that he must make a struggle to keep Kincorth; that he chose
to go into battle, and that he will not turn his back on the fray: but
still, I thought I would ask you,” finished Geraldine; and the dark eyes
were lifted to his once again, and her soft Irish voice laid an accent
at once pathetic and imploring on the “you.”

“Dear child,” answered Mr. Pryor—she seemed so young and so trustful, so
childlike and so pretty, that he would have liked to gather her in his
arms and carry her to his home, out of the way of sorrow and
trouble—“you would not have your brother a coward; he has elected to
tread a rough road, but he will reach the end some day; and you must
help, and not hinder him on his way.”

“But if you knew, Mr. Pryor; if you knew—papa is so—so jealous of him,
and Harold is the same; and everybody seems against him—every—every
one.”

“What a foolish little girl you are!” he said; and the longing grew
stronger to take such a little bundle of folly to himself for ever. “I
am not against Brian, you are not against Brian, your mamma is not
against Brian, many people are very much for Brian, and think him an
amazingly fine young fellow. Besides, he is doing right, and he will be
glad hereafter to think he did strive to keep a roof over his father’s
head. Although Mr. Drewitt does not thank him for it now, Brian will be
glad to remember at some future day how he stood between his father and
the world.”

“I wish I could be of any use,” she said, with such a wistful look in
her sweet face that Geoffry Pryor would have given more than Durrow to
touch it just once with his lips.

“You can be of use,” he answered; “you can tell Brian, whenever he is
dispirited, not to lose heart: you can tell him that the evil is only
apparent while the good is real: beyond all”—he hesitated for a moment,
and then went on—“you can tell him to be kind to, and patient and
forbearing with his father; patient as he would be if he were lying on
his death-bed; because, dear Geraldine—may I call you Geraldine?”

“Yes, anything,” she replied; “because why?”

“He will not, I fear, have to exercise his patience so long as he
would——”

“Oh! Mr. Pryor—Mr. Pryor!” and the colour faded out of her face and the
tears sprang into her eyes.

“My dear, it is true,” he said; “and that thought will make both you and
him very tender and very good.”

“Papa, my poor, poor papa:” it was all she could say; but she stretched
out her hands towards Mr. Pryor, as though in this dumb fashion she were
asking his help.

“God knows when it may be,” he went on, taking her hands, “but you must
be prepared any day, my poor child—Geraldine.”

“Mamma, mamma!” and Geraldine drew her hands away from him and covered
her face from sight; “mamma, mamma! I think I will go home now,” she
added after a pause. “I think I will go back to him: thank you, Mr.
Pryor;” and she left him crying bitterly.

 CHAPTER II.
 At the Dawning.


It was easy enough to see from Geraldine’s manner that although she had
never put the precarious state of her father’s health before herself
plainly, yet that she had vaguely dreaded the worst.

As a rule, our fears travel so much faster than actual danger, that
before danger absolutely appears in sight, we are well prepared for its
advent; and there was not one of Mr. Drewitt’s family, unless indeed it
might be Harold, who had not been long prepared for the worst.

Mrs. Drewitt, and Brian and Geraldine had known, for many a day, that he
could never be better. Once admit the idea that improvement is
impossible, and it is not difficult to imagine that the last change of
all is probable.

There are times when it is the truest wisdom to look upon our living as
dying; when it is well to meet the mournful truth boldly and without
disguise; when it is well to buckle on all our charity and forbearance;
to call together love, and duty, and tenderness, so that we may not pain
the departing by opposition which might be imperative for their sakes as
well as our own, were they not already at the mouth of that river where
all human leave-takings must be ended.

“If we had but known how ill he was—If we had only imagined he was
dying!”

“Had we thought he was in danger!”

How often we hear these sentences—happy only if we have never had need
to utter them ourselves!

Geoffry Pryor resolved that Geraldine should have no sorrow of this
kind. It was right for her to understand, and yet the intelligence did
not seem strange to her.

She had feared—and the fear was true—and she went down the hill towards
Kincorth, crying, as I have said, bitterly.

As she went Mr. Pryor began to marvel whether he had done right;
whether, after all, Mr. Drewitt’s case was so hopeless; but then he
remembered many an unfailing symptom which assured him that Kincorth
would soon be in want of a new master.

“And the sooner the better, say I,” was the conclusion he arrived at;
but then his conscience chid him, and he added, “though God knows best.”

As for Harold, nothing could induce him to think badly of his father’s
state.

“You told me more than a year ago he could not be cured, and he is alive
now,” he argued.

“I said I feared he never could be better, and I was right,” answered
Brian.

“Well, he may live for a hundred years yet,” was the reply. “He is
dying—you are dying—I am dying; but although we are dying we are not
near our deaths; at least I hope not,” added Harold; and he went
whistling out of the gates, to seek for more cheerful companionship
abroad.

He did not like all work and no play, not he; give him a good tumbler of
punch, a pretty country-girl for partner, and a jolly good dance up at
Regan’s—a public-house on the way to Cragantlet, where he and a lot of
other young fellows got together—and Kincorth might go hang.

 “‘For I’m a fine young Irish gentleman,
 All of the ancient school,’”

Master Harold would shout out as he came reeling home, arm-in-arm with
three or four more as drunk as himself; and Brian, who had never taken a
glass too much wine in his life, looked at his brother and asked
helplessly—“What can I do? what can I do?”

He never knew where to find Harold when he wanted him; and one night
when the worst, so long apprehended, had to be faced, Brian, flinging
himself on the fastest thing in the stable—Madcap—knew no more where to
go to look for his brother than if he were the greatest stranger in the
world.

“There is a dance up at Regan’s to-night, sir,” said the man who had
saddled the chesnut; “and if you’ll ride up there, I’ll cut across the
hills to Durrow, for Mr. Maxwell’s dining with Mr. Pryor, and should
Master Harold not be at Regan’s he’ll be there.”

“Which is the nearest road to Regan’s?” Brian asked, stroking the neck
of the chesnut, who was one of the most fidgety brutes a man ever
undertook to ride.

“Straight on till Cragantlet, then take the turn before you come to the
gates, and keep on about half-a-mile. He’s nearly sure to be there; but
if he’s not there, you might call at Dan Glynn’s as you’re comin’ back.”

“I’ll call there as I’m going,” was the reply; for Dan Glynn was an
individual who resided about a mile inland from Duranmore, and who was
reputed to make his living by less honest means than tilling bad land or
sending lean sheep to Ballinasloe fair for the Leinster graziers to buy
and fatten.

It was a dark night for August, but Brian knew every inch of the road,
and so for that matter did the chesnut, who brought him to the Moor Farm
in less than a quarter of an hour.

Brian rode straight up to the door and, without dismounting, rapped for
admittance with the handle of his whip.

There were no lights to be seen, there was no one stirring about the
place; but the youth knew Mr. Glynn’s habits, and not thinking these at
all sufficient reasons for supposing the household had retired to rest,
he jumped to the ground, and with whip and boots made such vigorous
onslaughts on the door as soon brought Mr. Glynn out of his bed to
inquire what the —— he wanted.

“I want my brother, Glynn; do you know where he is?”

“The Lord be good to me, it’s Mister Brian. I beg your pardon, sir, I’m
sure; I thought it was some damned gauger. No, I haven’t seen Masther
Harold, not these two days. Anything wrong, sir? can I be of any help?”

“No, thank you,” answered Brian, who had one foot already in the
stirrup. “I’ll go on to Regan’s.”

“How’s the Masther, Mister Brian?”

“Dying, Glynn,” was the reply that came back through the night, for the
chesnut was galloping down the cross road leading to Duranmore, and
Brian turned round in his saddle to shout out these words.

“Faith, then, an’ if he’s dyin’ it’s not Dan Glynn that’ll go back till
his bed, an’ sleep contint. I’ll follow the boy: shure an’ it’s at a
time like this a man stands in need of company, let him be rich or let
him be poor.” And straightway Mr. Glynn “clapped a saddle” on his black
pony Tom, and cantered away to Cragantlet in pursuit of Brian Drewitt.

Brian, meantime, had gone galloping on under the darkness. He gave the
chesnut her head, and she went tearing away as though she thought he was
riding a steeple-chase. Over those lonely roads, what did it matter?
Among those desolate hills, who cared if he rode like a fiend through
the night? The same country as Maxwell had traversed, while the moon
shone down on lake, and hill, and bog, and ravine, Brian now dashed
through without turning his eyes to right or left. He thought of his
father—of the great mystery—of how hard he had tried to do his duty—and
of how his reward was—

“Harold, Harold,” but never once, “Brian, my son.”

And now his father was going where all his eldest born had tried to
effect could benefit him no more. He would never see Kincorth clear of
debt; never know how disinterested Brian had been; never understand how
this boy-man had worked to free him from anxiety; never comprehend how
wild and reckless, how selfish and thoughtless, his favourite son was
growing up.

“It is best as it is,” thought Brian, bitterly; “better for him to die
than for Harold to break his heart!” And as he thought this, the sound
of music was borne down the lane, and he heard shouting and laughing and
the noise of the dancers’ feet as they were thumped at regular intervals
upon the earthen floor.

“Hold my horse, boy,” he shouted to an urchin standing by the open door,
and the boy ran forward and took the mare, and was about to lead her
round to the yard, when Brian stopped him.

“Don’t put her up,” he said, “I only want to speak a word inside. I
shall be out again in a minute,” and he strode into the house, a
finer-looking man than Harold was ever likely to grow to.

“Is my brother within, Mrs. Regan?” he asked of the landlady, who met
him at the door.

“Mr. Brian! an’ it’s welcome ye are, sir. Your brother? I’m not just
sure, sir; will ye step in and sit down while I see?”

“I’ll see for myself, Mrs. Regan, if you please,” answered Brian, gently
putting the landlady on one side. “You need not be afraid; I’m not going
to scold the boy; nobody is going to be angry with him, only his father
wants to see him before he dies!”

“God save us all, is he that bad, Mr. Brian?” But Brian had already
flung open the kitchen-door and was among the merrymakers.

In a moment every dancer stood still, every voice was hushed, and only
the piper who had been playing Paddy O’Rafferty went on with his tune,
unmindful of the intruder.

“Hush! Jamsie,” said one in his ear, “it is the young master;” and the
piper stopped.

Although it was summer, a tremendous turf-fire blazed on the hearth, and
collected together in the kitchen were a number of pretty young
country-girls, young farmers, labourers, and two or three gentlemen’s
sons, foremost among whom stood Harold.

There were wooden forms round the room for the convenience of the
lookers on; there was whiskey in abundance; one or two dogs lay on the
hearth, and the blind piper was accommodated with a seat in a dark
corner.

“Welcome, Brian!” shouted out Harold, boisterously; “welcome! we’ll get
you a pretty partner and drink your health with three times three.”

“Not to-night, Harold,” answered Brian. “I want you a minute; come
here;” and thinking all the time of the room in which he had left his
father, of the contrast between the one scene and the other, he led his
brother into the passage, and told him he must hurry home.

Like one listening in a dream, Harold heard all Brian said in silence.

In silence also he allowed himself to be taken out of the house into the
darkness, to the spot where the chesnut was standing.

“Don’t spare her, Harold,” said Brian, helping his brother into the
saddle; “lose no time.”

“But you, Brian—but you?” and Harold was on the ground again.

“I’ll walk back,” was the answer. “Hang it! can’t you do as I tell you
for once? Jump up; it is you he wants to see—not me.”

And Brian almost lifted his brother into his seat as he spoke.

“Are you all right now?” he asked.

“Yes, only I wish——”

Brian struck the mare on her flank, and the creature was off through the
darkness, through the night, bearing Harold towards Kincorth.

A mile after leaving Regan’s, Brian met Glynn. “Take my pony, Master
Brian,” he said; “I brought him up here on purpose, and I can send to
Kincorth for him in the morning.”

“And you, Glynn?”

“I can tramp it,” was the answer; and Brian accepted his offer; only as
he rode he wondered how much of all the respect and civility which was
accorded to him he owed to the fact of his being the eldest son, the
next heir.

“It is Harold they like,” he reflected, “and not me.”

But Brian was wrong; the country-people liked Harold much, but they
liked him better. They were fond of the free-mannered impetuous boy, but
they were fonder still of the young gentleman who was doing his best so
soon; who was out with the labourers in the morning, and was overseeing
the men, and planning this and measuring that, from sunrise to sunset.

“Doing his best to keep the ould place from the stranger,” as they
pathetically said.

It was two o’clock before Brian reached home. Glynn’s pony cast a shoe
six miles from Kincorth, and the young man had been obliged to walk him
every step of those six miles home.

“An’ ye never stopped to pick up the shoe, Master Brian,” said Nannie,
who was now stooped with age, and old and withered, regretfully. “I only
hope no harm will come of it. Won’t you eat a bite before you go up to
him? He has been asking for you.”

“For my brother, I suppose you mean,” answered Brian; “is he not with
him?”

“Yes; but it has been Brian, Brian, this last two hours. You look white
like, won’t you have a drop of something?”

“No, Nannie, not now; let me alone, like a good soul. Where is my
mother?”

“Where wud she be but wid him?” was the reply; and Brian ascended the
staircase and entered his father’s room.

There lay Mr. Drewitt, waiting for the final summons; there sat Mrs.
Drewitt, and Harold, and Geraldine, and Doctor Sheen. From side to side
the dying man tossed restlessly. “Brian, Brian!” he cried; and Brian
answered, “I am here.”

“Will you take care of your mother, Brian, and your sister, and—and
Harold?”

“I will, father,” and the young man bowed his head as though he felt
himself in the presence of something mightier than mortality.

“Will you be good to him, patient with him?”

“I will,” and Brian Drewitt took his father’s hand.

“Swear it,” murmured the dying man.

“As I hope for peace here and salvation hereafter, I swear!” said Brian.

“I have left you all,” Mr. Drewitt muttered feebly; “perhaps it is not
right—and I did not mean to do it—but you will be good to him, Brian,
you will?”

“Before God!” was the reply.

“And may He enable you to fulfil your vow!” said a voice behind him; and
Brian, looking round, saw the Rector of Duranmore, who laid a kindly
hand on his shoulder.

“My boy,” he whispered, “you have undertaken great responsibilities this
night; but God will give you strength if you ask for His help.”

And then they all knelt down, while the clergyman prayed that
intensely-pathetic prayer which is ordered, in the visitation of the
sick, for those for whom there appeareth small hope of recovery.

“Amen,” said the sick man, but there was no other Amen, only sobs from
the women and Harold, and a profound silence as regarded Brian and
Doctor Sheen.

“Amen!”—the sands in the glass had almost run out; the sun of his life
was close upon its setting; the river of his existence was flowing out
into the sea.

Amen! His day was declining; his grass withering. This man was going to
his long home, and the mourners were gathered round about him.

Amen! He could not take up his life again and make a better thing of it;
he could not go back through the years and redress the wrong, and undo
the misery he had brought on others. For him in this world there was no
future, only a barren and unprofitable past.

Amen! Throw open the shutters, and fling up the windows, that he may
look his last in the grey light of morning, over the country he has
loved so long.

“Lift me up,” he said feebly; and they raised him up that he might see
the trees standing quietly in the first blush of dawn—the sea lying
still as a glassy lake—the grand old mountains and Eversbeg’s Head.

From the far-off mountains, from the nearer hills, from the sea and the
woods, and the cold grey sky, the dying man’s eyes came back slowly to
the room where was a “sound of weeping and a noise of lamentation.”

Maxwell had come in and stood beside the bed, waiting till his uncle
should notice him. Against an old-fashioned escritoire leant Mr. Pryor,
who felt himself an intruder and a stranger in the midst of such a
scene. The Rector supported the sick man, and whispered some words of
consolation to him. He saw the end coming; he knew the moment when the
keepers of the house tremble, and the strong men bow themselves, and the
grinders cease, was close at hand; and though he could not hear what the
dying man was muttering, he yet liked to see his lips move, hoping it
was in prayer.

He looked very peaceful; his face was calm and placid; but as he brought
back his eyes from the Bennebeola mountains, from Eversbeg and the
nearer hills, his glance fell on Maxwell, who stepped closer to speak to
him.

But his uncle turned from him, and calling out “Brian—Brian!” fell
forward into the arms of his first-born.

And over his father’s grey head, which lay on his bosom, Brian looked
defiantly at his cousin. There was no man living could step between them
now; in that hour his son was closer to Archibald Drewitt than the wife
of his bosom.

As the magnet attracts iron, so the stronger nature attracts the weaker
in the hour of its most grievous need.

No man could separate the father and his child now—for still the grey
head lay on Brian’s breast, growing heavier—heavier each moment.

There was a great silence in the chamber, and then Doctor Sheen walked
round the bed, and taking one cold hand in his, felt the pulse.

It was gone—the “silver cord was loosed, and the golden bowl broken, and
the pitcher broken at the fountain, and the wheel broken at the
cistern.”

The dust had returned to the earth, and the spirit unto God who gave it.

 CHAPTER III.
 After the Funeral.


It was all over—another Drewitt was gone from Kincorth—another Drewitt
was about to swell that grim assemblage who lay shrouded and coffined in
Eversbeg Abbey, waiting for the judgment—and through the darkened rooms
and about the accustomed passages the family moved slowly and
mournfully. Not one was glad Archibald Drewitt had passed to his last
home—not one—child—servant—tenant—relative, unless indeed it might be
Maxwell Drewitt, who was, his wife remarked, a little more irritable
than usual, if that were possible.

Naturally Brian took the management of affairs. He ordered a hearse from
Galway, the nearest town where such a luxury was obtainable. He
despatched intimations of the death to all the gentry in the
neighbourhood; he sent hat-bands and scarfs to all the clergy,
dissenting, Roman Catholic and Protestant, within reach, (you perceive,
reader, more devolves upon the relations in Ireland than in England,)
and he saw that popular requirements were attended to in the kitchen and
servants’ hall.

Brian was not one to throw disrespect on his father’s memory by
neglecting any of the customary conventionalities, and accordingly,
while the corpse was watched up stairs by a profusion of ancient women,
in a room lighted by wax candles, whiskey was drunk _ad libitum_ in the
servants’ department, and the house slept neither by day nor by night.

There is nothing in which Ireland differs more from England than in her
observances concerning the dead; there is nothing which grates more
against English feeling than the riot and disorder of an Irish household
after a death; while the stately and undemonstrative propriety of an
establishment on this side of the water, where there is mourning for the
departed, strikes the Hibernian as cold and unfeeling.

In Kincorth the popular taste was allowed to run riot. By the lower
orders, Archibald Drewitt had been idolized; by the lower orders, his
loss was lamented exceedingly. It is possible for a vast amount of
regret to co-exist with an unlimited supply of whiskey, and Duranmore
sorrowed over Archibald Drewitt exceedingly.

As for the family, what more can I say than that they mourned—that Mrs.
Drewitt’s grief was silent, and Harold’s passionate, and Brian’s
stern—that the wife bowed down her head and wept for the husband of her
youth—that the children bewailed themselves for the father they should
never in this world see more?

All the latter and more unamiable part of Archibald Drewitt’s life was
by common consent ignored—his family, his tenantry, his dependents, and
society generally, never spoke of the man who, grey and bent, full of
fancies and of grievances, had gone about among them, but rather they
talked of the genial Irish gentleman—of the loving, gracious parent—of
the kindly, reckless master, who had dwelt with them for so long.

“An’ it’s Mr. Brian that will be the proper man to fill his shoes,”
crooned the old people over the turf-fire in the kitchen; “though he is
not so free as his father, and is quieter like, still it’s the cliver
man he’ll make, and it’s the fine place Kincorth will be yit, God bliss
it! The poor Masther had not the same sinse as his son, and he went
through a power of money too, intirely.”

“But the poor man niver was turned from his dure.”

“An’ he niver spoke a hard word to man, woman, or child.”

“An’ he was easily served.”

“An’ he had always the kind look and the pleasant smile, meet him where
you would.”

“An’ shure, it wasn’t his fault if he niver looked afther the dirty
money. He wasn’t reared to the like.”

“Ay, but it ‘ud be betther now for Mr. Brian if his father had thought
o’ them that was to come afther him.”

“Ye can’t both spend and have,” remarked one man, oracularly; “an’ it’s
no use frettin’ about the goold that can niver be brought back again. He
was good to many a poor body the time of the distress, and it will be
remimbered till him now, God rest his soul!” and the speaker crossed
himself.

Thus they talked over the turf-fire while the whiskey circulated, and
the servants, who were called away at intervals, returned to communicate
any strange piece of intelligence that had been made known to them
upstairs.

How the mistress was looking—how Master Harold was taking on—how Miss
Geraldine had fallen fast asleep on the drawing-room sofa, “tired out
wid the grief, poor thing”—how the corpse was looking more and more
beautiful—how they did say the funeral was going to be the grandest
thing ever seen in West Connemara.

Brian Drewitt had determined in his own soul that what the people said
should prove true. He would do honour to his father. No man should
whisper that a Drewitt of Kincorth went to his long home with a badge of
poverty on his coffin, though that badge had been won for himself by
years of prodigality—of reckless expenditure—of senseless profusion.

Poor they were, heaven knew—poor in consequence of his father’s bad
management; but they would scrape together enough to bury him, as he
himself might have desired, right royally—as the Drewitts had been wont
to travel to their vault in Eversbeg Abbey, down by the sea.

The fourth day was that fixed for the funeral. Like Abraham, the Irish
would lose no time about burying their dead “out of their sight.” Not
out of their memories, for there is no country in the world where the
dead are remembered longer, or mourned for more earnestly. Only out of
their sight; and this speedy interment, which so shocks and astonishes
the English, has, I think, its origin in the different customs of the
two countries with regard to the bodies of the deceased.

Till earth receives earth, the Irish never willingly leave a corpse
alone for a moment. Till the grave is closed, nothing in a house of any
kind, sort, or description, can go on as usual.

Now, human nature cannot remain with the clay, whence the soul has
departed, beyond a certain period; further, an establishment cannot sit
up for ever. Moreover, the novelty even of a death wears off. It is like
a state visit, exciting for the time, but apt to grow wearisome if long
protracted; and for all these reasons, and for many more of the same
kind, which it would take too long to mention, a “rest day, a guest day,
and a prest day,” are the utmost measure of time that a body honoured,
as is the case in Ireland, ought to intrude its presence on the family.

The prest day is a stretch of courtesy across the channel; but not to
fail in due respect, Brian accorded it to his father’s remains.

He required the time to arrange matters to his liking, and when the
morning came he had everything prepared to do honour to the man who was
going, after all these years, to lie down beside Nicholas Drewitt, and
George Drewitt, and all the long line of Drewitts who were waiting in
Eversbeg Abbey for that hour when the “heavens and the earth shall flee
away, and the sea give up her dead, and every mountain and island be
moved out of their place.”

It was a magnificent funeral; never had such a one passed through
Duranmore before. All the gentry for twenty miles round came in their
private carriages, drawn by the best horses their stables could turn
out; people who had not carriages came on cars, in dogcarts, in gigs, on
horseback. All the men who had ever paid rent to Archibald Drewitt,
though many of them had passed with his lands into the hands of
strangers, came to do honour to him now; all Maxwell Drewitt’s tenantry,
all Lady Emmeline’s, all Mr. Pryor’s, all the tradesfolk out of
Duranmore, all the fishermen, all the labourers, fell into rank behind
the black hearse, with its nodding plumes, with its four stately horses,
that led the way to Eversbeg.

Under the trees that bent their branches over the road; round the bay
where the waves came rippling up on the shore; through Duranmore—where
the dead had once made himself so busy about rival candidates, about
forgotten politics—past the cottage where Ryan had again taken up his
abode; over the hill beyond Duranmore, and down into the hollow where
the Abbey stood lonely and desolate, the funeral procession wound sadly
and slowly.

It was as fine a day as ever shone upon the earth; the sunbeams fell in
great patches on the tombstones, on the monuments, on the grass in
Eversbeg graveyard; the great mountains lifted their heads to the blue
sky grandly and unsympathizingly as ever; outside Eversbeg Bay the
Atlantic lay calm and quiet as a lake. The ivy about the east window
waved its branches as the light breeze passed by: frightened at the
incursion of such a multitude, the birds flew out with a great noise and
twitter, while the sheep bounded away like wild deer over the broken
wall and up the hill-side to a convenient distance, where they turned
and looked down upon the scene below as though it had been a spectacle
and they the spectators.

The rusty gate of the churchyard stood wide, but the hearse could not
pass through it, and the nearest of kin accordingly bore the coffin into
the centre of the ruins, preceded by the Rector of Duranmore, who walked
slowly along, telling to the sea and to the mountains, to the sunshine
and to the mourners, the one great truth that shall endure when there is
no more sea and no more sorrow—

“He that believeth in me, though he were dead yet shall he live, and
whosoever liveth and believeth in me shall never die.”

They placed the coffin on trestles, in what had once been the centre of
the Abbey; and there, with no covering but the sky, in the midst of that
ruined church, with nettles and grass underfoot, with the elder-tree
spreading abroad its branches in the chancel, with ferns growing where
the holy water had wont to be placed, the funeral service proceeded.

Think, oh! reader, of what that service must have sounded at such a time
in such a place.

Pigmies seemed the men in comparison to the eternal hills, but yet the
Rector lifted up his voice, and declared—

“‘Lord, thou hast been our refuge from one generation to another, before
the mountains were brought forth, or ever the earth and the world were
made. Thou art God from everlasting and world without end.’”

Many a one present sobbed when that psalm was read out. As David bowed
the heart of all the men of Judah even as the heart of one man, so
almost as one man that great assemblage was moved at the words of the
sweet singer of Israel. But there were two present whose eyes never
moistened, but who looked straight at one another over the coffin, each
seemingly keeping watch on his fellow.

It was the same when they stood beside the vault; and when the Rector
said, with a perhaps unintentional emphasis—

“‘Thou knowest, Lord, the secrets of our hearts,’” more than one of the
group round the grave noticed the look Brian Drewitt gave Maxwell.

“What’s up, do you suppose?” whispered Connor to Dan Glynn, who chanced
to be close at his elbow.

“I don’t know,” was the reply; “but did you not see the shove Maxwell
gave him when he went to take the right hand in the procession? I
thought coffin and all would have been over.”

“Have you any idea what it means?”

“No good,” said Dan, prophetically; and very vaguely Mr. Pryor was
thinking the same thing.

He could not imagine what had gone wrong, but he felt satisfied the day
would not pass over peaceably; and when the service was finished, when
all that man could ever do for Archibald Drewitt was accomplished, when
the living had left the dead to take his long rest under the shadow of
the hills, within sound of the great sea, the barrister, sitting in the
same carriage with the cousins, would have given much to know the
probable result of the next few hours.

“Can he have any power we know nothing of?” speculated Mr. Pryor: “have
they had any quarrel unheard of by me?” and he ran over possible and
impossible circumstances likely to account for the hostile attitude
assumed by both.

Almost in silence the four drove back to Kincorth. In one corner of the
carriage, with his back to the horses, Harold was crying. Beside him
Brian sat with his arms crossed, staring defiantly at Maxwell, who,
employed in biting his nails down to below the quick, never troubled
himself to open his lips to any one.

But as they passed through the entrance gates and up under the arching
trees to the house, Mr. Pryor noticed that Maxwell bent forward and
looked out—out over lawn and field and garden, with the air of triumph
he had noticed once before.

“What can be the meaning of it?” marvelled Mr. Pryor, but he was not
left very long in doubt.

Had Brian not been a drop’s blood to the late owner of Kincorth, Maxwell
could not more completely have ignored his existence than was the case.

“Pull up those blinds,” were the first words he spoke when he entered
the house.

“Do nothing of the kind, Robert,” said Brian, and once again he and
Maxwell exchanged glances of defiance.

“Shall I go away?” asked Mr. Pryor; “you would rather, I am sure, not
have any stranger present when the will is read?”

“No, stay, if you please,” answered Brian. “I feel as if something were
going to happen, though I have not the remotest idea what. Mr. James
Butler will read the will. Shall we get it over at once?”

They passed into the dining-room, where Mr. Butler was already seated,
with one sealed packet and several loose papers lying before him. As
Mrs. Drewitt and Geraldine entered he rose, and remained standing till
they had taken their places. For a minute afterwards indeed he stood as
if irresolute, but then drawing up his chair to the table, he said,
addressing himself to Mr. Pryor—

“I have looked, at Mr. Brian Drewitt’s desire, through all the papers
left by his late father, and can discover no trace of a will likely to
be available at the present moment. I find two or three rough drafts,
which were prepared for him by his former legal adviser, Mr. Bartholomew
Neill, none of which Mr. Neill informs me met with his approval. It
would seem as though he had intended to make some separate provision for
his younger son, but he has failed to carry out that design. An
unfortunate difference between my father and the late Mr. Drewitt
prevented our having such an intimate knowledge of his affairs as might
now be desirable. Mr. Neill has intimated that a will was made by him,
and executed by Mr. Drewitt, which will it would seem as though Mr.
Drewitt must subsequently have destroyed, as I can discover no vestige
of it.”

“What is that sealed packet there, Mr. Butler?” asked Maxwell.

“It is a will, dated on the cover, 1818, which would of course be
inoperative at the present juncture, since Mr. Drewitt’s marriage took
place fourteen years subsequently, and marriage renders all wills made
Pryor to that event null and void.”

“Not all wills, Mr. Butler,” said Mr. Pryor.

“Read this one, at all events,” broke in Brian Drewitt; and Mr. Butler
took up the packet. “But stop a moment,” he added. “Suppose it should be
of no effect; what would then be the result?”

“Just the same as though your father had died intestate.”

“And if he had died intestate?”

“You would succeed, of course.”

Across the room Brian looked defiantly at Maxwell, who returned the look
calmly and steadily.

“Had not we better proceed to business?” he said, addressing Mr. Butler.
“I shall be glad to return home as soon as possible.”

“And so shall I,” murmured Wilhelmina, thinking of her last baby.

Mr. Pryor walked across the room and seated himself beside Brian, while
Mr. Butler broke the seals, took out the enclosure, and began.

There is no need for me, reader, to weary you with the contents of that
will. It had been made shortly after Mr. Drewitt’s accession to
Kincorth, and set forth that as he, the testator, had succeeded to the
property to the exclusion of his eldest brother and his family, he
desired to restore the property to the elder branch after his death.
Never being likely to marry, (he proceeded to declare,) it was his
desire that all his estates, real and personal, should pass to his
brother; or, in the event of his brother’s decease, to Maxwell, son of
the said George Drewitt, chargeable with so-and-so and so-and-so.

It was a tremendously lengthy document, and Mr. James Butler read on
through the catalogue of estates that had now most of them passed into
other hands—wearily and rapidly.

“I should think my father must have paid for it by the foot,” he was
considering, when suddenly he turned over the last page and caught sight
of something that made him pause, and glance across at Brian.

“Go on,” said the young man; “I am attending,” and Mr. Butler went on—to
signed, sealed, and delivered, when he stopped once again.

“Is that all?” asked Mr. Pryor.

“No, there is a codicil.”

“Of what date?” inquired the barrister, rising and standing with both
hands on the table, while Maxwell’s eyes grew darker and darker, and the
veins in his forehead swelled and blackened—“of what date?”

“September 28th, 1832, more than three months after Mr. Drewitt’s
marriage.”

“What is the codicil?” said Brian.

“Read it,” suggested Mr. Pryor, and Mr. Butler read on.

When he had finished, every man present looked in the face of his
neighbour, for the codicil confirmed the will. It mentioned Mrs.
Drewitt’s jointure; all present were clear on this point, for Mr. Butler
was requested by Mr. Pryor to read it over again. Then the barrister
took the will and looked at it.

“When will Brian be of age, Mrs. Drewitt?” he asked, turning towards the
widow.

“The third of next April,” she replied.

“And what is the date of the codicil, Mr. Butler?” was his next
question.

“September the 28th, sir,” answered Mr. Butler.

Mr. Pryor went through a mental calculation, through every turn of which
Maxwell followed him.

“Did not the 28th fall on a Sunday?” he said, suddenly addressing
Maxwell; but Maxwell was not to be trapped.

“I am sure I cannot tell,” he said, and he thrust his hands into the
very bottom of his pockets.

“The best thing, it seems to me, now that we can do is to send for Mr.
Neill,” remarked Mr. Pryor.

“I doubt if he will come,” said Mr. Butler.

“Who was Mr. Drewitt’s solicitor in 1832?” asked the barrister.

“My father,” answered Mr. James Butler.

“Oh!” remarked Mr. Pryor; then he said to Mr. James Butler, “Is there
anything more to be done?” and Mr. James Butler said that there was not,
so far as he knew.

“Then we need not detain Mrs. Drewitt: all this must be very unpleasant
to her,” was Mr. Pryor’s next observation, and he held the door open for
her and her daughter to pass out.

“What does it all mean, Mr. Pryor?” she whispered. “I do not understand
it.”

“I do not exactly understand it myself yet,” he replied. “We must make
inquiries about the missing will.”

And he walked back into the dining-room, without, however, closing the
door behind him.

“I suppose we cannot do any good by standing here, looking at one
another,” he said, making a sign to Brian to remain silent, while
Maxwell marched across the apartment, and taking one hand out of his
pocket slammed to the door.

“I wish to make a remark,” he commenced, returning to his former
position. “I wish to state that this will has taken me unawares.”

“You lie!” interrupted Brian.

“I have not the slightest doubt on the point, Mr. Drewitt,” answered Mr.
Pryor. “And if such a proceeding were not illegal, I should say the best
thing you could do would be to put this will in the fire, and proceed as
though your uncle had died intestate.”

Maxwell bit his lip, but putting a curb on his temper, he proceeded—

“I shall do nothing rashly, I shall make no hasty promises. I must think
over the position in which this will places me, before committing myself
in any way. Thirty-seven years have elapsed since my father was
disinherited in favour of Brian’s father, and now, by a curious
fatality, Brian is disinherited for me. The estates were then valuable;
now, most of those estates are owned by strangers, and the property
which is left is heavily mortgaged, as no one knows better than you, Mr.
Pryor. With a large rent-roll, my uncle did nothing for us. Out of the
little his extravagance has left, I will, however, do something for his
children, if they permit me.”

“You shall never do anything for me,” retorted Brian. “I will never take
a sixpence from you; nor shall one belonging to me, if I can help it.”

“Be silent, Brian,” entreated Mr. Pryor.

“I will not be silent,” said Brian. “He has cheated us somehow; if that
other will be destroyed, he had some hand in making away with it. He has
determined for years and years and years to own Kincorth; and he has got
it. Curse you, Maxwell Drewitt! curse you, for a false-hearted,
double-faced, lying scoundrel!”

“Don’t stop so soon,” sneered Maxwell; “it is safe to insult a man in
his own house.”

“Then if it be your house, may it prove a sorrowful one to you! may you
never have an hour’s happiness in it! as you are childless, so may you
die desolate!”

“Take that, you foul-tongued young beggar,” said Maxwell, and he struck
him a back-handed blow across the mouth.

“Oh! Brian, Brian!” cried Harold, and he flew at Maxwell like a tiger.

But Maxwell put the boy aside, and holding him from him at arm’s length,
went on—

“Take your protégé with you, Mr. Pryor, out of my sight; and keep him
out of it, or I may do him an injury. Is it my fault that his father was
a fool? Is it my fault that Kincorth should come back to its rightful
owner? If Brian thinks it does not now belong to me in law, as it always
did in equity, let him contest the point. For my part, I swear that,
while I have a shilling, he shall never now own an acre of my land.”

“You have done for yourself, at any rate, Brian,” remarked Mr. Goldich,
taking the young man by the arm, and forcibly leading him out of the
room. “What an idiot you must be,” went on Mr. Maxwell Drewitt’s
brother-in-law, “not to stick up to him! because, if the other will be
destroyed—and I have not the remotest doubt in life myself but that your
father did put it in the fire when he took that notion about
Harold—where are you without his help—just tell me that?”

But Brian refused to answer that or any other question which Mr. Goldich
might feel inclined to propound.

He said, “I wish to heaven you would leave me alone!” and with this
explicit declaration separated himself from his relative at the foot of
the staircase.

Very slowly and very wearily he ascended to his own apartment, while Mr.
Goldich returned to the dining-room.

Maxwell met him in the doorway.

“You came here to hear the will read, I believe, sir,” he observed.

“Yes—with Wilhelmina,” answered Mr. Goldich.

“Then, as you have heard the will read, you and Wilhelmina can both go
home.”

“Well, I’m damned!” exclaimed Mr. Goldich; and he never put his foot
inside Kincorth again.

 CHAPTER IV.
 Farewell.


The first thing Maxwell Drewitt did when he got rid of his visitors was
to order a room to be prepared for his accommodation.

“And, Robert, tell Flynn to put the saddle on Madcap, and to bring her
round directly.”

“Yes, sir.”

“Give my compliments to Mrs. Drewitt, and say I shall be glad to speak
to her alone for a few minutes.”

“Yes, sir.”

And he walked up and down the room until Robert reappeared.

“Mrs. Drewitt’s compliments, sir, and she is in the breakfast-parlour.”

That was the first note of war on Mrs. Drewitt’s part; and Maxwell
accepted it as such.

Nevertheless, he repaired to the breakfast-parlour, where Mrs. Drewitt
was talking to Mr. Pryor.

“I thought,” said Maxwell, “I saw you leave with Mr. Butler.”

“I only went so far as the gate. I wished to speak to Mrs. Drewitt
before I returned to Durrow; but I have said all I had to say, and am
going directly.”

“Not on my account, I hope,” answered Maxwell, with a great accession of
civility. “I merely wanted to tell Mrs. Drewitt that I trust she will
consider Kincorth her home for the present. I have no desire to make her
position at all unpleasant. I am quite willing to meet her half-way in
any reasonable plan for the future. And if Brian will apologize for his
extraordinary conduct, I am ready to forgive and forget.”

“But I am not, Maxwell,” she answered. “You have always hated me; and
you have always hated my children. Twenty-one years ago you told me you
disliked me, and why? For twenty-one years you have been scheming and
plotting to get us out of Kincorth; and your schemes and your plots have
been successful. I cannot forget, and I cannot forgive. As if it were
not trouble enough to lose my husband, I must also see my children
beggars.” At which point Mrs. Drewitt burst into tears, and Maxwell
answered—

“When my father lost this property he accepted that loss without a
murmur. Many a one now living can tell you that. It is not half so hard
for your sons as it was for my father, for they are unencumbered, and he
was married and had a family: you have your jointure; my father had not
a sixpence. Kincorth, and the other farms and estates, which we have
usually put under one head and called Kincorth, brought in at that day
fifteen thousand a year. Without mortgages its nett value was probably
eight thousand a year. Am I stating my case fairly, Mr. Pryor?”

“I have no reason to suppose otherwise,” was the reply.

“After thirty-seven years Kincorth returns to the elder branch
absolutely valueless; so valueless that I declare to you, were it not
that it has been in the family for more generations than I could tell
you, I would let Mr. Pryor foreclose to-morrow and trouble myself with
the place no more: do you believe what I am saying?”

She made no answer, and he proceeded.

“I must ask you, Mrs. Drewitt, now, to look back to the time when you
came first to Kincorth. What had we out of the property?—food. Had we
anything else? were we well-dressed? were we well-lodged? were not we
thrust to the wall? were we not intruders and dependents in the house of
our fathers? Had we anything which a gentleman’s children had a right to
expect at the hands of the person who had supplanted that gentleman?
Many and various were the humiliations I experienced in those the early
days of my life. But I have no desire to pay back my uncle’s family in
kind. Many a long year ago, Mrs. Drewitt, you were good enough to
sympathise with me and to entreat me not to leave Kincorth—”

She made a gesture of impatience, but he disregarded it and went on.

“Since then I have succeeded; I have been prosperous; I am wealthy, and
the owner of the place where I once was little better than a servant. I
do not forget your kindness, but entreat you to remain; to consider this
house your home; to let me help you and your children as you were once
anxious to help me.”

He had made his speech, and it was not a bad one; and now he waited to
hear what effect it had produced on Mrs. Drewitt.

“Maxwell,” she answered, “I always admitted your position in life was
not what it ought to have been, until by your own industry and
cleverness you made yourself what you are. I always thought your
grandfather’s will most unjust; and even in this house, from whence my
dead husband has so lately been carried, I am not afraid to say that,
had he acted more wisely and fairly by his brother, his life would have
been a more prosperous one. But,” and here Mrs. Drewitt rose, as if she
could not find breath to speak sitting—as if in her nervous excitement
it was needful for every muscle to be strained, every limb stretched,
“my husband did not act unfairly by design, or through any premeditated
wickedness. He came honestly into Kincorth, and sure am I in my inmost
heart, Maxwell, that you have come dishonestly. I know that for
twenty-one years you have kept this end in view; and you have achieved
your object so far. When I think of my poor boy—when I think of
Brian”—and as she spoke the tears fell like rain over her altered
face—“when I think of how he has worked, of how you set his father
against him, of how you sowed dissension between him and Harold, and of
how you have destroyed every hope and plan of his life, I feel that if I
were to eat your bread it would choke me; that if I were to accept any
grace, any favour, at your hands I should be as bad as you.”

“What do you think of that, Mr. Pryor, as a civil answer to a kind
offer?” asked Maxwell.

“I will tell you by-and-by,” replied the Englishman; “meantime I think
Mrs. Drewitt will be more composed at Durrow, where she has no old
associations, where everything will be fresh and new. She is not capable
at present of appreciating your kindness, or in fact of clearly
understanding what you say; and as you are going to take up your
quarters here to-night, your aunt and cousins will accompany me back to
Durrow.”

“If they do then,” said Maxwell Drewitt, “they shall never return to
Kincorth.”

“Oh! Kincorth—dear, dear Kincorth,” broke out the widow, and she fell on
her knees, and burying her head in the sofa pillow, sobbed passionately.

“You had better leave her,” suggested Mr. Pryor, and he drew Maxwell out
of the room.

“They are a nice comfortable sort of people to have to do with, are not
they?” said Maxwell, with a forced laugh, slowly drawing on his
left-hand glove, and smoothing every wrinkle out of the kid while he
spoke. “There are not many men who would bear having their kindness
flung back in their face as I do. What do you think of the family now,
Mr. Pryor?”

“I think that they have received a great shock, following on the top of
another great shock, and that consequently every allowance ought to be
made for them, and for what they say under the circumstances.”

“And what do you think about the whole affair—about the will I mean?”

“That it must be very unpleasant for you,” was the reply.

“Well, it is: the property is not worth that,” and Maxwell snapped his
fingers, “only I am glad to have back the old place. If Brian had gone
on with his improvements it would soon have come to the hammer.”

“I cannot quite agree with you there,” answered the Englishman. He could
see the chesnut waiting for her new master, and he added, “You are going
to ride Harold’s mare?”

“Harold’s, indeed! I really was not aware.”

“I thought it was his: did his father not give her to him?”

“He said so,” answered Maxwell; “but, on the same principle, Brian might
say his father gave him Kincorth.”

“Of course; you are right. Then you are not going to confirm your
uncle’s present?”

“Give a lad like that a mare worth two hundred and fifty guineas? If you
have made your money so easily, Mr. Pryor, I have not.”

The Englishman did not press the point further. “Good-bye,” he said, “I
suppose you will not be back before we leave.”

“We! Then you really are going to adopt the entire family? Be advised—a
widow and three children is no light responsibility. Besides, you ought
really to consider _les convenances_.”

“What _convenances_?” asked Mr. Pryor, hurriedly.

“Why, the world may say one of two things—either that you are going to
marry the widow, who is only about a year or so older than yourself; or
else that you are in love with the daughter. I have long inclined to the
latter opinion myself, and I congratulate you on an arrangement which
will give you such ample opportunities of winning her young affections.”

“I am your debtor, Mr. Maxwell Drewitt.”

“Not at all; if you were so, I should be only too proud to be your
creditor.”

“You are very kind, but I prefer paying my bills as I go on. You have
asked me twice within the last ten minutes what I thought of your
position. Let me give you my answer now. If you have come into Kincorth
honestly, I think we are all dealing very hardly by you; but if you have
come into Kincorth dishonestly, then I think you have not had half your
deserts. And in either case,” added Mr. Pryor, “I must say that I
consider your manner unfortunate.”

“Our opinions with regard to each other may be identical on the latter
point,” retorted Maxwell; “but I suppose that need not hinder our being
friends in the future as we have been in the past. Good-bye for the
present,” and he held out his hand.

But Geoffry Pryor refused to take it. He bowed stiffly and walked back
across the hall to the breakfast-parlour, where he had left Mrs.
Drewitt.

“As you like, my fine fellow,” muttered the new owner of Kincorth;
“those that win may laugh.” But somehow Maxwell Drewitt did not laugh.
He looked—while he rode down the avenue and out of the gates, of that
property which was now his own for life—as unlike a person who had the
remotest intention of laughing as it is possible to conceive.

After all, the cup of prosperity is not entirely sweet. There are an
immense number of bitters in the pharmacopœia even of success.

But he had succeeded in annoying Mr. Pryor, that was one great
consolation; and if he could have imagined how desperately he had
wounded his opponent he would have been better pleased still.

To a man like Geoffry Pryor it does not matter much what the world says;
but it matters excessively what his own conscience tells him; and his
own conscience told him Maxwell had not spoken altogether falsely, for
that he should find it very sweet to have Geraldine living under the
same roof as that which covered his own head.

“I must go to Waller’s; it is the only way of cutting that knot,” he
decided; and more determined than ever that, so long as he had a shelter
to offer her, Mrs. Drewitt should not remain at Kincorth, he pressed
upon her the necessity of packing her things as soon as possible, and of
taking her departure before Maxwell returned.

“Your maid could follow with your luggage,” he suggested.

“Poor old Nannie was the only maid I could ever boast of,” answered Mrs.
Drewitt through her tears.

“Well, let Nannie put your things together, and the boys can soon find
their own; and I will send Flynn over to Durrow for the car and a light
cart. If there are any of the servants you would like to accompany you
we can make room for them at Durrow.”

“But how should I ever be able to keep servants?” she inquired.

“If you are not able to do so, I will find something for them,” he
answered.

“If I were to take one or two the rest would think I did not care for
them. I had better leave all, I had indeed.”

She was right, and Mr. Pryor could not help marvelling how so sensible a
woman had never been able to make a wiser man of the owner of Kincorth.

While he went round to the stable-yard to despatch Flynn to Durrow, she
called the servants together and told them all what they knew
beforehand—that Mr. Brian was heir no longer, that Mr. Maxwell now owned
Kincorth and everything in it.

“You must not think,” she added, for her sense of justice prevented her
saying anything to prejudice the servants against their new master, or
leaving anything unsaid which it was right they should know—“you must
not think Mr. Maxwell Drewitt is turning us out of Kincorth; on the
contrary, he offered me its hospitality for as long as I liked to
remain. It is by my own wish I leave the house I have loved so well.”
(“Ah! hear that, the craythur!” said some one in the rear.) “I could not
be happy here; and though it is very kind of Mr. Maxwell (dark faces and
lowering looks among the men), still I feel it is best for me to avail
myself of Mr. Pryor’s kindness and go to Durrow till I see what the
future may bring forth. It is so soon—” and Mrs. Drewitt paused to
recover herself—“so soon after my dear husband’s death, and altogether
so sudden and unexpected, that I cannot tell yet where I may go or what
I may do; but wherever I am—”

She paused again:—“wherever I am”—but it would not do: she put her
handkerchief to her face, and then the women crowded round her weeping
and bewailing themselves, and Mrs. Drewitt sobbed out too.

She was like a queen among them—she who had come there so lonely and
unwelcomed—and when she said, “You have been my friends; I have loved
you all,” and held out her hand for each to shake ere she departed, they
stooped down and kissed it, men and women alike, and a bitter cry of
lamentation arose to the chamber where Brian sat all alone.

He knew right well what was the meaning of it. He knew his mother and he
could be but of one mind, and that she meant to leave the house, and
leave it at once; but he did not think where she proposed going, and so
he descended the staircase and entered the drawing-room, where she and
Geraldine and Harold stood, and walked up to her.

“An’ here’s Masther Brian, ma’am; an’ it’s himself’ll be the comfort to
you from this day forth for ever.”

“An’ haven’t ye yer childer, ma’am? Shure there’s nobody living can take
them from ye: an’ wouldn’t him that has got Kincorth give Kincorth to
have any one of them for his own?”

“There’s a silver lining to ivery cloud.”

“But it’s us that’ll feel it, losing masther and misthress within the
week.”

“An’ it’s the good masther he was.”

“An’ the kind misthress you’ve been.”

“An’ the blissin’ of God will follow ye; and the prayers of the poor
have gone before ye.”

“She can’t stand it,” said Brian, huskily; “think of what she has gone
through and let her be quiet, Mother; come with me—mother darling.”

He led her through the servants, who made a way for them to pass out,
and took her to her own room, where he inquired what she meant to do,
what plan she had laid out.

Then he took the management of affairs on himself. He went and asked Mr.
Pryor what his mother was legally entitled to carry out of Kincorth, and
when Mr. Pryor told him, in strict law, only the clothes she wore, Brian
observed there would be the less packing needful.

“_He_ can send them after us if he likes,” said Brian, pointing towards
Headlands; “but she shall take nothing except what she has a clear right
to take.”

“And you, Brian?”

“I want to be alone for a while. I will walk over the hills to Durrow.”

“Let me walk with you;” and Brian saw that the Englishman was afraid of
what he might be meaning to do.

“Trust me,” he said; “I would not shoot Maxwell if there were no gallows
on earth. I am not going to seek an interview with him—upon my honour I
am not;” and Mr. Pryor was satisfied.

“Mamma wants to go all round the grounds, and would like you to go with
her,” Geraldine came whispering at this juncture. “She wants to go alone
with you,” the girl added, and Brian went.

Had Mrs. Drewitt been remaining on at Kincorth, I wonder how long a time
would have elapsed ere she set foot on lawn, in field, in garden? But
now, on the very day when the husband she had loved was laid in Eversbeg
Abbey, she walked about the place bidding to each tree and plant and
flower a sorrowful adieu.

Up the glen she went, and over a plank which was thrown across the
waterfall. She had been timid about that plank in former days, and
afraid to cross it; but now she stepped over without a helping hand.
Where the summer-house had once been she stood and looked down on the
country, speaking never a word. She plucked a leaf here and another
there; she picked up a pebble out of the stream, and pulled a fern and a
piece of moss from beside the waterfall. She went into the garden and
gathered for herself lavender, and heliotrope, and a sprig of bay, and
another of lemon verbena, a morsel of heath, a late-blooming rose, a
spray of fuchsia, two or three scarlet geraniums, and a handful of
woodruffe, and these she tied all together with a piece of ribbon, and
laid aside.

“When I am dead, Brian,” she said, “you will find those flowers
somewhere among my possessions. You will put them in my coffin. Promise
me, my son.”

“Mother, mother!” he remonstrated.

“Promise me,” she insisted, and he did as she wished.

Thus Agnes Drewitt bade farewell to Kincorth. In her heart she knew she
should tread its pleasant lawns, its shady walks, its sunny gardens, no
more for ever.

When she drove down the avenue with her children about her to the lodge,
where the way was blocked by a crowd eager to do her honour, eager to
tell her they sorrowed with her sorrow and sympathized with her
distress, she felt that, let who else would, return to Kincorth in
triumph, let who else would, come back to dwell in the familiar rooms,
to take their rest under those magnificent trees, she might return to
Kincorth no more!

 CHAPTER V.
 The Hour of Success.


Near the gates of Kincorth Brian separated himself from his mother, and
promising to meet her at Durrow, walked back up the avenue towards the
house. He had many things to put together—some few presents to make,
some last words to speak, ere he went out into the world to push his
fortune; and accordingly he retraced his steps slowly up the drive,
thinking of the change that twenty-four hours had wrought in his
prospects, and considering what it would be best for him to do now.
Half-way, Maxwell overtook him, and dismounting, walked with Brian to
the house, leading the chesnut, who looked distressed, as though she had
been ridden fast and far.

“I am glad to have an opportunity of talking with you alone, Brian,”
said Mr. Maxwell Drewitt. “Is there any earthly reason why we should not
be friends? why you should leave Kincorth at all?”

“I think there is,” Brian answered.

“Will you explain what you mean? Will you explain to me why there should
be ill-will between you and me any more than there was between your
father and my father?”

“Your father and my father were probably different men to what you and I
are,” was the reply. “Your father was wilfully disinherited, and he knew
why; I am disinherited by an accident, or by a fraud; you can best tell
which,” and Brian’s lip curled.

“You cannot think it other than accidental, surely,” said Maxwell.

“What I think cannot signify to you so long as you have got Kincorth,”
was the reply.

“Well now, Brian, that I have got Kincorth, will you tell me what I am
ever likely to make out of it? The mortgages, as you know, are almost
equal to its value. It is not a cheap house to keep up. More will be
expected from the owner of Kincorth than was ever looked for from the
owner of the Headlands. The place will be an expense to me, not a gain;
you must have sense enough to see that yourself.”

“Give it to me,” said Brian, grimly, “and see if I will complain.”

“Why, boy, you could never have made money out of it.”

“I should have tried,” was the reply. And there ensued a long silence,
which Maxwell broke, by saying as they approached the house—

“Shall we be friends?”

“No,” Brian answered; “for I mean to put you out of Kincorth, if I can.
If I cannot, then I mean to bide my time, and take my revenge. You need
not look so black, Mr. Maxwell Drewitt. I shall not shoot you; I shall
not come behind you in the dark and smite you under the fifth rib; but I
shall find out your weak point and hurt you there. A year or two is
nothing; I can wait: when you least look for me I shall be at your
side.”

“You are mad, Brian,” said his cousin.

“Then it is you who have made me so,” was the reply.

“Let us be friends.”

“I told you no, once; I tell you no, twice. There are places where even
you, Maxwell Drewitt, will be unable to injure me, to hinder my
succeeding; and I shall succeed yet; and I shall come back to Kincorth
yet. Let me pass, I am not going to steal anything out of the house. It
is yours now, I know, but it has not been so for long. Let me pass.”

“Can he have found another will?”—that was the thought which crossed
Maxwell’s mind, and for a moment he stood irresolute. Then he made way,
and Brian entered the house.

Before many minutes had elapsed he came down the staircase, with a bunch
of keys in his hand.

“Harold’s luggage and mine is together, and I will send over for both
to-morrow morning. Meantime, if you would like to see that we have taken
nothing belonging to you, there are the keys. Take them,” he insisted,
as Maxwell drew back, and he flung them at his cousin’s feet.

“I am going now,” the youth said, looking round him with a terrible
despair. “I am going now. Have you anything to say to me before I go?”

“Let us be friends.”

“You struck me to-day, Maxwell.”

“I am sorry for it.”

“You pushed me aside at my father’s funeral.”

“Inadvertently.”

“You have got Kincorth.”

“Accidentally.”

“For years you have been trying to put my father against me; you made
