him misjudge me towards the last; you tried to frustrate all my efforts
for freeing Kincorth.”

“I thought I was acting for the best.”

“Then,” answered Brian Drewitt, “I think I am acting for the best now,
when I refuse your friendship; when I say you are a coward and a
hypocrite; when I cross this threshold, saying that at peace with you I
will never re-enter it.”

“Brian, you will repent.”

“And so will you.”

With which mutual assurances the cousins separated, and while Maxwell
took possession of his new home, Brian strode out of the house and took
the hill-path leading to Durrow.

He had to pass by the stables on his way through the plantations, and he
turned aside to speak to Flynn, who was the last man to leave the yard.

“Mr. Maxwell wants you, I think, Flynn,” he said; “and I wish to say
good-bye to the horses; so you can come back here after you have seen
him;” and with that Mr. Brian Drewitt thrust a crumpled note into the
man’s hand, remarking—

“That will pay you for your overtime.”

“I don’t need it, Masther Brian,” said Flynn. “I’d work night and day
for you, I would, if working could give you back your own.”

“Keep it,” returned Brian. “I am not poor—keep it, I can make more; only
go—go away,” and he crossed the yard and pushed open the stable-door,
and went in, closing, but not latching it after him.

“Gently, my lady,” said the youth: he had walked up the stable and
entered Madcap’s loose box. At sound of his voice, the chesnut turned
round and whinnied.

Then Brian drew a pistol out of his pocket, looked to the priming,
cocked it, and took aim.

At the moment, Madcap put her head against his breast, asking in her
mute fashion for the caress to which she was accustomed: finding no
caress given, she lifted her head and looked at him with soft and
inquiring eyes.

“I cannot do it, Chessy,” he said. “I meant to shoot you, but I must
leave you for others to ride; for others to maltreat if they will—my
beauty—my pet! It would be greater kindness for me to put it out of
anybody’s power to do this; but I cannot—I cannot;” and he put his arms
round her slender neck, and leaned his head against her shoulder, the
chesnut standing still and quiet all the time, as though she had some
human comprehension of what was the matter with him.

At last she turned her head, and began rubbing it against his breast as
usual. Brian had always petted her more than anybody else, and she in
return, though he was not her master, had been wont to follow him about
the yard and round the meadows like a dog.

“I must go now, beauty,” Brian said, and he stroked her silky coat
slowly—slowly. “Good-bye,” and thinking there was no one near to see
him, he kissed the white star on her forehead, and then stepped back and
looked at her soft wondering eyes again.

“Good-bye, Chessy—good-bye,” and he walked out of the stall, not knowing
that Flynn and his cousin were standing in the next box, which chanced
to be vacant, looking on at his leave-taking.

As he went the chesnut turned round in her stall, and followed him to
the door. She put her head over the top of it, and watched him, with her
great brown sorrowful eyes, out of the stable.

“Hasn’t she a face just like a Christian, sir?” asked Flynn, but Maxwell
never answered him.

He had come to the yard suspecting his cousin’s design the moment Flynn
said Mister Brian had told him to go to the house. He had thought to
catch the boy doing mischief, and behold! he only saw that leave-taking,
which is always so pathetic, between a man and a favourite animal.

Something about the manner of that leave-taking caused Maxwell to walk
back to the house slowly and thoughtfully. He had got Kincorth, a
valueless property, for which he had paid a tremendous price. The man
who once stood between it and him was dead, the man’s widow was gone,
the man’s children were departed likewise, and now as he passed from
room to room, he found each in succession desolate and deserted.

He ascended the staircase and entered the chamber he had occupied in the
days which were now gone and passed away for ever. He crossed the bare
floor—he threw open the window and looked out.

In the gathering twilight, the mountains seemed frowning upon him, the
trees to be sunk in gloom; and that feeling of desperate depression he
had felt for the first time as he rode home one night, years and years
before, under the moonlight, came upon him once more.

Life was then before him: where was his life now? Half gone—more than
half gone—even if he survived to that threescore years and ten beyond
which man’s strength is but labour and sorrow. The twenty-one years had
passed like a watch in the night, like a tale that was told! Twenty more
years, and he should be as old as the man whom he had seen laid that day
in Eversbeg Abbey.

For twenty-one years he had been scheming, and planning, and plotting,
and his schemes had been in the main successful—he had gained wealth,
position, houses, and lands.

Suppose he lived for twenty years more, and for twenty to the back of
that, and through the whole of the time went on adding house to house,
and acre to acre—still the twenty, and the twenty, must come to an end
at last, and where would he be then?

That was Maxwell Drewitt’s dark hour: many such had come to him lately;
for it does not require a man to have either a very sensitive
conscience, or to think much on holy things, to make such considerations
strike home to him at times.

On the contrary, as sickness seems most terrible to the strong; as pain
seems most dreadful to those who have been exempt from it; as age seems
most wretched to the young; as poverty seems most frightful to the
affluent—so the very fact of a man rarely taking courage to face this
bugbear of our common mortality is sure to make its aspect more
unendurable when it forces itself upon him.

Maxwell had heard read that morning, beside his uncle’s grave, the words
which he knew must one day be read over his own:—

“Man that is born of a woman hath but a short time to live, and is full
of misery. He cometh up and is cut down like a flower; he fleeth as it
were a shadow, and never continueth in one stay.” And now, without in
the least knowing that thousands and thousands of years before the same
ideas had passed through the soul of one in great distress and
tribulation, the owner of Kincorth, leaning out into the night, and
looking away to the lonely hills, was thinking precisely the same
thoughts as those to which Job in his anguish gave utterance.

“There is hope of a tree, if it be cut down, that it will sprout again,
and that the tender branch thereof will not cease. Though the root
thereof wax old in the earth, and the stock thereof die in the ground;
yet through the scent of water it will bud, and bring forth boughs like
a plant. But man dieth, and wasteth away: yea, man giveth up the ghost,
and where is he? As the waters fail from the sea, and the flood decayeth
and dryeth up: so man lieth down, and riseth not: till the heavens be no
more, they shall not awake, nor be raised out of their sleep.”

Friends, oh! friends, is there not something unutterably mournful in the
figure of that man standing lonely, lonely as ever, in the room he had
left when a young man, so many years before? He had been solitary then,
he was solitary now; when he was lying in his grave he could not be more
alone than he felt standing there, looking out into the night.

Beyond all that he could have expected the wild dreams of his youth had
met with fulfilment. From the position of a dependent he had raised
himself to that of the first landed proprietor in the neighbourhood;
from beggary he had risen to wealth; from indigence, to prosperity and
consideration. If all Galway were not his, he owned quite enough of the
county to make the name of Drewitt a wonder to tourists—a memory in
Connemara.

There was not a house whose doors did not fly open at his approach. If
he had not effected all he had intended for the people’s good—for the
people’s advancement—that certainly was more their fault than his. He
had learned, as he assured Mr. Pryor, that a nation cannot be civilized
in a day; that a love of tidiness and of cleanliness, a desire for
luxuries, cannot be implanted in one generation, nor yet in two. He had
long comprehended that there was the same distinction between the upper
and lower classes in Ireland in the nineteenth century as existed
between lord and serf in the earlier portions of English history; not to
the extent of a power of life and death—not to the extent of a power of
oppression, or imprisonment, or disposal of the bodies of the poor; but
the same social difference. The lords lived in profusion, with a certain
wasteful magnificence, while their hinds were contented with the
coarsest fare. The lords went about in grand attire, while the serfs
were clothed meanly. Between the two classes in those days there could
have been no more distinction than there is between the rich and the
poor to the present day in Connemara; and it was this state of things
which Maxwell Drewitt at last declared to himself he was not sent into
the world to alter.

“The rice pot to the Hindoo,” he was wont to say, “the potato kish to
the Irish;” but the meal barrel and the potato kish were both better
supplied on the estates belonging to Maxwell Drewitt than on the estates
of any other man in West Connemara.

He had no cause to be greatly disappointed on that score; he had done
something for his countrymen and countrywomen, and having done as much
as he thought possible without their own co-operation, Maxwell rested
content. He loved Ireland better than he loved her sons and daughters;
but he loved himself better than Ireland.

And now having gained the summit of the hill of success, he stood on the
top looking out into the night a lonely man. Most lonely! Was this what
he had bargained for in those days of poverty and struggle? Was this the
end he had striven to reach? Was it thus he had expected to feel when
that end was compassed? Money—station—health—position: he possessed all
these, and yet the man whom he had followed to his grave that morning
was not so lonely as he.

There is a story current in Ireland about a traitor, by name Parese, who
undertook to give the Castle of Maynooth into the hands of an enemy of
the Fitzgeralds. He stipulated for a certain sum of money, but forgot to
stipulate also for his life; the consequence of which oversight resulted
in the “devil betraying the betrayer,” whose head was cut off as soon as
the reward was counted out.

Whether this tale be true or false, we need not stop to inquire, seeing
how often analogous cases must have come under the observation of all of
us; but I think that what happened to Parese happened also in a great
measure to Maxwell Drewitt. He had bargained for money and power, and
money and power he had, but nothing more.

When the bells that now hang in the tower of the church of St. Nicholas,
in Galway, were being cast, it is related that from far and from near,
men and women brought their golden brooches and rings, their precious
ornaments and their stores of silver and gold, and threw them in with
the iron, to the end that all might he fused together. And it is further
stated that the precious metals may be seen shining to this day in the
bells, and that those offerings of the faithful have caused their tones
to be peculiarly sweet and beautiful.

It is a lovely legend, and all the more lovely because it is so tenderly
suggestive, so touchingly pathetic. The men and the women who flocked to
the place where the casting went on have passed away, and their names
are forgotten on earth; but still the bells peal out the sweet music,
still their silver and their gold make the tone fuller, the notes more
exquisite.

I cannot help recalling these two stories while I think of Maxwell
standing there in the darkness, spite of all his wealth, alone—alone! He
had not bargained for happiness, thinking success would insure
happiness.

He cast no stores of love into time’s furnace on faith that he would
receive back, in some shape, principal and interest, and lo! the dark
days were advancing towards him, and there was never a one to take him
by the hand and bid him be of good cheer.

For money, he had, like Parese, sold his best friends, deserted the
woman who had trusted him, married a wife for whom he did not care; for
money he had schemed to ruin his uncle, and when his schemes failed, he
had accomplished his purpose by more dishonest means still. Money he had
elected to serve—Mammon had been his god, and, as has been cleverly
said, cent. per cent. his prophet; but a man cannot serve two masters—a
man cannot hoard all his wealth, or else spend it entirely on himself,
and still hear God’s bells making sweet music in his ears—music that
never sounds so sweet as to the sick and weary.

There was a preacher who once undertook to prove that life’s voyage
would be a pleasant one, even were there no hope of land on the other
side, nothing but the long sleep and the cold grave as a finish to each
man’s story. His theory seemed to be, that there is so much enjoyment in
the goodly company, in the lively conversation, in the health-giving
breeze, in the witty anecdote, in the daily work, as to make the journey
a desirable one in itself, without any reference to an expected haven;
and while the goodly company is around us, while the conversation never
flags, while the sky is clear and the body strong, perhaps he may be
right. I have known many who seemed to think so. Only, reader, how is it
when man has to go below; when he is lying in his narrow berth; when the
guests have dropped off one by one; when the voices of the singers are
hushed; when the evil days are come and the years draw nigh when he
shall say, “I have no pleasure in them;” when the sun and the light, and
the stars and the moon be darkened, and the clouds return after the
rain—how is it then?

Standing in the gathering night—type of that still deeper night which he
would one day have to pass through, Maxwell Drewitt was seized in the
hour of his success, in the hour of his victory, with a strange
horror—with a frightful dread. It seemed but a day to him since he was
young; it would be but a day till he was old, and then—

Maxwell closed the window, and groped his way out of the room. All in
darkness, he descended the staircase, and when he reached the hall, he
took his hat, and saying to one of the servants, “You need not wait up
for me. I shall not sleep here just at present,” walked out of the
house, and down the avenue, under the trees, past the porter’s
lodge—home.

“I do not think I shall go up to Kincorth till you can go with me,” he
said to his wife, and Lady Emmeline looked at him in amazement.

It was the first time during sixteen years she had heard him express a
wish for her companionship, and so much affection astonished her.

Further, instead of kicking her poodle out of his way with a curse,
Maxwell, thinking of that scene in the stable of which he could not get
rid, stretched his hand towards the creature, and called it by
name—“Prince—Prince!” But Prince, unused, like its mistress, to such
attentions, only crouched behind Lady Emmeline, who, pushing the dog off
the sofa, said—

“Go, Prince, there.”

“Come here, Prince,” added Maxwell, and he made a movement to stroke
him; but Prince backed from the proffered caress, and, barking and
growling, retreated under the sofa.

“Prince is not accustomed to your noticing him,” said Lady Emmeline,
apologetically, but Maxwell did not seem to hear her words. He was
walking up and down the room with his hands buried in his pockets, his
head bent down, his face looking stern and thoughtful.

“Has Mrs. Drewitt really left Kincorth?” asked Lady Emmeline after a
long pause.

“Yes, they are all gone,” he answered.

“Dear, dear; I am very sorry; poor things!”

“I do not see why the devil you should be sorry for them,” he said,
stopping in his walk; “nor why you should call them poor things. I did
not want them to go. Heaven knows, I would much rather they had stayed.
And as for their being ‘poor things,’ they cannot be a great deal poorer
than they were; and I suspect Mrs. Drewitt knew pretty well what she was
about when she went to Durrow. Pryor is sure to marry Geraldine, and
that, you may say, will be a provision for the family.”

“How very clever you are, Maxwell,” remarked Lady Emmeline, her old face
lighted up with admiration. “Who could ever have pieced that all
together as you have done? I never should; but now it is as clear as
possible. It is not exactly nice; but still one can scarcely blame
her—can one?”

“Blame her! though she has placed me in a most awkward position, I
commend her prudence. Candidly, I did not think Mrs. Drewitt had so much
sense,” and Maxwell looked as he spoke at his wife’s face, and wondered
with an unutterable astonishment how he ever came to marry such a fright
of a woman.

“And Mr. Pryor is so nice, and Geraldine so pretty,” said Lady Emmeline.

“The only drawback—” Maxwell began, but he stopped in his speech
suddenly and resumed his walk.

“Is what?” inquired his wife.

“I was wrong—there is no drawback,” said Maxwell, who did not intend to
make any remarks for the future about disparity of age, if he could
avoid doing so. He had been thinking of something he meant to ask his
wife to do for him, and to compass that object he intended to be civil
to her; civil so far as it was in his nature to be towards any one with
whom he was thrown in daily communication.

Many a taunt he had flung at her, but he would try to taunt no more. If
she fell into his plan and helped him to carry it out, he would bear
with her silliness; he would say nothing to her about their childless
hearth—about her rouge—her age—her affectation. He would command his
temper and his tongue, and do his best for her, if she would do her best
for him.

 CHAPTER VI.
 On the Way to Durrow.


Through the twilight Brian Drewitt pursued his way over the hills to
Durrow, keeping as near the sea as the nature of the country would
permit.

Instinctively he felt that the waters rolling on the shore, that the
plash of the waves which rose to his ears, that the noise of the ocean
washing up to the base of the cliffs calmed and comforted him.

When no human being could have quieted his heart, the voices of the
night and the murmurs of the sea came round and about him, bringing
tranquillity and repose with them.

All his plans had that day been wrecked, all his hopes had foundered. It
is no easy matter to realize the full extent of such a misfortune in a
minute, and Brian sometimes caught himself wondering about the leases,
about the grass land, about how soon the oats would be fit to reap, even
while he knew, in a dull, stupid kind of way, that the leases, the grass
lands and the oats could never more signify anything to him.

When we go out of the light we find it difficult to see clearly in the
darkness; and after the flare of excitement in which he had been living
all the day, Brian discovered that the intense stillness, the utter lull
of human passions and human wishes around him, prevented any distinct
thoughts or plans taking shape in his mind.

For a time everything seemed mixed and confused. He walked on like a
person in a dream, now thinking of one thing, now of another, until at
last, as he drew near Durrow, every incident of the past week came back
to his memory clearly and separately. When it did so, Brian Drewitt
threw himself down on the grass by the edge of the cliff—lay with his
face touching the cool earth—thinking of his past, tracing out his
future.

He thought of his father; of the coldness that had come between them; of
the way in which he had worked and struggled to free Kincorth; of how
his endeavours had fretted his parent; of how his motives had been
misunderstood, his intentions doubted; of how Harold had been preferred
to him; of how Harold had been everything, he nothing—and Brian’s heart,
as he lay and considered these things, became a perfect well of
bitterness.

But then, again, he remembered how his father’s grey head had lain on
his breast, how his father’s last action on earth had been to stretch
out his arms towards him, and Brian’s heart was softened, and grew
exceeding tender and sorrowful within him.

And the waves came rolling on the shore, slowly and gently, and the sea
washed the base of Durrow Cliffs, and it seemed as though he could hear
the voices of the great waters coming nearer and nearer, talking to him,
mourning with him, as if they too had suffered.

He recollected what his father had said about having left everything to
him. He repeated to himself the warning he had received years and years
before. He called up every word and look of Maxwell’s, and then
suspicion became certainty. Kincorth _had_ been procured by fraud;
though by what fraud Brian was unable to imagine. Which idea brought him
almost to the end of his reflections on the past, by causing him to
wonder what he should do in the future.

There, however, all seemed vague and shadowy. He could not yet tell what
he ought to do. He had no idea on what course his mother would decide.
He must talk the matter over with Mr. Pryor; and having settled so much,
Brian rose from the ground, and walked onwards for a little distance
towards the house.

Then he turned and went back to the cliff once more, where he sat down
and listened dreamily to the song which the ocean was murmuring so far
below. Heaven knows what the waves whispered to him, what message the
soft wind brought to his soul—I only can tell, that with hands clasped
over his head, with his face bowed forward on his knees, Brian hearkened
to the sermon which nature preached to him that night, taking, as we all
do, his own construction out of it.

We have, most of us, some memory of this kind—some scene that, so often
as we think of any great trouble, immediately recurs to our memory.

With one it is a forest glade, where the sunbeams pour through the
branches and make bright patterns and golden traceries on the turf
beneath; with another, it is a bare expanse of country, upon which the
winter afternoon is darkening down; with a third, it is some river
reach, where trees overhang the waters, and the grass looks green and
beautiful on the banks, and a boat or two is gliding past; while a
fourth sees in imagination the city streets all lighted up, and full of
people hurrying—hurrying away.

But sadder to my mind than all, is the memory of a sunset watched from a
lonely seashore; a sunset seen over a waste of angry waters, of roaring
billows; or a night-watch on the deck of a vessel, while the stormy
waves are lashing themselves to fury all around; or such a vigil as that
kept by Brian Drewitt, during which his soul seemed to go out of him,
and stand on the shore below, greeting the white foam as it was thrown
upon the shingle, and asking the waters questions that might only be
fitly put if the ocean washed, on one side, the sands of time, but on
the other the headlands of Eternity.

His life—it was all before him then, to make what he would out of. His
life—he had reached that point in the highway of existence where his
father had once stood, where Maxwell had once stood, where all those who
have grown to man’s estate must surely have paused for a moment to
survey their road. His life—what a much grander thing life would have
seemed to this youth had he looked out at it from the windows of
Kincorth instead of from the cliffs of Durrow! And yet, to my thinking,
it was in his power to make as good a thing of his existence one place
as another; unencumbered by a mortgaged estate, as burdened by it; free
to go where he liked, instead of being chained to one spot, and forced
to make his fortune there. For he determined to make his fortune. He
resembled Maxwell in that respect, and he resembled him, unfortunately,
in another—viz., in his resolution to win back Kincorth.

“Earth fighting for earth, earth struggling after earth, earth losing
its youthfulness, its innocence, its light-heartedness to gain earth!”
Did the wind and the waves utter no such plaint as this in his ears, as
he sat listening to their voices? Had the night, which had spoken so
plainly to Maxwell, nothing to tell Brian?

Life, oh! life—who is there that has lived, who would not go back and
try to make a better of it if he could? Who, when the last day comes,
does not think that, if he might be permitted to retrace his steps, to
stand once more on the threshold of existence, his prayer would be to
wash his hands in innocency, and to keep himself unspotted from the
world?

Stained and soiled, torn and defiled, who does not wish that he could
commence wearing the fleshy garments of our poor mortality once again,
fondly believing he could keep them out of the mire, and bring them
almost white to the end of the journey? And yet, as the instrument can
give forth no music till its strings have been played upon—as we can
never tell the quality of the gold till it comes forth from the
furnace—as we know little of our own capabilities till our strength has
been tested and tried in the battlefield of the world—so there is no
man, or woman either, who dare say that, if return were possible, a
better working out of the great problem would be probable also.

And for these reasons I do not say that Brian would have passed through
the fire scatheless had he laid out a different course to travel; though
I believe it would have made all the difference had he decided to pursue
his onward path without permitting hatred to gain dominion over
him—without letting envy and malice and all uncharitableness find
lodging in his young heart.

But yet it was scarcely Brian’s fault that he disliked his cousin so
intensely. He had drunk in that enmity with his mother’s milk; he had
fed on it from his youth upward. Mrs. Drewitt was the one to blame here.
She had been pleased to see Brian at war with Maxwell. She had done her
best to make her son hate the man who desired her son’s inheritance:
for, like all weak people, in striving only to put Brian on his guard
against his cousin’s machinations, she had overdone her part, and caused
her boy to look with suspicion on every act of his relative—to search
for some hidden meaning under every word—to listen greedily to stories
concerning Maxwell’s wickedness, Maxwell’s avariciousness, Maxwell’s
heartlessness—to consider the owner of the Headlands, in fact, as the
_bête noire_ of the county, and to believe that honey could as soon be
bitter as any act of Maxwell Drewitt’s good.

Add to this the well-meant warning which he had received, and his own
experience of his cousin, and you will see that, unless Brian Drewitt
made up his mind to leave Ireland entirely, to forget Kincorth and
Connemara, and Maxwell, and to start on a new life which should have no
sort of connexion with the past, there could be nothing between the pair
but war to the death for ever.

In his hour of triumph it might be that Brian would hold out his hand to
Maxwell, and contemptuously offer him friendship as his cousin had
offered friendship to him; but even that was scarcely likely; for in his
hates Brian was more sincere than his cousin. He was a staunch friend,
an open enemy; and if his constitution, his education, his temperament,
his country, and his misfortunes, all tended to render him implacable,
who may blame him?

It is not so easy to turn the other cheek as those who have never
received a blow on one, would have us believe. We are all rather more
apt to fight for our coat than to bestow our cloak on the thief also.
What man is there that, having Brian Drewitt’s strength of character,
would venture to say he could always keep clear of that dangerous
frontier ground where strength becomes sin?

Nevertheless, I would it were possible for me to record that the voices
of the night, the murmurs of the sea, the light wind that came down from
among the tops of the hills, taught Brian a different lesson; but alas!
when the youth arose he was the same as when he sat down; death had not
softened him, suffering had not subdued him, nature and silence had no
power to change him.

When in the after years he returned to Ireland and sat on the cliffs of
Durrow, listening to the plash of the waves, to the sighing of the wind,
he would have given all his wealth, all his influence, aye, and his life
also, if he could but have kept that sorrowful vigil once again, and
learned a different lesson out of it—if he could but have started on his
journey through the world resolved never to look back, but always
forward.

Through the years, the sea ebbed out, and the sea flowed in; through the
years, in storm and tempest, in calm and sunshine, the waves stole up on
the sands, and kissed the feet of the great rocks lovingly; through the
years, the shore-birds built their nests, and fed their young, and
winged their flight far out over the ocean, and then came back, with
pinions weary and drooping, to the land; through the years, the
fishermen dried their nets, and put out their lines, and pushed off
their boats; through the years, the ships went by, staggering among the
billows, or with their white sails glistening and shining, sped onwards
to their destined haven; through the years, the sun set, and the
darkness drew on, and the great hills hid themselves behind the curtains
of the night; through the years, the deeps never ceased calling unto one
another, now softly, now in louder tones; through the years, the youth
who had once sat and listened to them was a stranger in a strange
country, working for his daily bread.

He did not know as, at last, he walked away from the edge of the cliff,
that it would be necessary for him to leave Connemara; on the contrary,
he had some vague idea of renting a cottage for his mother, near
Analore, and of trying to make his fortune there. He knew that with
worse land Maxwell had done well, and although there was no Lady
Emmeline for him to marry, yet he thought that, with Mr. Pryor’s help,
he could, perhaps, still weather the storm.

“I must talk to him,” thought Brian, and he went up to Durrow House,
where Mrs. Connor met Brian in the hall, and informed him that his
mother and brother and sister were all gone to bed, “tired out” as Jenny
expressed it.

“And Mr. Pryor?” Brian asked.

“He is staying at Eversbeg with Mr. Waller.”

“What’s that for?” inquired the youth.

“He said he thought Mrs. Drewitt would be more comfortable if she felt
she had the house to herself,” was the reply. And then Mrs. Connor went
on to ask what he would have to eat, and brought in supper herself, and
made the boy a cup of strong coffee. Look at her as she sits in the
candlelight—would you think that from her eyes had ever fallen such
passionate tears, that she had ever pleaded so vehemently, that she had
ever loved so unavailingly, as you can remember to have been the case?

Those calm, kind eyes look now as though sorrow had never dimmed them.
Away in her girlhood, far, far back, lies the dream, and the grief, and
the sorrow, and the sin.

To such natures, to such gentle, loving women, time is very kind; he
refuses to wear channels in their cheeks, to draw lines across their
foreheads, to take away their beauty, or to destroy their power of
conferring happiness upon others; and thus Jenny was still pleasant to
look upon—pleasant, though she was young no more; though the flush of
her girlish loveliness had faded off her cheeks, though the warm
brightness of her hair was dulled, though the years that had come and
gone could not come and go without leaving traces behind them, still
Jenny was pretty still, and Brian, who liked her greatly, found her face
pleasant, as I have said, to look on.

Had she done wrong to marry? Jenny’s nature, facile as it was, had yet
set itself resolutely against marrying or giving in marriage; but time,
as it passed by, worked wonders, and Connor’s patient devotion and
enduring constancy prevailed at last. All were dead who knew her
secret—all save Maxwell and one other; and so, after ten years, she
married, and came, as we have seen, with her husband to take charge of
the great house at Durrow.

Mr. Pryor’s agency was of course immense promotion to Connor. There was
a wonderful difference between living at Durrow Park and in Duranmore
Cottage. Altogether, the Connors were rising people, greatly loved by
the poor, and much appreciated by their employer; and Brian could not
help thinking; while Jenny coaxed him to eat, and set dainty viands
before him, that the Englishman had been fortunate; that no man and no
woman could have been found in the length and breadth of Connemara
likely to suit him better.

For many a day Jenny and Brian had been friends. She it was who, seeing
the boy idling away his time, sought opportunity and occasion to give
him the caution he spoke of to Mr. Pryor; and now, when all his
struggles, all his efforts had proved of no avail, Mrs. Connor spoke
tearfully of the sorrow that had come upon them, and told the son how
deeply she grieved for his mother.

“You must be a good boy to her,” she said; “you must make up to her for
what she has lost.”

“Yes,” answered Brian, “and I mean to try and give her back what she has
lost.”

“You cannot give her back the dead,” Mrs. Connor remarked, sorrowfully.

“No; but I may give her back Kincorth,” the youth replied.

“Mr. Brian,” began Jenny, “since ever I came to Duranmore I have heard
of Kincorth—heard of it as a bone of contention, as a place about which
there had been quarrels, and broils, and heart-burnings, generation
after generation. But for Kincorth, maybe your cousin might have been a
better man: not that I have any reason to speak ill of him,” she went on
hurriedly; “for though I am afraid he did put Timothy out of Mr.
Waller’s agency, still he has stood his friend since, and he has been
very kind to Dennis; but still everybody knows, unless by chance his
wife, that Mr. Maxwell has not been a good man, which I think he might
only for Kincorth——”

“How do you make that out, Mrs. Connor?” asked Brian, pushing away his
plate as he spoke.

“You are not eating a bit,” said Jenny, piteously.

“Never mind me,” answered the young man. “You were speaking about
Maxwell: how do you think Kincorth made him what he is?”

“Why, this way,” she replied. “He always thought himself wronged, you
know, and it is not for anybody to say, Mr. Brian, that your grandfather
did the thing that was right when he passed by his own eldest son and
left all to your father. Do you think, Mr. Brian, it would have been
fair for Mr. Drewitt to cut you out for Master Harold? Would it, now?”
and Jenny’s voice grew quite earnest and pathetic as she pleaded the
cause of the man she had once loved too well.

“Putting it in that way, Mrs. Connor, I must agree with you,” he
answered; “but then it was not with Maxwell a question between brother
and brother, but between uncle and nephew. If George Drewitt had been
bitter about the matter, I must say his feelings would have seemed to me
perfectly natural, and if Harold had superseded me, perhaps I should
have felt angry too. That, however, would have been no reason why my son
should feel himself injured. There is no just cause why Maxwell should
have imagined Kincorth belonged of right to him.”

“Yes, there was, Mr. Brian,” she persisted. “Your father said he would
never marry, and then he did marry. He promised to be a father to his
brother’s children, and then——”

“Mrs. Connor, he was buried to-day; let what he did and what he left
undone be buried with him,” Brian interrupted.

“I beg your pardon,” she said, humbly, and the tears came up into her
eyes as she saw how hurt the lad was. “He was a good man and a true,
even if he did leave many a thing undone he ought to have seen to; and
all I meant to say was, that as Kincorth has been a curse and a snare to
your cousin, I hope and pray, Mr. Brian, you will never let it be either
to you. Sure there’s many a place as nice in Ireland. To my fancy, this
house, with its look-out over the sea, is heartsomer than those woods
and mountains”—poor Jenny! she shivered as she spoke—“and I think Dennis
is right in saying you are better without so large a property so heavily
mortgaged. And, indeed,” finished Jenny, “if I was a young gentleman I
would not stay in these parts at all. I would go to England, and see the
world, and make money, and come back here maybe for a month or two in
the year, as Mr. Pryor does, but no longer. For many and many a day
before I was married I used to wish I could go away. I did—I did.”

“I have not a notion what I shall do yet,” Brian answered, while he took
another cup of coffee from her. “There is one thing, however, I do
know—that wherever I go I shall always feel grateful to you for making
me what I am. But for you I do not think I should have been fit for
anything; and now I do not feel afraid of turning out into the world to
earn my bread.”

“I told you before, Mr. Brian, that your mother had been my friend once
in a time of bitter trouble, and that if at any hour of the day or night
I could serve her or hers I would get up to do it. You have no call to
feel grateful to me. There is nothing I could do for her, God knows,
that would be half enough—not half.”

“I cannot fancy your ever having had a trouble, Mrs. Connor,” said the
lad. She was the same in middle age as she had been in youth, a woman
who took men’s fancies, to whom it came easy and natural for them to
talk. “You look as if you never could have known a care.”

“Do I?” and she dropped her head forward for a moment on her hands.
“There are few who come to my time of life,” she went on, “that have not
met trouble; but there are few who, when in trouble, find such a friend
as I did in your mother. If I could have died to keep this grief from
her, Mr. Brian, I would have done it. You may think that strange now,
having both Dennis and the children to value my life for; but I would,
and it is because I love her, and because I love you for her sake, that
I bid you quit caring for Kincorth—that I want you to make her quit
caring for it too.”

Brian turned away. He could not bear to think of his mother dependent
even for a shelter on the kindness of friends, on the courtesy of
strangers.

For himself it would not have mattered, but for her—

That was the keystone of the lad’s character; that was what had made a
man of him, whilst in years he was yet a boy—love for her—love for the
mother who had not been true to her first allegiance, but who had loved
two other babies since, and her last child, Harold, most of all.

“What am I to do?” he asked after a pause; “how can I make her cease
caring for her home, for the place to which she came a wife, which she
has left a widow? Tell me, Mrs. Connor,” he added, “what made you think
so many years ago that Maxwell was determined to have Kincorth; what
induced you to warn me; I should like to know that now.”

“Well, then, you cannot know,” she replied, taking one of the candles
off the table; “for I shall not tell you. If I had to do it again, I am
not sure that I should act as I did then, though I meant my warning for
your good;” and with that Jenny stretched out her hand a little
hesitatingly towards Brian, who stooped and kissed it as though she had
been a queen. As he did so Jenny tore her hand away and broke out crying
hysterically.

“Have I offended you, Mrs. Connor?” he said, standing abashed at his
boyish gallantry; which was no impertinence, but an act of pure, and
honest homage, and liking. “I am very sorry; I never meant to vex you.
Forgive me—pray, forgive!”

“Forgive!” she cried; “what have I to forgive? only, Mr. Brian, don’t do
that again—don’t. And may God bless—and may God keep you!”

Having completed which intelligible sentence Mrs. Connor left the room,
only turning back at the door to suggest that Brian should accompany her
and see the apartment which had been prepared for him.

“Try to get some sleep,” were her parting words; and worn out in body
and in mind, Brian followed her advice, and was soon forgetful alike of
past, present, and future.

 CHAPTER VII.
 Another Difficulty.


“I have been considering the suggestion you made about Analore, Brian,”
Mr. Pryor said one morning, a fortnight after Mr. Drewitt’s funeral;
“but I should not advise you to commence operations there.”

“Why not?” asked Brian. He had been over to Analore, and seen the
foundations built up of the house his father had intended for his
residence, and had drawn the granite for some twenty years before. “I
think a good deal might be made of it, and with a comparatively small
outlay, too. The land is very fair—fair, of course I mean, for
Connemara; and my mother would be only too glad to let me do the best I
could with it.”

“That I supposed; but the reason I object to your doing anything with
Analore is, that it would scarcely answer your purpose, to spend your
youth, money, and time in reclaiming another person’s property. To farm
land is a very different affair from reclaiming it.”

“But my mother’s interests and mine are identical. What is best for her
must be best for me; and if I reclaim Analore it will be quite as much
to the advantage of all of us as of one.”

“We need not discuss that question,” answered Mr. Pryor, “since it has
nothing to do with the matter. What I mean is this: Analore only belongs
to your mother for life; and though I trust she will stay with us till
her hair is white, still you would not care to plough and drain and sow
and plant for your cousin’s benefit.”

“He has nothing to do with Analore. It will surely belong to me—or to
all of us after——”

“Your mother’s death. No, Brian, it will not; and it is very lucky that
before you began to delve and dig I looked into her settlement. I cannot
imagine how any lawyer could overlook the probability of her having a
family; but that it has been overlooked is certain. Analore belongs to
your mother for life, and to Maxwell afterwards. It would have belonged
to you had your father died intestate. Unless your father were swamped
with debt at the time of his marriage, and that some pressure was
brought to bear upon him, the whole affair is inexplicable; but no one
can alter the wording now, and it is for this reason I say you had
better do nothing with Analore; because, in the first place, you would
be spending your strength on another man’s property; and, in the second,
you would—supposing any one advanced money on the place—have to insure
your mother’s life to secure the lender against loss.”

“Beaten,” said Brian, “at every turn;” and the pair walked on a step or
two in silence.

“You will not tell my mother,” were the next words spoken.

“Why not? I think it is much better for your mother to understand
exactly how you are situated,” replied Mr. Pryor.

“Harold and Geraldine?” Brian said; and the Englishman understood that
he meant it would pain Mrs. Drewitt to know they would be totally
penniless.

“I have been down with Neill,” Brian went on; “but he can tell me
nothing about the missing will. He says he gave it back to my father
himself; but that he has some impression the will was returned to his
office; that is, he says, he remembers my father making some mention of
either having left it with Ryan—who was then Neill’s clerk—or of his
having intended to do so.”

“Rather vague,” remarked Mr. Pryor.

“Then I went to Ryan. ‘Upon my word, Mr. Brian,’ he said, ‘if I knew
where that will was I would tell you; but Mr. Drewitt never gave it back
to me.’

“‘Mr. Neill thinks my father said something to him about having returned
it.’

“‘Burned it—depend upon it that was what he said.’

“‘But why should he burn the one and not the other?’ I inquired.

“‘My opinion is,’ he said, ‘that he forgot all about the other, he was
so mighty keen on, just at that time, aye, and indeed for long after you
thought he had done with Mr. Neill, considering how he could leave your
brother independent of you, that it is not for me, or you, or anybody to
say what he did or what he did not do, what he thought or what he did
not think.’ And then what do you think he proposed?” asked Brian.

“I have not the remotest idea.”

“He offered to lend me five hundred pounds to work Analore. I asked him
if it was Maxwell’s; but he said every penny of it was his own that he
had made by hard work. ‘I tried to keep it, thinking my sister might
want it some day,’ he added; ‘but if you have it, she can ask for it
should she need it, and it may be of use to you now.’”

“Well, Brian?”

“Well, Mr. Pryor, I refused it, of course; but wasn’t it good of him? As
I came home, I called again on Mr. Neill, and told him what Ryan said.
‘It is very possible,’ was all I could get out of him. Then I inquired
if my father had been with him after the mortgage was transferred, and
he said, ‘Yes, often.’ ‘About leaving Harold independent of me,’ I
inquired, and Mr. Neill nodded. So I am beaten there again, you
see—beaten again.”

“What do you think of doing now?” asked Mr. Pryor.

“Of trying my old scheme. I should like to get a situation and endeavour
to push my way.”

“Right; you cannot do better than adhere to that resolution.”

“Dan Glynn offers, as you know, two hundred and fifty pounds a year for
Analore. I do not think, if I am not to farm it, that we can do better.”

“Perhaps not. What does Mr. Dan Glynn expect to make out of it?”

“Potheen, I suspect,” said Brian, laughing; “but does that signify to
us?”

“Not in the least, if he pays his rent; and at any rate it will be much
less trouble to look up one man than twenty or thirty tenants. He will
not turn the present occupiers out?”

“They are only occupiers of the ground, you know,” replied Brian; “there
is but one hut on the fifteen hundred acres, and that has the roof off
it. Glynn, however, will not send any of them adrift. He will keep them
all to help.”

“And now, with regard to this plan of your mother’s about living in
Dublin? Do you not think she had better stay on for a year, at any rate,
at Durrow? The house is there, and empty, and——”

“I think she would be happier away,” Brian interrupted. “In a house of
her own, no matter how small, she would feel more settled than she could
ever do at Durrow; and besides, Mr. Pryor, there is Harold—we must get
him away from Connemara, at all hazards.”

“He would be better away certainly, and he must do something to earn his
living; but, Brian, promise me now—promise faithfully you will not
encourage this notion of Mrs. Drewitt’s about going to law: she has not
an inch of ground to stand upon, and it would be beggary. You have no
idea how soon law runs away with money; and she could about as soon
overturn Bennobeola, as put your cousin out of Kincorth now.”

“Not even if that codicil were proved to be a forgery?”

“How could it be proved such?” asked Mr. Pryor; but Brian remained
silent, and the barrister went on.

“I will be perfectly frank with you in this matter. I believe that
codicil to be a forgery. I thought so the moment I heard it read. I
thought it because of many trivial circumstances I had noticed
previously. But you could never convert a jury to the same opinion; it
would be waste of time, of money, and of patience to attempt such a
thing. The solicitor who made the original will, and who, it is
supposed, added the codicil, has been paralyzed these six years, and has
now lost all power of speech. The witnesses are dead. It is the distance
of time, you see, that is so much against us. In law, the codicil is
perfectly regular, and what is worse, perfectly natural. Mr. Neill
explains the absence of another will; and we have no reason to suppose
that Mr. Neill speaks otherwise than truthfully. As for your father’s
talk on his death-bed about having left the place to you, the first
question would be, was he delirious at all during his last illness? and
what could Sheen answer but that he frequently wandered? It is quite one
thing, you see, to be satisfied oneself, and quite another to satisfy a
jury. For which reasons, take my advice, and leave Kincorth to its fate.
After all, the estate is not worth the trouble. Your father did not
leave it as he got it.”

Brian winced. No one let him imagine Maxwell had secured so great a
prize as he wanted to believe.

“You will go out into the world,” went on Mr. Pryor, “and make your
fortune, and forget all about Kincorth, as I have forgotten all about my
disappointment when I was beaten at Duranmore before you were born. We
will get you something to do, and you shall do it, and be a rich man
yet; richer than Maxwell, perhaps, and much happier, we will hope.”

Having uttered which sanguine expectation, Mr. Pryor mounted his horse,
and without asking Brian to accompany him, rode straight to Duranmore.
The first person he went to was Mr. James Butler, with whom he had a
long and confidential chat. Having put up his horse at the “Marsden
Arms,” he walked from The Retreat to Mr. Neill’s office in Duranmore,
and satisfied himself Mr. Neill, at any rate, was not one of the
conspirators. From Mr. Neill’s office he crossed over to Mr. Ryan’s, and
after taking the chair that was nervously handed to him, he commenced
the conversation by thanking the lawyer for his friendly offer to Mr.
Brian Drewitt, “about which offer,” he added, “I have come to speak to
you.”

“Indeed! it is very kind,” and Mr. Ryan looked as though he would have
liked to skulk round the corner of the next street and get a glass of
whiskey before proceeding to business.

“Not at all; it is only my duty. I am quite certain when you made so
liberal a proposal that you were not aware of the actual position of
affairs regarding Analore, and a five hundred pound legacy is not one
which it is safe to jeopardize. Uncles do not die every day—do they, Mr.
Ryan?” added the barrister, mercilessly following every thought which
was passing through his listener’s mind.

“He is considering now whether he told Brian it was a legacy,” thought
Mr. Pryor; while Mr. Ryan answered, vaguely, “That they did not indeed.”

“And most probably, if anything went astray with this five hundred, you
have no further expectations from your relatives?”

“None, sir, none—he was the last;” and Mr. Pryor wondered whether Mr.
Ryan had any idea of weeping.

“That is precisely what I thought: you have got five hundred pounds to
lend, but you have not got five hundred pounds to lose. Analore is not a
desirable property, therefore, for you to risk anything upon, since Mrs.
Drewitt has no power of mortgaging it.”

“What do you mean, sir?—what do you mean?” and Mr. Pryor saw at once
that his suspicion about Ryan being mixed up in this matter also, was
unfounded.

“Precisely what I say. Mrs. Drewitt has only a life interest in Analore.
At her death it reverts to Maxwell Drewitt.”

“And the children?”

“Have not a sixpence in the world.”

“The Lord help them!” exclaimed Mr. Ryan.

“He had need,” answered the other, a little bitterly, “for it would seem
that they are not likely to get much help from man. Mr. Ryan,” he went
on, “probably you know the mystery of that marriage settlement—of course
nothing can make a better or a worse of it now—but as a simple matter of
curiosity, I should like greatly to know how such an instrument came to
be drawn.”

“I was not Mr. Maxwell’s solicitor then, sir. I had nothing to do with
him then at all, I assure you, sir.”

“Of course not; and that is the reason why I ask you for information on
the subject—as a mere matter of curiosity.”

“I do not know anything about it, sir.”

“Are you quite certain? Was no pressure put on Mr. Drewitt?”

“There might have been: if there were it wasn’t by me. I was only a
beginner then. I had no good clients at all.”

“Nor any five hundred pound legacies, we may suppose, Mr. Ryan,” said
the other, with a smile. “I hope you are getting good interest.”

“I—I haven’t put it out yet—I have it in hand.”

“Then your amiable relative has died recently, I presume?”

“Within the last three months.”

“What a strange people you Irish are!” remarked the other, rising to
depart. “You get a five hundred pound legacy, offer to lend it to a mere
lad on no sufficient security, and still grudge the expense of a
hat-band to prove how sincerely you deplore the dead!”

The two men were standing and their eyes met. In a moment Mr. Pryor knew
that what he had suspected was true; and Mr. Ryan knew the Englishman
was in possession of his secret. Then Mr. Pryor said good-morning, and
was going away, but the other detained him.

“For God’s sake, Mr. Pryor,” he said, laying a hand on his shoulder,
“never mention what you are now thinking to any one. He would kill me,
he would. If ever you hear of my being found dead by the roadside, with
a bullet through me, you will know whose bullet it is; though as likely
as not some innocent man may be hung for the murder.”

“Why do you not leave the country?”

“I will if you drive me to it,” was the significant answer.

“Mrs. Drewitt intends to contest the will,” remarked Mr. Pryor.

“Does she?—then I wish her well out of her troubles,” replied Mr. Ryan.
“If need were, he’d take it from court to court for twenty years. She
has not an inch of ground; you must see that yourself. There is some law
place in London that they say it is as bad for a man to get into as for
a cat to go through hell without her claws. Well, Mrs. Drewitt will find
going to law with her nephew as bad as that, or worse.”

“And yet you have been his friend for twenty years?”

“Have I?—more fool Timothy Ryan; that is all I have got to say: but
friend or no friend can make no difference about Mrs. Drewitt. You can
just tell her in confidence, out of your own mind as it were, that those
who sup with the devil need to have a long spoon.”

The notion tickled the barrister’s fancy, and he laughed outright.

“I think Mrs. Drewitt would be greatly surprised if I did,” he said.

“Surprised or not, it is the truth I’m telling you; but I would not tell
it to you if himself wasn’t safe in Dublin at this blessed minute.”

“If he is in Dublin, he is in Duranmore as well,” answered Mr. Pryor. “I
see his chesnut mare at the door, and I see him throwing the reins to
his groom.”

“Lord save us! I think he is the devil,” murmured Ryan, nervously
shuffling among his papers. “Good-morning, sir—good-morning,” he added
aloud as his visitor reached the door.

“I beg your pardon,” said the owner of Kincorth, who was entering the
office as the Englishman left it; “your most obedient, Mr. Pryor,” and
he almost swept the floor with his hat, his mocking bow was so low and
deferential.

Without answering him by a word, Mr. Pryor bowed in acknowledgment and
passed out, while Maxwell walking straight up to Ryan, took him by the
arm.

“What brought him here?” said the great man, looking his accomplice full
in the face, as if expecting him to falter in his reply.

But desperation gave Ryan courage. “He came to inquire about Mrs.
Drewitt’s settlement.”

“What about it?”

“She has it only for her lifetime, with no remainder to her children. He
seemed to think I knew all about it, but I did not. I told him it was
the first I had heard of the matter.”

Maxwell dropped his hold of Mr. Ryan’s arm, and leaning over the back of
a chair, indulged in a long low whistle of delight.

“Better and better,” he said. “I did not know that either, Ryan. I did
not,” he repeated, noticing a look of incredulity on the lawyer’s face.
“Do you imagine I have had time to read over every paper concerning the
property since I came into possession of it? No such thing. I shall find
plenty to pay, I expect, when I do take that trouble. But if this should
prove to be correct, we may as well make her an offer for Analore. You
can write on chance, Ryan, and tell her she shall have three hundred
a-year for Analore if she likes the offer; or should she prefer to sell
her life interest, I will take the place off her hands.

“When shall I write, Mr. Drewitt?”

“To-day, if you choose—yes, better do it at once, for I should like to
have Analore,” answered Maxwell.

Acting upon which suggestion, Ryan penned a polite epistle to Mrs.
Drewitt, who at once declined the proposal.

“Having already let Analore,” she finished.

“Well, it cannot be helped,” sighed Maxwell; “and, besides, it is only
for her life.”

“Only for her life!” echoed back some mocking fiend within him: “she is
not more than two years older than you! Only for her life!—you may be
dead before her; and then, to whom will you leave Kincorth, and the
Headlands, and Cherryfield, and the property in the Joyce country, and
Analore?”

Maxwell had decided that point. There was a boy finishing his education
at a very select school in the county Wicklow, whom he meant to make his
heir, whom he intended to send to one of the English universities and
bring up in the way he should go.

There was a boy to inherit—but alas! that could not make the idea of
“passing away” at all more endurable to the man, who, though he had no
fear of God, had yet an intense horror of death.

 CHAPTER VIII.
 Harold in Trouble.


It was summer once again: summer twelve months after the events recorded
in the last few chapters, and Mrs. Drewitt and Brian, and Harold and
Geraldine, were living far away from Duranmore, far beyond the Connemara
Alps, far beyond sound of the great ocean that washes the headlands of
the “wild west.”

Brian had got a situation in Dublin, which however necessitated frequent
absences from home. He was working hard, and giving great satisfaction
to his employers; and Mrs. Drewitt, who had greatly crippled her income
by feeing lawyers and running after all manner of people who advised her
to dispute her husband’s will, resided with Harold and Geraldine in a
small house in the outskirts of Dublin.

From whence Harold walked into the city every morning, and sat at a desk
from nine o’clock till four; an amusement he was never weary of
grumbling about; while his mother, likewise, never wearied in
commiserating him for it.

In the present, as in the past, it was Harold she pitied, Harold she
loved; it was the bold-eyed, bright-faced boy she clung to—her youngest
born—the Benjamin of her life to her!

Such likings are natural, perhaps; but they do not generally tend to
make matters pleasanter in the family circle, and perhaps it was quite
as well that Brian’s occupation took him much from home, as otherwise he
might have had less patience than was the case with Harold’s
repinings—with Harold’s conduct.

For Harold was the same in Dublin as he had been in Connemara—wild,
reckless, dissipated. His acquaintances were none of the most desirable,
his life none of the most regular; but still the mother would not
see—the mother would not believe.

He was her darling, and nothing it was in his power to do could make him
otherwise. When he found she came to meet him in the summer afternoons,
he walked by a circuitous route home; when he found she sat up for him
at night, he came home early, went to bed with the rest of the
household, and then stole down in his stocking-soles, with his boots in
his hand, and let himself out, taking the door-key with him.

Of course their servant—who was often unfastening the shutters when
Harold reappeared—knew of these pranks; but she had never the heart to
tell his mother.

And if she had told Mrs. Drewitt, what difference could it have made?
His mother had no influence over him—none whatever. He had nothing to
say but “I will leave the house,” or “I will go and ask food and lodging
from Maxwell,” and she was silent at once.

Whenever Maxwell came to Dublin, which was not seldom, Harold always
dined with him, and always came back reeling drunk.

“Told him—I—bore—no grudge—welcome to—Madcap—poor—old—girl,” the young
rascal would hiccup out, and when induced to go to bed, he invariably
did so under protest, retiring from view between the sheets in his
boots, coat, waistcoat and trousers, and refusing to let go of Mary’s
hand—Mary was their maid-of-all work—until she promised to call him
early.

“Don’t f—get, Marish. If you’re waking—you know—call me early, Marish
dear.”

And indeed had it not been for Mary’s repeated calls, Harold could
hardly have kept his situation so long as was the case.

“Damn business—curse work!” I think that was the refrain of almost every
sentence Harold Drewitt uttered for some months after starting in the
world on his own account, and Mrs. Drewitt listened sympathetically, and
would oftentimes confide to Mary, the maid-of-all-work before honourably
mentioned, the fact, “that she thought that office was killing her poor
boy—he looked so pale, and ill, and thin since he left the country.”

Mary had her own opinions, which, seeing Master Harold was the lamb
without blemish in his mother’s eyes, she wisely kept to herself.

But even the fondest parent cannot remain blind for ever, and
accordingly there came a time when Mrs. Drewitt, instead of pitying
Harold, went up to her own room and wept; when she prayed him not to go
to the hotel where Maxwell stopped while in town; when she entreated him
to remember that by business he must live, and that to live he must
work.

“Look at Brian,” she would remark, and the injudicious speech invariably
made Harold shrug his shoulders, and declare Brian “never was fit to be
anything better than a horse in a mill, or a counting-house drudge, or a
pack-horse which he is,” finished Harold.

“Now Harold, do not say such things of Brian!” Geraldine would exclaim,
her cheeks all a-glow; and then Harold was wont to laugh and declare—

“Oh! we all know what makes you so much in love with business; don’t we,
mamma?” For which speech Mrs. Drewitt invariably pulled Master Harold
up.

In her heart, the mother did not like the way in which Mr. Gyton was
always running over from London; she wished he would either propose or
stay away; and yet, she argued, a man must have some opportunity for
making up his mind. Altogether, I cannot say that the position of the
Drewitt family was one perfectly free from care; but still time
travelled on—on—and the seasons came and went; as they will do whether
people are happy or whether people are sad.

It was summer once again, and though there is, perhaps, no town in the
three kingdoms where the country seems so near and stone walls so little
oppressive as in Dublin, still Mrs. Drewitt missed the hills, and the
valleys, and the ocean more than she would have cared to acknowledge.

She had loved Connemara, and she had no other strong ties to any part of
the earth. Mother, and father, and sister, had long been dead, and Sir
Everard Ebbutt was married again, and her sister’s children were very
aristocratic young ladies, who would scarcely have cared to acknowledge
so very unfortunate an individual for their aunt. Altogether, she
mourned greatly after Connemara; and many a time when she and Geraldine
were walking along the Monkstown Road, or pacing slowly round Stephen’s
Green, or sitting at work together in their modestly-furnished house,
the poor lady would say suddenly—“I wonder how the Twelve Pins are
looking now, Geraldine! Must not the sea seem calm seen from Durrow
cliffs to-day?”

“Yes, mamma,” Geraldine would answer, a little guiltily conscious that
she had kept one thing from her mother’s knowledge, viz., her own
refusal of Durrow and its owner.

She could have given a comfortable home for life to her mother; she
could have let her spend all the remaining days of her life among the
scenes and the people she had learned to love so much—and she refused.

Had she done right? For many days Geraldine pondered that question over
and over again, and at last took courage and referred it to her mother.

They were sitting side by side in one of the loneliest parts of the
Phœnix Park—in one of the glades where the blackberries and the brambles
grow—and in reply to a remark from Mrs. Drewitt about the quietness
being pleasant—about the place being almost as solitary as Connemara,
though so close to Dublin, Geraldine took her mother’s hand in hers, and
said—

“Mamma, I want to ask you something.”

“He has proposed”—that was Mrs. Drewitt’s first idea, as she beheld her
daughter’s face covered with blushes. “He has proposed”—the “he” having
reference to Mr. Gyton, who was at that present moment galloping as fast
as an Irish horse could take him to the Broadstone Terminus, whence he
was soon travelling, at the mild pace which seems in fashion across the
Channel by express train, to Galway.

“I wish you were coming down with me, old fellow,” he said to Harold
Drewitt, who had accompanied him to the station, and who stood with his
hands on the top of the carriage-door, looking very wretched and
disconsolate. “A run into Connemara would do you good.”

“I know that,” Harold answered, mournfully.

“Ask leave, and come down on Saturday. There is a car or something that
will bring you on from Galway to Durrow in no time; and my uncle will be
delighted. Shall I tell him to expect you?”

“I don’t see how I can get away,” hesitated the boy.

“Nonsense; come on Saturday. Remember, I shall be looking out for you.
Good-bye;” and the train steamed away from the platform, and went
leisurely onwards into the country, through which it proceeded in a
quiet, inoffensive manner, stopping at all sorts of out-of-the-way
stations, where no passengers ever seemed to get in or out; but where
there was always a knot of idlers collected to see the engine and
carriages, as if the seventh wonder of the world were taking a little
tour through Ireland.

“No need to ask where one is,” remarked Mr. Gyton to a fellow-passenger,
who chanced to be an Englishman; “battered hats, short pipes, ragged
clothes, and plenty of policemen—I beg their pardons—of the constabulary
force.”

“Fine country, though,” returned the other.

“So I am told,” answered Mr. Gyton, drily.

“And the women are so handsome! positively, walking about Dublin
yesterday I met hundreds of lovely faces!” pursued the enthusiastic
gentleman.

“The town is not large,” remarked his companion, “and it is within the
bounds of possibility that you may have met the same faces many times
over.”

“There is something in that,” considered the other.

“And then,” continued Mr. Gyton, “if you pay particular attention to the
subject, you will observe that most Irishwomen—the women who wear gloves
I mean—have a slit in those gloves; either the lady’s hands are plump,
in which case there is an aperture at the side; or her fingers are long,
in which case the nails make a way for themselves through the tips; or
the articles are of an inferior make, which causes gaps about the thumb;
or they are too small and refuse to button. I have made gloves my
study,” added the young swell, “and I never get into a railway carriage
in which there is a woman without adding to my stock of information. I
came up from Kingston yesterday morning with a young lady, and ’pon my
honour, for a long time I thought she had on mittens, she was out at
every finger down to the middle joint!” and Mr. Gyton complacently
surveyed his own well-fitting glove, towards which his companion’s
glance was directed also.

“Do you smoke?” asked the younger man at last, presenting his cigar-case
to the stranger, who, being graciously pleased to avail himself of the
offered politeness, lit one of Mr. Gyton’s prime Havannah’s, and the
pair, stretched out one on each side the carriage, smoked until they
reached Mullingar, at which point they abandoned their cigars, and
wrapping their railway rugs about them, slept on until the guard came to
collect the tickets and to inform them that “when they reached the next
station they’d be there.”

“A most original and valuable piece of information,” remarked Mr. Gyton,
yawning desperately. “Now what do you make out of that—we shall be
there? Does he mean we shall be at the next station, or at Galway?”

“The next station is Galway,” replied the other, referring to his
time-table, and failing in his sleepy condition of mind to perceive any
wit in the young man’s brilliant remarks. “I hope we shall be able to
get a bed.”

“If my memory serves me right, you can have your choice of about a
hundred,” answered Mr. Gyton, collecting his effects, and humming an
opera air softly the while.

This was the “he” to whom Mrs. Drewitt had mentally referred while
waiting for the rest of her daughter’s sentence.

“You often talk, mamma, about Connemara, about the mountains, about the
Atlantic, about those exquisite little bays, about all sorts of things,
in fact,” and Geraldine leaned back her head till it rested gently on
her mother’s shoulder as she spoke.

“Yes, love.”

“Would you like to go back there, mamma—to go back to live?” and the
evening sunbeams rested upon the pair, upon the deep mourning of mother
and child, and Mrs. Drewitt stretched out her hand towards those patches
of golden light as though she could touch and retain them.

“That would depend, Geraldine,” was the answer. “Home to me is now
wherever my children are: if you were all in Connemara, I should love
the West; as you are all in Dublin, I am learning to love it.”

“You are certain, sure, positive, mamma?” and the pretty head nestled
itself down on Mrs. Drewitt’s shoulder as well as was possible,
considering the millinery miracle, which seemed to be stuck on the top
of various rolls and twists of hair.

“Certain, love—why?”

“Because, mamma, we might all go back to Connemara—to Durrow, if I were
to marry Mr. Pryor. He asked me, and I said no; and I have been
miserable ever since—wondering, wondering, whether it was not wrong of
me, when we are so poor, and when he likes me so much.”

There was a long pause, during the continuance of which Mrs. Drewitt
held her daughter’s hand in hers, and stroked it slowly, slowly, very
tenderly and very gently. “Poor fellow,” she said at last, and Geraldine
began to cry.

“Have I done wrong, mamma? have I done wrong?”

“You do not love him, darling?”

“No, mamma.”

“You would only marry him for the sake of Durrow, and for what he might
do for me, and Harold, and Brian?”

“Yes, mamma.”

“Then, Geraldine, you have done quite right, and never vex yourself
about the matter again. Only it is a pity of him, a great pity!” And
Mrs. Drewitt took up her daughter’s hand and commenced stroking it once
more.

“I felt that, mamma,” whispered the girl, “but I could not help saying
no. I thought only of myself then; but I have cried myself to sleep many
a night since thinking about you.”

“My poor child, and I never knew anything about it,” said the mother,
sorrowfully.

“I could not tell you. I was afraid you might be vexed; I was vexed
myself, and——” Geraldine paused.

“And what, darling?”

“Oh, mamma—mamma!” that was all the confession, but it was enough. The
mother knew the daughter’s secret, and the daughter knew her mother
understood the whole business without another word being spoken.

They sat on for a few minutes longer in silence, and then they walked
home side by side still saying little, and that little having no
connexion with the subject on which they had been talking.

Mrs. Drewitt made no further remark, asked no single question; only that
night Geraldine cried herself to sleep in her mother’s arms, and until
the early dawning of the summer’s morning Mrs. Drewitt lay awake, and
thought about the husband she should see no more on earth—about the
children whose future seemed so vague and uncertain.

As for Harold, the household had not much of a life with him until he
worried permission out of his employers to grant him a holiday; then the
very same evening he started for Galway, and worked his way thence by
car and coach to Duranmore, where Mr. Pryor met him.

“Your letter and Frank’s must have crossed each other,” he said. “He
left for Dublin by the first train from Galway this morning, and wrote
begging you to meet him at Morrison’s.”

“Then we both slept at the same hotel last night, and never knew it,”
said Harold, disconsolately; “will he be long in Dublin?”

“Only a few hours. He leaves to-night for Holyhead, and is going direct
to London. His father is very ill.”

“Wasn’t it queer we should both be in Galway together, and never know
it?” repeated Harold, whose sympathies were none of the keenest, and who
would not have cared very much who lived or who died providing he had
his little comforts. Mr. Pryor, though he had known much more singular
coincidences in the course of his life, agreed that it was “queer,”
while he gave the reins into Harold’s eager hands, and let him drive
back the ten miles to Durrow within the hour.

For the sister’s sake, Mr. Pryor was very kind to Harold, though his
affection for the boy had never been overwhelming, and consequently
Harold found his stay in Connemara exceedingly pleasant, and was very
much grieved—grieved to the extent of tears when he had to leave.

“My time’s up,” he said disconsolately to Mr. Pryor.

“Is it?” remarked that gentleman, sympathetically; “you must get leave
to come and see me next summer.”

“Ay; but that is such a devilishly long time to look forward to,”
answered Harold.

“You must employ the interval in breaking yourself of a bad habit,” Mr.
Pryor observed.

“What is that?” asked Harold, quickly.

“Swearing,” was the reply.

“There is no harm in swearing—everybody swears but you.” Fancy the
feelings of a middle-aged gentleman who was obliged to bear such
speeches from a lad. “I don’t know a fellow that does not swear,” went
on Harold; “but then, to be sure, all the fellows I know are young.”

It would have done Mr. Pryor good to have pitched Master Harold over
Durrow cliff, but one cannot always do as one likes in this world, and
he refrained.

“You may think I am making fun,” resumed the boy, “but I am not: next
summer does seem a devilishly long time away—that is not swearing, it is
truth—and I sometimes feel when I am looking about me here that I am
looking for the last time—you may laugh if you like, but I do.”

“What does the lad mean?” marvelled Mr. Pryor.

“Just what I say; neither more nor less: maybe I may see Connemara
again, and maybe not; but most likely maybe not. I have gone to every
place I could think of to-day, and said good-bye. I feel that I shall
never come back—never—never—never.” And the boy twined his hand in the
branch of a tree against which he was leaning, and, resting his head on
his arm, sobbed aloud.

“Would you rather not return to Dublin at present?” asked Mr. Pryor, who
really felt impressed by Harold’s grief. “I dare say I could make it all
right with your employers.”

“No, I must go; but I feel that I am going to meet my fate, Mr. Pryor.
If nothing comes of this, try not to think me a fool; but last night,
after I had been asleep for about a couple of hours, I awoke suddenly in
a cold perspiration and shivering from head to foot. I think you might
have heard my heart beating. I was in such a horrible fright, and there
was a dread on me—a terror; what was it? tell me;” and the boy turned
towards Mr. Pryor, who thought in a moment about Maxwell’s words—

“He will never make old bones;” and it was true, poor Harold!

“You were ill,” answered the Englishman.

“I was not ill,” returned Harold, petulantly; “I was as well as you are.
I had neither ache nor pain; only I was frightened: what frightened me?”

“You had been dreaming disagreeably.”

“I had not been dreaming at all—at least if I had I do not remember
anything about it. I was wide awake in the time you could clap hands,
and thinking about death, about Eversbeg, and my father, and that Kathie
I used to hear them talking over when I was a child, Maxwell’s sister. I
have not got over it all day. Do you think me a coward? I am not a
coward; only—only it would be very hard to die and never see Connemara
again.”

“It would, Harold; but I hope and believe you will see Connemara many
times before you die. And promise me you will go (quietly by yourself,
you know, and without alarming your mother) to some good doctor in
Dublin, and tell him you are not very strong, and to give you a tonic.
Promise me,” and Mr. Pryor slipped a five-pound note into the boy’s
hand, which Harold looked at as well as tears would let him see it.

“In the night,” he began again, “I made good resolutions—I did, Mr.
Pryor. I wish I could keep to them; I wish I could quit drinking; I wish
I could quit vexing my mother. I’d like to be good. I think it must be
nice to feel good when one wakes like that in the middle of the night.”

That was the note on which Harold’s sentences began and ended. It was
the common chord of his conversation, until at last Mr. Pryor became
really anxious.

“He would not get on this way if he were in good health,” thought the
barrister, and when he parted with Harold he felt many misgivings as to
whether he ought not to go so far as Galway, at any rate, with him.

But there was an absurdity on the face of the thing which could not fail
to strike Mr. Pryor. “The boy does not want to go to work,” he thought;
“and if we make too much of him he will never be good for anything.” As
for spirits, out of doors, Harold’s were at fever-point!

“Put on your hat, man,” he said to Doyle, who, happy as a lord, was
standing flourishing an old white apology for a hat in the middle of the
High Street of Duranmore. “I have often been drunk myself.”

“And good-bye, Kitty,” he called out to the barmaid at the “Marsden
Arms;” “see that you’re married against I come back.”

“Good-bye, Maxwell,” he shouted to his cousin, who was riding by on
Trumpeter. “Take care of Madcap; I may come down some day in the hunting
season, and ask you to let me have one more ride on her.”

“And good-bye, Mr. Pryor,” he said last of all; “try not to think about
what I told you—it was folly.”

“Write to me what the doctor says, Harold,” entreated his friend. “All
right,” was the reply and the car drove off.

Far as he could see, Harold kept waving first his hat and then his
handkerchief, and it was not until the vehicle turned round the base of
the mountain, on the road to Cragantlet, and bore the boy off through
the land of a hundred lakes, that Mr. Pryor could prevail on himself to
retrace his way towards Durrow.

He could not sleep for thinking of the lad; he resolved to write to
Brian concerning him, and he worried himself almost into a fever over
the matter, while Harold was shouting out snatches of songs, and
pitching compliments to the pretty country girls as they tripped so
daintily over the grass by the wayside, and telling stories, and
plaguing the driver’s life out to let him take the reins, and eating his
supper in Galway, and lying fast asleep in one of those huge beds at the
Railway Hotel in which Finn Macoul and his whole family might find ample
accommodation.

“If you believe me, sir,” said Harold, who travelled second class, to a
fat priest, his fellow-passenger in the railway carriage the next
morning as far as Athlone, “I had to ring the bell to find myself; I
went wandering over the bed every time I woke trying to discover where
on earth I was;” and the priest took kindly to the boy, and laughed at
his light-hearted merriment, and all the way up to Dublin, Harold found
some one to talk to, some one to laugh with.

As he drove along the streets leading from Broadstone Station to
Carlisle Bridge, he chatted to the car-driver, and was, as the man
expressed it, “in great heart entirely.” “Pull up at the first shop,”
ordered Mr. Harold, “where they sell cigars; I want some;” and obedient
to the command the man pulled up at a shop in College Green, where
Harold alighted.

He picked over the cigars with the air of a connoisseur, and, selecting
three dozen of what he considered the best, threw down a pound-note in
payment. He had changed Mr. Pryor’s gift in Galway, and this pound-note
was part of the proceeds.
