“I must send out for silver, sir,” said the man behind the counter, who
had never taken his eyes off Harold’s face (when Harold was not looking
at him) for a moment.

“All right,” remarked Harold.

“Sorry to keep you so long waiting,” remarked the shopkeeper, politely
handing his customer a chair.

“It is of no consequence;” but Harold was kicking his heels with
impatience for all that.

Time went on. “I think,” said the lad, “they must be quarrying that
silver.”

“Not at all,” said the shopkeeper; and at the words his messenger
re-entered the place, in company with a policeman.

“There is your prisoner,” remarked the cigar-dealer; and the policeman
laid his hand on Harold’s shoulder.

“Come along, young gentleman,” he said. “The less fuss you make, the
better.”

Advice which Harold followed by striking the policeman right between the
eyes, and then asking what the devil he meant by his cursed impudence.

“It is your cursed impudence, I think,” said the shopkeeper, “coming
here to change another of your notes.”

“I never set foot in your doors before, to my knowledge,” answered
Harold, amazed.

“Well, you have to mine, which is more to the purpose,” was the reply.

“Will you tell me what you all mean? Are you all mad?” asked Harold, who
began to feel that the affair was getting serious.

Then half a dozen people began talking at once, and all Harold could
make out as they dragged him along the streets was—

“Robbery of two thousand pounds—fifty pounds reward—assaulting a
constable in the discharge of his duty—passing the stopped notes.”

“But now look here, sir,” remarked Harold, to the inspector. “I know no
more what you are all talking about than a baby in arms. That is my name
and address; and I have just come up from Galway, where I have been
staying at Durrow Park with Mr. Geoffry Pryor.”

“You are not bound to criminate yourself,” interposed the inspector.
“What you say after this caution will be used in evidence against you.”

“Let it. I am trying to vindicate myself—though, upon my conscience, I
have not the remotest idea of the nature of the crime with which you
charge me. I tell you what it is,” he added, “you have got the wrong
bird in crib; you must have mistaken me for somebody else; before God, I
know nothing of any two thousand pounds!” he added passionately. “I
never saw so much money together in all my life—I never did!”

“If you can persuade his worship of that, it will be all the better for
you,” was the incredulous reply; and forthwith Harold was locked up, but
not before he sent this message to his mother:—

“Tell her,” he said to a policeman, “that I am in the station-house; I
have not the remotest idea why. I have done nothing. I cannot think what
you have brought me here for—unless you are all mad.”

But though the police delivered this message with tolerable accuracy,
they persisted in hunting over all his little possessions, trying to
find some trace of the missing money—of something to criminate the boy.

“You see he is innocent, you see,” Mrs. Drewitt kept saying, in her
frantic sorrow.

“Yes, ma’am, we see,” answered the police; and all through that long,
long night Geraldine and her mother kept repeating, as if to reassure
their own faith, “He is innocent! Harold could never have done a thing
like that!”

 CHAPTER IX.
 An Unfortunate Likeness.


Next day Harold Drewitt was brought before Mr. Boice, charged with
having on the first of August, in the year of our Lord eighteen hundred
and fifty-four, stolen two thousand pounds, the property of the Dundalk
and Drogheda Banking Company; when the following evidence was produced:—

Jeremiah Kearns having been sworn, deposed, that on the day in question
he was an up passenger by the five o’clock express train from Dundalk to
Drogheda. Had with him two thousand pounds in notes, and one thousand
pounds in gold and silver. The gold and silver was packed in a wooden
box hooped with iron; the notes he carried in a black bag. Asked the
guard not to put any one in the carriage with him, and his request was
complied with, until the train arrived at Dunleer, about eight miles
from Drogheda. At that station the guard said he would be obliged to
give him a fellow-passenger, but it was only a young gentleman going on
to Dublin. Seeing the prisoner, made no objection, and they travelled on
together to Drogheda, where the witness got out.

When he reached the Bank, he found he had lost his bag; spoke to the
car-driver about it, but the car-driver was positive no bag had been
among the luggage, nothing but a portmanteau and the box. Telegraphed at
once to Dublin to search the carriages on the arrival of the train in
Amiens-street. Telegraphed also that the guard would recollect witness’s
fellow-passenger.

Telegraphed, and sent messages to all their branches, stopping payment
of the notes.

Several of the notes had been changed by Dublin shopkeepers during the
period which had elapsed between the first and the fifteenth.

Had received a telegram from the inspector on the previous evening, and
identified the prisoner at the police-station. He was dressed
differently on the day in question, and his hair was now a little
longer, but he had no hesitation in swearing to him as the youth who had
got in at Dunleer.

Prisoner assisted him to lift the box out at Drogheda, and made a remark
to the effect that it must have something of value inside. Witness did
not suspect such a respectable-looking lad, travelling first class, and
belonging apparently to the upper ranks, of stealing his bag. He thought
he, witness, had forgotten the bag, and that if any other passengers got
in between Drogheda and Dublin, prisoner would be able to give
information concerning them. Had heard the description given by several
Dublin tradesmen of the person who had changed the notes, and identified
that description as answering to his fellow-passenger and the prisoner
at the bar.

Had no hesitation in declaring the two to be one and the same person.
Was confident he never mentioned that he was carrying money with him;
but thought the guard made some joke about there being no fear of that
young gentleman robbing him. Was in the habit of taking money from one
bank to another; the guard knew him perfectly well.

Peter Monaghan being sworn, stated, that on the night in question he was
acting as guard on the up express train, which arrived at Dundalk at
five minutes to five o’clock. Recollected Mr. Kearns getting in at that
station, and requesting him, witness, to keep the compartment he
occupied empty if possible. Knew Mr. Kearns well. At Dunleer there were
an unusual number of passengers waiting for the up train, and
consequently asked Mr. Kearns’ permission to put prisoner in the same
compartment. He was such a genteel-looking young gentleman that he
thought there could be no objection. Believed he did make some remark to
that effect, but could not be just certain. At Drogheda Mr. Kearns got
out, and bade witness good-evening. When witness collected the tickets
prisoner was in the train. When the express reached Dublin the
station-master told witness about the telegraphic message, and they both
asked the prisoner if Mr. Kearns had left a bag in the carriage.
Prisoner said, not to his knowledge—that he had not noticed any article
at all when the gentleman got out at Drogheda. Had he done so, he would
have mentioned it to him, witness, at Balbriggan. Several passengers got
into the same compartment at Balbriggan, and one lady who got in at
Drogheda travelled on to Malahide, where every one in that particular
carriage got out except the prisoner. About a mile on the Dublin side of
Malahide a black bag was found next morning by a labourer working on the
line. It must have been thrown out of the right-hand window of the
carriage, as it lay between the metals on the down line. He thought from
the young gentleman’s manner that Mr. Kearns must have taken the bag out
of the train, and lost it between the station and the Bank. Never
suspected the prisoner.

Had identified him at the station-house the previous evening, but should
not have known him in the “wide-awake” he wore. He had on a hat at
Dunleer. A hat was sent for and tried on. He was able to swear to him
then, though he had fallen away in flesh during the fortnight.

Michael Brinny, tobacconist, was then sworn and examined. Had a shop in
College Green. Recollected the first of August. Just as his shutters
were being put up on the evening of that day, prisoner came in, and
bought a pipe and some Cavendish tobacco. He had not silver enough to
pay for these articles, and so he laid down a pound-note, out of which
witness returned him twelve and threepence change. Never saw him before,
and never saw him since, until the previous afternoon, when he came to
his shop again, and purchased three dozen of cigars, offering a Bank of
Ireland one-pound note in payment.

Witness made an excuse about having to send out for change, and kept him
in conversation till his assistant fetched a policeman. Prisoner was
very violent, struck the policeman in the face, declared he did not know
what they were talking about, and gave a great deal of trouble before he
was removed to the station-house. Knew him the moment he came into the
shop, though his hair had grown longer, and his face thinner since he
changed the first note. Thought he tried to disguise his voice, and that
he spoke with more of a West of Ireland accent than when he bought the
Cavendish.

John Garvan, Daniel Laharan, Thomas Molrhany, and several other
respectable shopkeepers identified the prisoner as having made small
purchases, and changed notes at their establishments; while Barry
Croggan, who appeared with a great lump on his forehead, gave an account
of the assault committed on him by the prisoner; and stated that, but
for the assistance rendered by Mr. Brinny and his shopman, he could not
have retained his prisoner until another policeman came to help him.

In answer to all these charges, Harold, having been duly cautioned not
to criminate himself, stated that there must be some mistake; that he
never was at Drogheda in his life; that he knew nothing whatever of the
robbery, never having even heard of it till the policeman told him about
it the previous day. Very earnestly Harold protested his innocence; he
said he had not gone out of Dublin the whole summer, except for a week,
the entire of which time he had spent with Mr. Pryor, of Durrow Park.
Being asked by the magistrate if he could recollect where he was on the
first of August, he said he could not; but upon being told what day of
the week the first fell on, he declared he had spent the whole of it in
the company of Mr. Francis Gyton, who could swear to the fact.

Being requested to send for Mr. Francis Gyton, he said he did not know
where that gentleman was; which reply produced a malevolent chuckle from
the “cloud of witnesses,” and a statement from police constable Croggan
to the effect that prisoner was anything but a respectable young
gentleman, though he believed he had respectable friends. Many of
Harold’s proceedings were recounted for the magistrate’s edification;
and whilst every old sin, every mad prank, every boyish folly was
brought up in judgment against him, the lad leaned forward and covered
his face with his hands to hide the tears of shame and mortification
that he had not strength enough to keep back.

He had never been alone in any trouble before—never; and now, in the
hour of his bitterest trial, he had not a soul to speak a word in his
favour.

“If you give me time, sir,” he said at last, looking up and addressing
the magistrate, “I will be able to prove I never was near the place;
that it is all a mistake—” and then he gave a cry of joy, for Brian, who
he thought was far away from Dublin, entered the court at the moment,
and offered to find bail to any amount for his reappearance.

If he had not assaulted the officer, most probably his brother’s
application would have been listened to; but as it was, the magistrate
refused to entertain the idea even for a moment.

He could not think of accepting bail in so serious a case, he remarked,
and remanded the prisoner.

“I am innocent, Brian. I know nothing about the money. Don’t let them
take me back there!” panted out the poor lad in his terror and distress;
but Brian could do nothing except go to a solicitor and place the case
in his hands, write to Mr. Pryor, and send a telegram to London begging
that Mr. Gyton would, if possible, come back to Dublin without delay.

Then he went home, and it was the hardest day’s work Brian Drewitt ever
performed, facing his mother without Harold. Poor lady! poor mother!
though she did not believe one word of the accusation they had brought
against her youngest born, still she wept till she had no more tears
left to shed at the thought of her boy being in prison—of her darling
being dragged to the dock and tried like a common felon.

“I did all I could,” said Brian, hoarsely. “I offered to get bail, but
they refused it. I was not there time enough to get a solicitor for him,
but I have found one since. I have written to Mr. Pryor and telegraphed
for Mr. Gyton. Is there anything else you would like tried? Is there
anywhere else you would like me to go? Tell me. You have nothing to do
but say what you would wish, and I will endeavour to accomplish it,
mother.”

It would not be possible in a whole volume to describe the intense
tenderness—the womanly compassion Brian managed to put into that one
word; and as he spoke it Mrs. Drewitt went up to him and, without
speaking a sentence, flung her arms round his neck and kissed him over
and over again.

“Mother,” he said, “mother darling,” and he touched her hair, which was
growing grey, grey, and he kissed her forehead, and he let her poor
weary head lie on his breast, but her plaint was still, “Harold, Harold,
my boy, my boy!”

“I could do no more, mother,” Brian repeated, “and nobody can do
anything more for a week yet. Mr. Gyton will be over here by that time,
and then Harold is sure to be liberated at once.”

“Brian,” the dear fingers fidgeted with his neck-tie—with the studs in
his shirt, “Brian.”

“Yes, mother.”

“Where did Harold get all the money he has lately had to spend?”

“I have given him some,” answered Brian.

“But not enough. I should fear not enough.”

In a moment Brian saw where the sting lay; in an instant he perceived
that though she would not believe, still that she dreaded.

“Make your mind easy,” he answered confidently. “Wherever Harold had his
money from, it was not got dishonestly. There is some great mistake, but
I could stake my life he is as innocent as I am; whatever the world may
think, we must not turn against him—must we?”

She placed a hand on each shoulder, and looked Brian full in the face.

“You are not saying that to comfort me?” she asked. “You have no doubt
yourself?”

“Not the slightest.”

“You feel confident he was with Mr. Gyton?”

“I know he was. At least, I remember your writing to me on the evening
of the first that Mr. Gyton had passed that day through Dublin on his
way to Durrow, and that Harold had seen him off from the Broadstone
Station. Harold could not have been at Broadstone and Drogheda on the
same day; besides, he says he never was at Drogheda in his life. And
whatever his faults may be, he is neither a thief nor a liar. Make your
mind easy. It is a case of mistaken identity; but it is confoundedly
hard lines for Harold to be locked up instead of the fellow who really
did steal the notes.”

“You think they were stolen then?” said Mrs. Drewitt.

“They were certainly stolen, and by some one very like Harold; but I
have never seen anybody like Harold in my life. It is a strange
business, and we must wait with patience for the mystery to be
explained.”

“It is not easy to have patience, Brian,” remarked Mrs. Drewitt, and
then she began crying again about her boy—her poor delicate boy, while
Brian thought within himself that if the one-half he had heard during
the day concerning Harold’s mode of life were true, it was no wonder he
grew delicate, and pale, and thin.

“Not home any night till well on in the small hours, Mister Brian, as I
am a living sinner,” remarked Mary-of-all-work to him, “and dhrunk five
nights out of the six, and not at the office regular as he ought be. I’m
just telling _you_,” she finished, “for I wouldn’t like that the
misthress would hear anything of it. Dear pity her poor sowl! She thinks
the sun was made to rise and set for him.” All of which was exceedingly
agreeable information for a young man to hear who had his own way to
make in the world, and who had not found his path so far strewed at all
with roses.

“Never mind,” he thought, as he flung himself wearily on his bed, “it is
all in the day’s work, we shall find rest sometime.”

Possibly, but the day occasionally proves very long, and the rest is
frequently delayed for an unreasonable period. So Brian discovered, as
the years went on, bringing little repose with them.

The Dublin papers were posted as regularly to Kincorth as “The Times” to
Durrow, and accordingly Mr. Maxwell Drewitt had the pleasure of seeing
his cousin’s name figuring largely in a column headed,

“Robbery on the Dublin and Drogheda Railway. Arrest of the Supposed
Thief.”

“That is the end of you, Master Harold, is it?” remarked Maxwell, as he
finished reading the report. “I wish it had been Brian instead. Why,
bless my soul! he must have been caught the very day he returned from
Galway. I will go up to hear if he can prove his alibi, though I dare
swear it is a lie, and that he was not with Gyton at all. He will very
likely be transported.” Having settled Harold’s affairs in which summary
fashion, Maxwell went about the house and grounds in his usual manner,
and slept soundly, not knowing what the post was bringing to him.

He got his missive while he sat at breakfast, and opened it dreading no
evil.

“I should like to see you at once,” he read. “Come to Dublin
immediately, if possible. It is important that you should do so.”

“What can the fellow mean?” marvelled the owner of Kincorth. “What on
earth can he want?” Then all in a moment he remembered the trial, and
connected the two things together.

“My God!” he thought, “can he have been mixed up in that? Have they
mistaken the one for the other?”

He looked for the newspaper which he had put aside in his desk, and
spelled over every word of the evidence. “Thinner—hair longer—more of a
Connaught accent—did not know him till he put on a hat,” he repeated to
himself, “and they have got that other boy locked up for it.”

He put the paper away again, told his servant to pack his portmanteau,
and to order post-horses, “For I must catch the night mail from Galway
to Dublin,” he explained to the man, who did not seem to understand
things, while he invoked a hundred curses on the head of his wife, who
querulously remarked that he was always going from home.

“And always likely to do till you are out of it,” he answered, savagely.
They had been living a more wretched life than ever, lately, for Lady
Emmeline had unexpectedly turned restive, and refused to fall in with
her husband’s plans, as he had fondly hoped she would.

“Anything but that,” she said, firmly. “Anything but another woman’s
child;” and she relapsed into hysterics whenever he ventured to approach
the subject, and adopting the mode of warfare most in favour with her
sex, of digging a moat round her citadel and filling it with tears,
rendered her position alike invulnerable to threats and entreaties.

He might have known he had no chance of success with her, and by holding
his tongue have kept peace, at any rate; but he preferred trying to
compass his end, and the result was a state of domestic warfare which
caused the servants to declare emphatically that Kincorth was like hell.

“They never are but bickering and quarrelling, and snapping at one
another from morning till night,” said Lady Emmeline’s maid to a select
assemblage in the servants’ hall. “It was only this morning I heard
master saying he could wait till she was dead; and mistress answered she
would live to spite him.”

This was what marriage had done for Maxwell Drewitt and for Lady
Emmeline. He had paid his price and got the article he coveted, and this
was the result. No wonder he spent much of his time in Dublin; no wonder
his heart—for he had some human feelings, some need for human
affection—turned hopefully towards his son, whom he meant to make a
great man of one day.

A great man! Alas! already his dream castle was tottering to its
foundation. It was to prop it up and save it he hastened to Dublin,
where the first person he went to seek was a schoolmaster, grave and
discreet, who resided near that loveliest of all sea-bathing villages,
Bray.

“You wanted to speak to me, Mr. Blosse,” began Maxwell; he had travelled
all night and looked haggard and exhausted. “You wanted to speak to me.”

“Yes, Mr. Drewitt, particularly,” was the reply. “I think we might take
a walk if you are not tired. I should like to say what I have got to say
out of doors.”

“Let us go then,” and Maxwell put on his hat.

“You will have some breakfast?”

“Not any; I am ready to go if you are.”

“Will you not take a glass of wine?”

“At this hour? no, thank you, Mr. Blosse; let us go,” and he moved
nervously towards the door.

When they were once out into the open air, he turned towards his
companion inquiringly; but Mr. Blosse said, “Not yet,” and they walked
on—on to the very top of Bray Head; where, unmindful of the view,
unmindful of everything in earth, or sea, or heaven, Maxwell demanded
what was the matter; what Mr. Blosse had to say.

“That trial, Mr. Drewitt—you know the trial I mean?”

“Yes,” Maxwell answered.

“Is not the accused some relative of yours?”

“My first cousin—go on.”

“I take it for granted there must be some extraordinary likeness between
him and Arthur,” continued Mr. Blosse.

“I do not know why you should take anything of the kind for granted, Mr.
Blosse,” said Maxwell, stiffly. “I really do not.”

“The youth who is in custody did not take that money, Mr. Drewitt,”
observed Mr. Blosse, “and Arthur did; therefore I say some extraordinary
likeness must exist between them.”

He had been expecting this all along. He had hoped for nothing different
from the moment he opened his letters the previous morning. He had faced
the question as he drove past the desolate lakes, under the shadow of
the lonely hills. He had not concealed the probability of such an
announcement greeting him on his arrival at Bray; and yet now, when the
blow did fall, Maxwell felt it in every nerve of his body—in every vein
of his heart.

He did not know till then what hopes he had built on the chance of what
this handsome illegitimate flesh of his flesh and blood of his blood
might turn out. He had not realized what dreams he had cherished about
him; how he had been turning year after year more and more to this one
thing which belonged to him, which he called his own on earth, till,
standing on Bray Head, with the blue sea washing a far different strand
from that whereon he was accustomed to gaze below him, with the clear
sky above him, he heard Mr. Blosse speak words that might have been read
out of a death-warrant, judging by the effect they produced.

For a long time Maxwell stood silent; for a long time he remained
staring down on the sea, while he swallowed, drop by drop, the contents
of that bitter cup. Then when at last he could speak—when at last his
voice would answer to his will, he asked Mr. Blosse to explain
himself—to tell him all the particulars.

“Begin at the beginning,” said Maxwell, “and do not stop till you get to
the end.”

“He spent his holidays, as you know,” commenced Mr. Blosse, “partly at
Killarney, with you, and partly with one of his schoolfellows, whose
father has a property near Carlow. He was to have returned here on the
first of August; but he never made his appearance until the evening of
the third, when he stated that he had gone down with a friend to the
North, and that he had come back from Derry that day. I asked him who
his friend was? and he said an officer who was going to be married to
Grey’s sister. I knew Grey’s sister was engaged to an officer, and so I
never thought of disbelieving his story at the time, though I have since
had reason to think he did not go to Derry at all, but only to
Carlingford, in company with some not very desirable acquaintances.”

“Go on,” interrupted Maxwell. “It is the last part of the story I want
to hear. What makes you think Arthur——”

“Is the culprit? I think so—because I found the notes secreted in his
bedroom. I noticed something strange about him ever since his return,
and determined to watch his movements. I remarked that he was
perpetually making excuses to leave the library; that he was always
going upstairs; that——”

“Go on!” entreated Maxwell. “I do not want to know what you noticed; I
want to know what you found.”

“I found there was a short piece of board loose in his room, and that
these notes were under it—or, rather, not under it, but pushed far away
to one side below the sound boards. I should never have guessed it but
for his own over-anxiety. He could not rest away from the packet; he was
always fancying somebody had discovered it.”

Maxwell tried hard to keep his hands off the man who had dogged and
watched and trapped his son; and who now sat and told him—not without a
certain subdued triumph—how his sagacity had been rewarded—his trouble
repaid. He dug his nails into his flesh, and looked at his clenched
fists, with the dark look of his wicked youth gathering deeper and
deeper about his eyes.

“Go on!” he repeated; and Mr. Blosse went on.

“I had heard of the robbery,” he said; “I had listened to the story
without any great interest, as one does listen to such things, unless
there is some circumstance of special importance connected with them;
but when I saw the notes I taxed Arthur with the theft, and he confessed
all. How Mr. Kearns left the bag behind him——”

“You can stop now,” Maxwell interrupted; “you have told me all I care to
hear. Now listen to me. I know exactly all you would say, and I can
guess your questions without hearing one of them. First of all, the
money must be sent back to this precious Drogheda Banking Company.
Secondly, this boy—this Harold Drewitt—he must be cleared. He can do
that for himself, I know. Mr. Gyton did leave Dublin for Galway on the
first, and Harold was in his company all the afternoon, at any rate.
Thirdly, you want Arthur out of your house, and you want no fuss,
because you think a fuss would damage you with the parents of your other
pupils. I will take Arthur away with me; and last, but not least, you
desire a pecuniary acknowledgment from me—a suitable thank-offering for
the silence you have maintained, and will maintain, on this subject. Is
there anything which I have overlooked, Mr. Blosse?”

“Nothing. You are thoughtfulness itself,” was the reply.

“Now, you can go back to your house and leave me here for half an hour,”
said Maxwell; “at the end of that time I shall want to see Arthur.”

“But, Mr. Drewitt——”

“I know my own business, sir, and shall transact it after my own
fashion,” interrupted the owner of Kincorth, and he followed the
retreating figure with his eyes as it grew less and less in the
distance—followed it till it was mixed up among other figures, and lost
to view; then Maxwell himself descended slowly to the shore, and walking
along the beach, which was bare and dry, the tide being out, he kicked
the pebbles before him just as he had done so many years previously when
he lounged home to Kincorth by the edge of Duranmore Bay.

He had been worsted, and a man like Maxwell Drewitt does not bear such
an indignity patiently. He had longed to kick Mr. Blosse—to taunt him—to
dare him—to defy him; but he was afraid. Mr. Blosse could transport the
boy. He had father and son in his power, and he only refrained from
using that power because he thought it might injure his reputation as an
instructor of the sons of the “nobility and gentry” if it were known a
felon had come out of such an unexceptionable establishment.

“He has the ball,” reflected Maxwell, “and can make what terms he
chooses, damn him!” and at this point Mr. Drewitt lit a fresh cigar, and
walked on more slowly and leisurely than ever.

He was thinking—thinking what to do with Arthur. “I will take him to
England,” he decided. “I will put him under the care of some good
clergyman, if there is such a _rara avis_ to be found. I will try to
reform him, for he is mine, and he is all I have.”

Oh! lonely and desolate, poor and unregarded. There was something about
the departure of that pair—of that father and son, from under his roof
which touched even Mr. Blosse.

“You may depend upon me, Mr. Drewitt, you may, indeed,” were his parting
words, and he spoke them with a certain warmth of manner upon which
Maxwell’s reply fell like ice.

“I do not doubt it, Mr. Blosse, for you know it will be to your interest
to remain true to me,” and having uttered this pleasant speech, Maxwell
turned his back on Mr. Blosse, and telling the car-driver to take them
leisurely to Kingston, he and Arthur left Bray behind them.

Maxwell did not breathe freely until they reached Holyhead, and were
speeding along through the darkness to Chester. He had never reproached
his son—never spoken to him, in fact, on the subject, and he did not
mean to do so till they were safe in London. The notes were in his
possession; but he could do nothing with them for the present.

Harold was still a prisoner, and “he must remain a prisoner unless Mr.
Gyton can prove a satisfactory alibi,” thought Maxwell. “After all, he
is out of harm’s way where he is, which is more than could be said if he
were at large.” And so far Maxwell was right, for Harold had nothing to
do the whole day long except form good resolutions, and vow, and
declare, and swear he would turn over a new leaf when once he got out of
his present scrape.

He had been up again before the magistrate, but his solicitor had
procured another remand. Mr. Gyton was in the south of France with his
father, but he would return to England immediately. Mr. Pryor pledged
himself to that, and in order to make assurance doubly sure, started off
in search of his nephew. Brian grew dreadfully anxious as day after day
passed over and still brought no tidings of Mr. Gyton’s movements. He
began to feel on how slight a thread Harold’s chance of escape was
hanging. If anything happened to Frank Gyton—if he were killed in a
railway accident—if he caught a fever and died—if he could not swear to
the date—if, like Mrs. Drewitt, Geraldine, and Harold’s employers, he
had attached no definite memory to the day, or the hour, or the date,
why, then Harold would be convicted; without doubt he would be
convicted.

“And he never could live out his sentence,” thought Brian, in despair,
“and it would kill my mother; her hair is getting quite white with this
suspense, and that certainly would kill her.”

He was making some observation of the kind to the solicitor who had
undertaken Harold’s defence, when a telegram arrived from Mr. Gyton.

“Am in London,” ran the message; “shall be in Dublin to-morrow morning.”

“I hope he will,” said the lawyer; “for your brother, remember, is to be
brought up again on Thursday, and nothing can save him but a miracle or
an alibi.”

Which speech sounded anything rather than consolatory, under the
circumstances.

 CHAPTER X.
 Hearing is not believing.


Thursday arrived, and Harold Drewitt was again brought up on remand
before the magistrate.

He had been taken into custody on the 15th of August, and it was now the
middle of September; and as he came into court, two or three people
remarked that the air had not seemed to agree with him—that he evidently
wanted a change. “Which he is not likely to get to-day,” observed one
policeman.

“His witness is here, though, I am told,” was the reply.

“I don’t care if fifty witnesses were here, they could not bring him out
clear,” answered the other, oracularly; and, indeed, this was the
general feeling in the court. Everybody thought Harold had made a
mistake in not pleading guilty, and begging for mercy, as it was his
first offence, and the temptation had been great.

In one way the policeman was right. Mr. Gyton could clear him in law,
but Mr. Gyton could never wash him white in the estimation of the
public; and poor Harold appeared to feel this, as he looked round the
court, and saw every eye fastened on him, not compassionately, but
curiously.

He never seemed to have courage except when he glanced at Brian, who had
been faithful to him through all—faithful, as he had promised his
father, on his death-bed, that he would be to Harold.

There was the magistrate, severe and incredulous; there were some of the
directors of the Dundalk and Drogheda Banking Company, eager for
revenge; there was Mr. Jeremiah Kearns; there were all the witnesses,
mentally checking off the depositions as they were read aloud, and
thinking how impossible it was any man living could prove an alibi in
the face of such overwhelming evidence.

One or two fresh witnesses had been procured by the bank from impossible
regions of the earth: the lady who got into the carriage at Drogheda;
the passengers who had travelled from Balbriggan to Malahide;—all were
there to swear to Harold’s identity with the thief.

The lady was the only person who hesitated about the matter. She could
not swear to the prisoner being her fellow-passenger. “He was very like
him; but—”

“But what, my dear madam?” suggested the solicitor for the prosecution;
and the court waited for her reply, while the lady still hesitated.

“Pray tell us in what respect the prisoner is unlike?” suggested the
magistrate, insinuatingly; for the lady was pretty, and his worship had
eyes in his head.

There was a profound silence in the court, while the witness turned and
looked again at Harold.

Very imploringly he lifted his eyes to hers, and perhaps that settled
the matter.

“I cannot swear to the prisoner being the young gentleman with whom I
travelled from Drogheda. He is very like—but——”

“Pray proceed, we are all attention;” and the solicitor for the
prosecution struck an attitude while he waited for the rest of the
sentence.

“The young gentleman I travelled with had a slight suspicion of a
moustache and beard coming,” said the lady, desperately, “and——”

A roar of laughter, in which the magistrate himself could not help
joining, drowned the remainder of her words.

“You mean, I conclude, that the prisoner’s hirsute appendages have not
as yet made their appearance,” suggested the magistrate.

“He has likely been hungry where he was, and ate them off,” remarked
some one in the rear.

“If I hear any more indecent interruptions,” said his worship, “I will
order the court to be cleared.”

“If ye’d have it cleaned,” observed the same voice; whereupon the police
made a dive into the crowd, and seizing an old woman and a boy, hustled
them outside the door.

Meantime the solicitor for the prosecution was asking the witness, “if
she did not think it possible she was mistaken—that she had been
thinking of some one else?”

Upon which the lady got cross and said, “She was not mistaken, and that
she was not thinking of any one else;” and as nothing more could be made
of her, she was ordered to stand down.

The solicitor for the defence, when it came to his turn, said very
little about the business—as little as he could help. He remarked, that
it was clearly a case of mistaken identity, and observed that the
prisoner had been in Dublin at the very hour when the robbery was said
to have been committed. In support of which he produced Mr. Francis
Gyton, whose appearance made an impression both on the magistrate and
the court generally.

Mr. Francis Gyton being sworn, deposed, that he was a son of Mr. Gyton,
of the firm of Gyton, Lark, Munday, Hatfield, & Co., Austinfriars,
London.

“He has got fathers enough,” remarked the former speaker from among the
crowd, and the police made a fresh dive, and this time carried off in
triumph a woman who had an infant in her arms.

Mr. Gyton proceeded—Knew the prisoner Harold Drewitt very well indeed;
called at his mother’s house, and subsequently at the office of his
employers, on the first of August last. Remembered the day, because he
left London on the evening of the thirty-first of July, by the
twenty-five minutes past eight express, and crossed to Kingston by the
night mail-packet. Was only the one day in Dublin. Left the very evening
of his arrival for Galway. Slept in Galway at the Railway Hotel, and
proceeded the next day to Durrow Park, near Duranmore, the residence of
his uncle, Mr. Henry Pryor. The prisoner dined with him and accompanied
him to the railway station.

Cross-examined.—Was certain he did not stop the night at Chester. He had
a glass of very bad sherry there, and returned to the compartment he
occupied immediately. Was not left behind. Begged the solicitor for the
prosecution would recollect to whom he was speaking. Had not the
slightest idea whether it was the Ulster, Munster, Leinster, or
Connaught, he came over in. If he, the solicitor, sent down to Kingston,
very probably he could find which of those steam-vessels arrived at
Kingston from Holyhead on the morning of the first of August.

Knew they had a good passage. Could not possibly be mistaken as to
Harold’s identity, Had been acquainted with him too long for that. Was
certain he only remained in Dublin one day. Did he, the lawyer, think he
should forget his own name next? Left Broadstone by the half-past seven
train. There was one gentleman in the compartment with him. Arrived in
Galway about twelve. Had some cold beef; remembered it, because he had
never seen such a joint before, or since; and a glass of brandy and
water. Slept at the Railway Hotel. Could not recollect the number of his
room; but knew it had three windows in it, and that all of them would
open. Had never in any other house in Ireland seen a window that would
open, or a door that would shut. Was not in love with Ireland. The
solicitor for the prosecution was perfectly correct in that supposition;
but came often to Ireland to see his uncle. Had frequently been told it
was a fine country, but could not say of his own knowledge that such was
the fact.

Admitted that he liked English customs best. Did not admire Irish
peculiarities when they carried people to the length of shooting
landlords, and locking up an innocent boy for a month, on suspicion of
his having stolen a black bag from a bank clerk. Would swear fifty
times, if the solicitor wished it, that the prisoner could not have been
at Drogheda, because he was at Broadstone; and he, Mr. Gyton, supposed
that even an Irishman was unable to appear in two places at once. Had
kept none of his hotel bills; always tore up the rubbish the moment he
was outside the door; did not consider that a proof of English
extravagance, and could not see, if it were, that it had anything to do
with the matter in hand. Did not know whether the prisoner led a
respectable life or not, and was at a loss to understand what his life
signified to anybody except Mr. Harold Drewitt, and Mr. Harold Drewitt’s
friends. Had come back from France solely for the purpose of giving
evidence in this case; would have come from Jericho for that matter. Was
quite certain prisoner had dined with and accompanied him to the
Broadstone Station. Did not think it at all singular that he could
remember the name of a station while he forgot the name of a boat. Most
probably he had never indeed heard the name of the steamer. Was positive
about the date. How much oftener was the solicitor going to ask him that
question?

Henry Pryor, sworn, stated that his nephew, the last witness, arrived at
his house on the second of August, and told him he had parted with
Harold Drewitt at the Broadstone Terminus of the Midland Great Western
Railway on the preceding evening. His nephew informed him (Mr. Pryor)
that he had asked Harold to come down to Connemara, and that the boy
would endeavour to get permission to do so. Mr. Gyton was suddenly
summoned back to London, in consequence of the dangerous illness of his
father, with whom he subsequently proceeded abroad, and consequently,
when Harold Drewitt did arrive at Durrow, Mr. Gyton had left that place.
Was certain his nephew arrived in Connemara on the second, and that he
said he had not slept in Dublin, but in Galway. He had known Harold
Drewitt for two years, and believed him to be quite incapable of the
crime laid to his charge.

After which evidence, the solicitor for the defence intimated that he
did not purpose calling any other witness. It was, as he stated in the
commencement, a clear case of mistaken identity. From the first his
client had said he never travelled by the Belfast Junction, or Dublin
and Drogheda Railway, in his life, and an alibi had now been
unmistakably proved.

After a good deal of hesitation, his worship fell into this view, and
having remarked that Mr. Gyton’s evidence was irreconcilable with the
assumption of the prisoner’s guilt, and that it appeared to be a most
singular case of mistaken identity, he said that the prisoner must be
discharged.

There was, however, something in the magistrate’s manner which seemed to
imply “not guilty, but don’t do it again;” for he assured Harold he had
had a very narrow escape, advised him for the future to lead a more
regular life, and began a lecture which the prisoner cut short by
saying—

“I did not steal those notes, I know nothing about them, who stole them,
or whether they ever were stolen. I never was at Drogheda in my life,
nor at Dundalk either. I never, to my recollection, entered Mr. Brinny’s
shop before the time I went in there for those cigars, and if ever I
chance to meet the person who appears to be so like me, I will take the
value out of his bones for what he has made me suffer during the past
month.”

This indiscretion on the part of Harold was a perfect godsend to the
magistrate, who told him such language was highly improper, remarked
that he did not seem duly grateful for his escape, and finally dismissed
him with a caution.

Then the next case was called on, and Harold, to quote the newspaper
reports, left the court with his friends.

There is, perhaps, no way in which a person can be acquitted which is so
eminently unpleasant as for an acquaintance to come forward and swear
that on such a day, and at such an hour, the accused was with him in
such a place.

Of course it is better to get off in this way rather than not at all;
but an alibi always sounds too like a pretext to be perfectly
satisfactory to the public; while the released prisoner (if he be
innocent) invariably leaves the court with the comfortable idea either
that a score of people have been in league against him, or else that
there is some blackguard walking about the world wearing his face,
assuming his gait, speaking with his voice, who may be committing fifty
crimes that will, in due time, be fathered on him.

Harold Drewitt was not, however, old enough to comprehend all that a man
would have felt, had a man stood in the boy’s shoes. He knew he had got
off, and it was impossible that he should understand he had escaped
being committed for trial by the merest shave of the wheel of accident.
Had he been with any of his ordinary companions during the afternoon in
question, there can be no doubt but that the magistrate would have
pooh-poohed the evidence in the prisoner’s favour, while probably the
witnesses for the defence might have got so worried and tormented as to
narrowly escape being committed for perjury.

Mr. Gyton and Mr. Pryor were not, however, people whose testimony could
be treated with disrespect. They might be mistaken. In his heart the
magistrate thought they were; but still he had no choice but to act on
their evidence and allow Harold to leave the court with his friends;
while many people remarked—that the young fellow had been born under a
lucky planet, and that if he had been a poor boy he would have been
transported.

“He would, as sure as God made little apples,” observed one man,
indignantly elbowing his way out of the crowd; “for it’s meself knows
the dacent chap that was hung on half the ividence they brought against
this one. If he didn’t stole that money I’d be very glad to know who
did.”

That was the general feeling throughout Dublin; but Harold knew nothing
about the general feeling, and only remembering that he was free once
more, returned home on a car laughing and amusing himself as usual.

He mimicked the magistrate; he mimicked the witnesses; he mimicked the
policeman, and went rollicking about the house in the wildest spirits
imaginable.

He was as thin as a whipping-post and as pale as a corpse; but he
insisted that he was not ill, that his mother should make herself happy
about him.

“I am not ill,” he said; “not a bit ill; all I want is something to eat
and to get out into the fresh air. We shall have many a walk before the
fine weather is quite gone. There is a fortnight still left of
September. Am I to go back to the office or not?” he added, looking
towards Brian, who said—

“Your place has been filled up.”

“Has it? A good job too. There was not one of them came to speak a word
for me, and none of the fellows sent me a line or a message, though they
must have known I did not do it—mustn’t they, mother?”

“Yes, Harold.”

“We will go down for a day to Bray, and take a car on to the Dargle,
shall we, mother? I should like that. I’d like to see the purple bloom
of the heather if it’s not quite gone off, and I love the sea down
there.”

“May we go with you?” asked Mr. Pryor, and Mr. Gyton at once followed
suit, and voted that it would be nice for them to make up several little
parties and go wherever Harold chose. There was an indescribable
tenderness in the manner of all towards the boy who had been, as they
knew, so hardly done by, and who presently insisted on going as far as
Sackville Street, “Just to be sure I am free,” he laughed.

As Brian had business to attend to, and Mr. Gyton appeared a little loth
to leave Geraldine, Mr. Pryor offered to accompany Harold; and the pair
accordingly sallied forth and walked into Dublin, where the youth at
every turn met some one he knew, some one he paused involuntarily to
speak with.

But no one voluntarily spoke to him: a cold bow; a nod; a how-d’ye-do; a
reluctant shake hands. Harold could not understand it, and at last
appealed to Mr. Pryor.

“Am I not innocent? It looks almost as though they thought me guilty,
does not it?” and he drew closer to his companion, who told him—

“Not to trouble himself about it; that they would learn better in time.”

“I think,” remarked Harold, after another old acquaintance had passed
without taking the slightest notice of his salutation, “I think it is
nearly time for us to go home;” and, accordingly, they turned and went
back over Carlisle Bridge, and along Westmoreland Street, and past
College Green, and down Nassau Street, and so on to the suburb where
Mrs. Drewitt resided. As they walked along Merrion Square a
disreputable-looking fellow came up and, accosting Harold, shook him so
heartily by the hand as to threaten dislocation of his wrist.

“Glad to see you out,” he said; “thought at one time you were in Queer
Street for life: when will you be round our way?”

“I don’t know,” answered Harold, nervously feeling Mr. Pryor touch his
arm warningly. “I am not very well—that is, I have friends—that is, I am
going out of town.”

“Well, it’s about the best thing you can do till the talk has blown
over. It is a wicked world, and living’s hard work. Good-bye.”

“Who is that man, Harold?” Mr. Pryor asked a little sternly, and his
tone at once put Harold’s bristles up.

“He was a very respectable person—very indeed; but had been unfortunate
in business. His name was Grinnell, and he lived some place up near
Mountjoy Square.”

“Does he really? I should have thought some of those nice respectable
streets in the neighbourhood of St. Patrick’s would have been much more
in his way.”

Of which speech Harold refused to take any notice.

“I hope,” continued Mr. Pryor, “that you do not intend keeping up your
acquaintance with him; you are but just starting in life; you have been
extricated with considerable difficulty from a most awkward position,
And it behoves you, therefore, to be doubly careful for the future in
choosing your associates.”

Still Harold made no answer.

“I wish you would promise not to have anything more to do with people of
that description: you will ruin your own prospects, and break your
mother’s heart.”

“Hang it!” interrupted Harold, “one must speak to somebody, and he is
the only fellow who has said a civil word to me since I came out. You
must see for yourself that the ‘genteels’ have cut me; don’t you now?”

“If they have, do you think you will mend matters by taking up with all
the riff-raff who come and shake hands with you?”

“You need not call them names if they are not gentlemen,” suggested
Harold.

“I think I, or any one else who styled the person from whom we have just
parted an honest man would be calling him very much out of his name.”

“I suppose I am not good enough for you, then?” said Harold pausing.

“Nonsense, boy. I want to save you from the consequences of your own
want of firmness. You believe in everybody who says a kind word to you,
and the result is you have made intimates and associates of people with
whom you ought never to have exchanged a word. Do you not remember what
the police stated about the character of your companions—swindlers,
betting-men, drunkards, profligates, of every description? If your
mother had only heard the one-half we have heard about you, Harold, she
would be more anxious about you than she is.”

“I wonder you are not ashamed to be seen out walking with me,” said the
boy. “I am sure I am not fit company for a rich swell like you. Had not
you better take the other side of the street?” added Harold, who was by
this time worked up to a pitch of passionate fury. “We may meet somebody
else who will come between the wind and your nobility. Go on; I will
follow, if you like; it cannot be pleasant for you to feel that you are
out walking with—with—a blackguard and a thief!”

“Don’t be an idiot, Harold;” and Mr. Pryor laid a hand on the boy’s
shoulder, and led him, or, rather, pushed him, on towards home.

“I’m not an idiot,” said Harold. “I know you all think I am, and that I
do not understand anything—that I am a fool; but I am as wise as Brian,
though I may not be such an old-fashioned sneak; and I will speak to
whom I like, and go with whom I like, and visit whom I like, without
consulting anybody.”

“You are resolved to be independent, at any rate,” observed Mr. Pryor.

“Yes, I am. I mean to stick by the friends who stick by me——”

“Would you think me impertinent if I ventured to remind you, I and my
nephew have tried to be your friends?” said the barrister.

“I know you have; I know why, too; because Maxwell told me something
long ago. It is not because you like me, it isn’t. Nobody likes me; and
now you are going to set my mother against me, and tell her every little
thing I have ever done.”

Having completed which dreadful picture, Harold stopped again in the
very middle of the pavement, and seemed disposed to have a good cry on
the spot.

There was a car passing at the moment, which Mr. Pryor hailed. “You are
tired, Harold,” he said; “you have tried your strength too much. Jump
up, and let us drive home.”

When they reached home, Harold went off to bed; but whether because he
was sick, or whether he was sulky, Mr. Pryor could not decide.

Next day, however, he saw clearly that the young gentleman’s temper was
greatly damaged; for he insisted, against the advice of every one of his
family, on going to his late employers, and asking why they had filled
up his berth.

“I wish you would not do anything so foolish,” Brian remarked.

“I am not a child,” was the reply; “and I mean to see to my own business
for myself. I won’t be kept in the dark by anybody.”

And accordingly he went.

It was the best thing that could have happened to a youth of Harold’s
peculiar disposition, to be able to hear for himself what the world said
of him.

He had been so pampered and spoiled at home, that he was never likely to
learn the truth there. Strangers, however, did not stand on so much
ceremony, and when Harold grew impudent, gave him quite as good as he
brought.

“I know you have got off,” said one of his late employers; “and I know
how you have got off, also—by an alibi; for which I would not give
that!” and he threw a scrap of waste paper on the floor. “Of course, I
am sure Mr. Gyton would not have said what he did unless he fully
believed it to be the truth; but he was mistaken, that is all; and a
very lucky mistake it turned out for you. I really wonder at your
assurance in coming here at all. As for taking you back, I am only too
glad to be rid of you; there is not another house in Dublin who would
have kept so idle, useless, and disreputable a young vagabond for a
week.”

Harold walked out of the inner office, and as he passed through the
outer looked defiantly at the clerks he had once been intimate with: one
or two nodded; but none came forward to shake hands—to say they were
pleased at his escape.

He felt tired, but he had no heart to go home; he had taken his own way,
and his own way not having proved a pleasant one, he did not like to go
back and confess to such a disappointment: accordingly he went on—on
along the North Wall, where his respectable friend of the preceding
evening dropped upon him, and proposed that they should go out for a
drive together: “Round by the strawberry-beds, and on as far as Lucan.”

Harold jumped at the idea. “And we can have some dinner together, and
then spend the evening at Birdley’s; there are to be a lot of fellows
there. What do you say? will ‘the old folks at home,’ be singing all the
evening, ‘Willie, we have missed you?’”

“No,” answered Harold; “I’m your man.” And while Mrs. Drewitt was
marvelling what in the world could have become of her boy, he was
rattling through the Dublin streets, talking and laughing and joking, as
though he had never been in any trouble, as though he had never been a
prisoner in his life.

Before evening came he was pretty well exhausted, but still his
companion insisted on taking him to Birdley’s, where he was greeted by
the assembled guests with great enthusiasm; his health being drunk with
all the honours.

“Now tell us how you did it?” exclaimed one. “How many of the flimsies
did you manage to get off?” asked another.

“I suppose you have made a bonfire of the rest: it would be no go trying
to pass them now,” said a third.

“You must have made a tidy haul of it though, altogether,” observed one
unwholesome-looking young man.

“And how did you contrive to hoodwink those respectable friends of
yours, for I suppose they would have been above sharing the swag.”

“Tell us all about it?” repeated the first speaker. “Silence now,
gentlemen, if you please. Mr. Harold Drewitt rises to address the
meeting.”

“I want to know something,” cried Harold, who was indeed on his legs.
“Do you really think I stole those notes?”

“Draw it mild,” suggested the young man above referred to; who looked
very much as though he had been buried and dug up again. “Stole is such
a confoundedly ugly way of putting it.”

“Do you believe then I ever was at Drogheda? Do you think I ever saw
that money? Do you imagine that all I said was lies?”

“Well, very few people looked upon it as the truth,” was the reply.

“You consider me a thief, then, in plain words?”

“Oh! no, nothing of the kind; you merely annexed the contents of that
carpet-bag——”

“I did not—I did not,” Harold vehemently interrupted. “I know no more
who took that money than you do.” (Here the auditors winked at one
another.) “If I never should speak another word, I am as innocent in
this matter as any man among you.”

“Well, then, who the devil is guilty?” asked the chairman; “for no
person in his senses can suppose all those witnesses were in collusion
against you. It must have been you or your twin brother, if you have
one.”

“If you look closely at his face, it must strike you as too old, too
second-hand, if I may use the expression, to have served only one body,”
said Mr. Grinnell. “Rely upon it there’s another trunk somewhere that
borrows that face and goes out with it at nights while this good child
is sleeping it its bed.”

“Gentlemen,” began Harold.

“We are all attention.”

“I wish you would believe me.”

“Lord help the boy, we are so simple we could be made believe anything,”
said his neighbour.

“I never saw that money. I never changed one of those notes. Everybody
is against me, though I am innocent—innocent;” and Harold’s voice began
to shake a little and his lips to tremble.

“Really this is becoming affecting,” remarked the corpse-like young
gentleman, addressing Mr. Grinnell.

“Allow me to perform a parent’s part,” said the chairman, offering
Harold a handkerchief.

But Harold’s temper was now up: he pitched the handkerchief back at the
speaker; he threw off his coat, and declaring he would fight the matter
out, dared any one to say he was a liar, and requested the company
generally to “come on!”

“Now, now,” said Mr. Grinnell; “now, now,” and he walked round the
table, and picked up Harold’s coat, and begged him to put it on, and to
sit down and take things peaceably.

“How can I take things peaceably when you are all against me—all?” asked
Harold, looking first at one of his tormentors and then at another. “Are
you cowards, that none of you will come on, when I ask you? Will you not
even let me fight for my good name? will you not——”

“Catch him, some of you,” cried the chairman, and involuntarily every
man sprang to his feet, and stretched out his arms towards the boy, who,
stopping suddenly in his sentence, reeled back a few paces, and then
fell into the chair he had pushed away from him.

“Send for a doctor,” said one.

“Give him some punch.”

“He’s had too much already.”

“No, he hasn’t,” said Grinnell, “he has scarcely tasted a drop.”

“Get him some water.”

“Stand back, stand back,” entreated Grinnell, “and open the windows,
some of you; it is not a common faint though, he’s quite gone,” and he
wiped the perspiration from Harold’s face, and put the hair back off his
forehead, and took the clammy hands in his and chafed them gently.

“I will take him home,” he said, when Harold recovered a little. “Tell
the waiter to call a car. Birdley will lend me something warm to wrap
round him. Are you able to walk now, do you think?” he continued,
addressing Harold, “or shall I carry you? That is right; take hold of my
arm; don’t be afraid of leaning. Now come with me.”

“Good-night, gentlemen,” Harold said, turning round and addressing the
group that were looking after him. “Likely I shall never see any of you
again; but, whether you believe it or not, I am innocent of what you
think.”

“He looks to me,” remarked the chairman after he had left the room, “as
though he were going precious fast to that place where it’s best known
what he is. It is a pity, too, for he is a plucky lad;” and there ensued
a great silence, during which every man present was employed wondering
whether it were indeed within the bounds of possibility that Harold had
been mistaken for some one else, who might be thinking at that very
moment how deucedly lucky it was for him that he had escaped scot free.

 CHAPTER XI.
 Called Home.


The September wind blew cold and keen as Mr. Grinnell and Harold drove
along the quays to Carlisle Bridge, and the man that had so strongly
excited Mr. Pryor’s disapproval drew the borrowed rug closer round the
boy, who shivered and trembled as the blast swept by.

It was better when they entered the south side; there they were
sheltered from the cutting wind, and Harold grew quieter and calmer as
the car bore him nearer and nearer home. He asked Mr. Grinnell not to
drive up to the door, but to set him down at the end of the street where
his mother resided; and then they had a quarrel as to which should pay
the man, that resulted in Harold, as usual, having it all his own way.

On one point, however, Mr. Grinnell was firm—he would not let the sick
lad walk up the street alone, neither would he allow him to throw aside
Birdley’s rug. “I will take it back with me,” he said. “I am resolved to
see you safe and sound to your own door.”

Which he did, and then bidding Harold a cheery “good-night,” left Harold
as Brian admitted him.

“Who was that came with you?” the elder brother asked.

“A friend of mine, a man who brought me home. I have been ill, Brian—I
have indeed,” and Harold dropped into a chair and put out his white thin
hand deprecatingly towards his brother.

“I am going to be good,” he said. “I am. If you all will only do what I
ask you, I will never get drunk again. I will try to be a credit to
everybody belonging to me yet. It is not too late, I think, is it?” and
with a gesture which was unspeakably touching, he put Brian’s hand on
the table, and laid his cheek upon it.

Brian had persuaded his mother and Geraldine to go to bed. The servant,
however, was still up, and now stood in the doorway. She came to ask if
anything more were needed; but seeing Harold’s head bent down and his
brother leaning over him, she remained silent.

Brian saw her and whispered the one word “Doctor.”

In a moment, Mary was running down the street, and late though the hour
was, returned in five minutes with a surgeon.

“My brother is not very well,” said Brian, after the first greetings
were over. “Can you suggest any little thing that will set him to
rights?”

“I would rather not see a doctor. I do not want any medicine,” remarked
Harold, lifting his head, and looking with his great dark eyes straight
in the face of the surgeon who was feeling his pulse, and counting its
beats with the greatest apparent indifference. “I am quite well,” he
went on, pettishly, trying to wrench away his wrist; and then he dropped
his face again on Brian’s hand, and his brother knew it was to hide the
hot tears, that were falling thick and fast as rain.

“Hysterical,” said the doctor, calmly; and Harold gave Brian a push, as
a hint that he should contradict the medical statement.

“His strength has been greatly tried lately, as most probably you know,”
answered the elder brother; and his low, full voice had a great virtue
of quietness in it. “He has suffered much, both mentally and bodily; but
we hope rest and change may do wonders for him. Don’t we, Harold?”

The boy made no reply, but he pressed his lips on Brian’s hand.

“Is this the young gentleman, then, who was charged with a crime that
some one else committed?” asked the surgeon; and for those words Harold
turned round and thanked him.

“Nobody would believe me, sir,” he said. “Nobody, that is, out of my own
home. But I did not do it. I did not, indeed.”

“That I am sure you did not!” answered the surgeon, heartily; and
immediately he and Harold became good friends; and the boy, pushing back
the mass of black hair that had fallen forward over his face, answered
all the doctor’s questions, and told all his symptoms, so far as he knew
them himself.

“It is the nights I dread,” he finished. “If you could only give me
something to prevent my being so frightened. It is horrible to be all
alone in the dark, like that;” and he clutched Brian’s hand, and held it
fast while he spoke.

“Why did you never tell any one of these attacks before?” asked Brian.

“I told Mr. Pryor. The first of them I ever had at night was the time I
was staying at Durrow; and he tipped me a five-pound note, and made me
promise him to go to some of the great guns here, and ask them about it;
and I meant to go, only, you know, they took me up the very day I came
back—they did.”

“Poor lad!” said the surgeon, compassionately.

“I was a poor lad, I think,” answered Harold. “There’s nobody ever can
know what it was—to be like what I have been to-night, in that horrid
place. Another week of it would have killed me; I am sure it would.”

“Well, we must try to set you up again now,” observed the surgeon,
cheerfully. “If your servant can come with me,” he added, addressing
Brian, “I will send back some medicine with her, and see your brother
early to-morrow morning;” and he stretched out his hand, which the two
youths shook gratefully and departed.

“You won’t leave me to-night, Brian, will you?” pleaded Harold; and
Brian was just saying that he would not, when Mrs. Drewitt, who had got
up and dressed herself at the sound of a strange voice, came
down-stairs, asking what was the matter.

“I did not feel up to the mark,” Harold answered; “and that obstinate
Brian must needs go disturb the whole neighbourhood by fetching a doctor
to see me. What a shame it is to have roused you up! but now that you
are here, I want to ask you a favour. I want you and Brian to do
something for me.”

“What is it, dear?” She was sitting on a chair, drawn close up to his;
her arms were about him, and his head was resting on her breast.

“I want to go away. I want you to leave Dublin, and take me where I
shall not be known—where I shall not be called a thief and a liar at
every turn.”

“Who dares call you either the one or the other?” and Brian’s hand
clenched, and his mouth was drawn tight as he asked the question.

Harold laughed out.

 “Who killed Kildare? who dared Kildare to kill?—
 I killed Kildare, and dare kill whom I will,”

he repeated. “He would go out and fight for me, I do believe,” he said,
turning to his mother; “but he would have to fight all Dublin if he took
up that quarrel. I have found out to-day,” Harold went on, as he kept
curling and uncurling the broad strings of the widow’s cap that fitted
so closely to Mrs. Drewitt’s face, “that there is no place like home,
after all, and that there are no people like our own people, when they
are true to us.”

“Come up to bed, Harold,” interposed Brian, who dreaded another fit of
weeping. “Come along;” and he lifted his brother off his chair.

“Wait a moment,” entreated Harold, standing with his arm round Brian’s
neck, and Brian’s arm supporting him. “Wait a moment; will you do what I
say? Will you leave Dublin?”

Mrs. Drewitt’s glance wandered for a moment round the room. It was not
large; it was not well furnished; but it had been a shelter, and it had
seemed like home. For the instant it appeared hard to have to turn out
once more into the world; to be in middle age a wanderer on the face of
the earth; but the feeling only lasted for a moment; then she said—

“I will do whatever may be best for my children, and the child that has
most need of me I must consider first.”

The last few words were addressed to Brian, who answered—

“Harold has most need of you, mother. Do what he asks. Leaving Ireland
may be the making of him yet.”

“Do you wish to leave Ireland, Harold?” Mrs. Drewitt inquired, and the
boy nodded.

“I should like to go to London,” he said; “I want to be lost; I want to
be able to be a good boy. I am tired and sick of being bad and foolish.”

“Then God forbid we should keep you in the way of temptation!” exclaimed
Brian: “but now come to bed; if you are to travel to London you must
make haste and get strong.”

“Oh! I shall be all right enough,” answered the lad as he crawled up the
staircase, with Brian’s arm still encircling him, with his right hand
still twined round Brian’s neck.

And thus it came to pass that, instead of going to the Dargle, or to the
Seven Churches, or to the meeting of the waters, or any of the lovely
places to which Mr. Gyton had planned little excursions, the Drewitts
set about packing up their effects forthwith and removing to London.

“You can do as you please about it, of course,” said the surgeon to
Brian; “but——”

“It is his fancy,” the elder brother answered; “and I would not baulk it
for any consideration. And do not tell my mother, Mr. Ross; she will
find it out for herself in time. I know what you mean. I have been
afraid of it from the first.”

And there was another person who knew also; upon whom the feeling that
he was dying came slowly but surely; who day by day clung to his mother
with a more despairing attachment, with a more passionate love.

After they removed to London he and she were never separate. In the dull
winter days he sat at the windows of their lodgings, looking out on the
constantly-changing panorama that was passing below. For the sake of her
boy, Mrs. Drewitt had rented apartments in a house that commanded the
view of a crowded thoroughfare; and it was Harold’s delight to sit from
noon to dusk, and from dusk to nightfall, watching the people passing
and repassing, the endless conveyances, the lights, the bustle, the
noise, the excitement.

Hand clasped in hand, mother and son spent the days thus together. It
was a fearful winter, that of 1854–5, but Harold lived through it. The
spring of 1855 was late, and the season ungenial; but Harold lived to
smell the violets which Geraldine bought for him, and to see the
primrose-roots hawked in baskets about the streets.

It was a lovely summer, and Harold lived to take many a drive and many a
walk through the great Babylon. He loved the parks—he liked to go where
there were hundreds and hundreds of people, and not a face he knew among
them save one, his mother’s; while she—it was impossible for her not to
know that to Harold’s life there could be but one sequel—an early death:
and when at last, in the September following their arrival in London,
the heart that had once throbbed so high with youth and hope and courage
was cold and quiet, Mrs. Drewitt, alone with her dead, fell on her knees
and thanked God that her boy—her frail, erring, unfortunate boy—belonged
at last solely to Him and her.

She made no mourning for her child, she raised no cry of grievous
lamentation, of unutterable woe, but she lay through the night thinking
of how he had come home at last—home to the one sure shelter to die in
peace. The touch of his hand seemed never to depart from her; she could
not forget how day after day he had pillowed his head on her bosom; she
could not shut her ears to the “mother—mother” that he kept repeating
towards the last if she left his side for a moment.

“God has been very good to me, Brian,” she said. “He gave my boy back to
me before he died, and I am content.”

With some difficulty Brian at last found a resting-place for Harold: not
in one of the great cemeteries, but in a quiet churchyard in one of the
northern suburbs, where the evening sun falls aslant over the graves,
where great trees throw a pleasant shade across the grass, where the
grey church-tower flings its shadow over the tombs, where the bells ring
out Sunday after Sunday, where there is quiet without loneliness, rest
without desertion.

There Brian laid his brother down to sleep, himself the solitary
mourner.

Mr. Pryor and Mr. Gyton were both out of town; and in the vast city
Brian Drewitt had no other friend.

As he left the grave, his thoughts went back to another funeral, at
which he had been chief mourner likewise: where, under the shadow of the
mountains, within sound of the sea, he had seen the mortal remains of
his father placed in the vault of the Drewitts.

He would have felt better satisfied had Harold slept there likewise. He
would have liked a train of mourners, and a procession such as had wound
down the avenue at Kincorth, and under the trees, and round the bay, and
over Duranmore Hill, and down into the hollow.

Each man has his special weakness, and this was Brian’s. He had never
felt so fully that they were Drewitts of Kincorth no longer, as when he
followed his brother to the grave alone.

What did the words graven on his headstone, “Harold Drewitt,” imply to
the men and the women who passed through that churchyard Sunday after
Sunday? Nothing. They only served to show that there a stranger had laid
him down to rest.

What guess could they make about how the nineteen years of his young
life had been spent? What could they know of his story—his errors—his
repentance—his wrongs?

But as though it had been all contained in a single page, Brian read the
tale which was now finished over again—over and over again.

He could see the boy riding round and round the meadows; he could see
the proud glances which both father and mother directed towards their
youngest born; he could see the daring imp taking his leaps, bearing his
tosses, laughing advice to scorn, and mocking at all grave counsels. He
could see him walking about with Maxwell; looking defiantly across the
dinner-table, at the Headlands, at his more prudent brother, and then
draining off his bumper out of mere bravado. He thought of him that
night at Regan’s: he saw him disappear through the darkness on Madcap;
he remembered his life in Dublin, and the great trial which had, so far
as any human being could see, saved his soul, while it destroyed his
life; and then—then there came the days when Harold was tractable as a
child—when he clung to his mother—when he turned back home to die.

The short story was finished—the tale told—the book closed in this world
for ever.

It was with the life of Harold Drewitt “as it is with a ship that
passeth over the waves of the sea, which, when it is gone by, the trace
thereof cannot be found, neither the pathway of the keel in the waves;
or, as when a bird hath flown through the air, there is no token of the
way to be found; or, as when an arrow is shot at a mark, it parteth the
air, which immediately cometh together again.” He had “passed away like
a shadow, or as a post that hasteth by.”

Maxwell Drewitt had been right. It was a young body that Brian left,
with an exceeding bitter and sorrowful heart, to sleep in the grave
until the Judgment-day.

 CHAPTER XII.
 Geraldine is Satisfied.


In the lives of all men there come long pauses, when the river of
existence seems to flow on without a ripple, when day succeeds to day
without any event of sufficient importance to be set down and chronicled
occurring in the even course of their experience.

Such a time was the year following Harold’s death to Brian Drewitt.
Though some changes had taken place, they had come about so naturally
that they scarcely seemed changes to him. All perhaps he noticed
specially or cared for particularly was, that he found it would take him
a long time to grow rich, and that during that time he was separated
from revenge and Maxwell and Kincorth.

Had he forgotten Kincorth, do you imagine? Had trouble driven all
thoughts of vengeance out of his head? Had struggling with the world not
tamed him, experience not subdued him, time not softened him? No, my
reader. The object he had planned to work for in Connemara he was
working for still, and though the end seemed no nearer of attainment,
but further off than ever, yet Brian Drewitt never withdrew his eyes
from it, but kept that distant goal in view by day and night, in
solitude and when he was mixing among his fellows.

Long ago, I said a person had but to look in Brian Drewitt’s face to
know that he could wait through years. He was waiting through years now
for the end.

He had been very fortunate in business; fortunate, that is, considering
his training and the age at which he made his start in the world; but
still Brian felt dissatisfied.

It is not easy to be content with pounds when you have been calculating
thousands; it is difficult to work for small pay when you desire to make
money _ad libitum_ for the purpose of wiping out old scores; and thus,
though Brian was in the receipt of a hundred and fifty pounds a year, he
worked like a galley-slave to make himself of more value to his
employers, who were now not the Dublin firm in whose establishment he
had made his first start in life, but the great London house of Gyton,
Lark, Munday, Hatfield and Company, Austinfriars.

After his brother’s death he felt that he would like to be nearer his
mother than could possibly be the case if he retained his situation in
Dublin.

As for asking Mrs. Drewitt and Geraldine to return there, to return to a
country which was now so full of painful associations, he felt that such
a request would be both foolish and unreasonable, and when Mr. Frank
Gyton proposed to him that he should take his chance of the “governor’s
temper,” at a salary of one hundred and fifty, which was an advance of
fifty on the pay of the Dublin firm, Brian close with the offer on the
spot, and three months afterwards walked into the city office as
corresponding and foreign clerk.

That Mr. Gyton had a temper; that Mr. Munday had a worse; that the
managing clerk had a worse temper than all his principals’ tempers put
together, Brian was not long of finding out; but he had sense enough to
find out at the same time that if he did his work no one could find
fault with him; that if he kept his time, held his tongue, did what he
was told and refrained from idling, nobody would meddle with him.

Had he been gay and light-hearted, like other lads; had he been given to
merriment, or to merry-making; had he been sociable and lively, like
Harold, with a jeer or a joke for every one, probably he would never
have made way in the good graces of Mr. Gyton; but as matters stood that
gentleman took to him in due time amazingly.

Mr. Gyton, in the City, was a different man from Mr. Gyton at the
West-end. At the west he did not seem to have much voice in any matter
on which his son and wife chanced to entertain a decided opinion; but in
the City he was an individual of many prejudices, with old-fashioned
ideas; who gave himself all the airs of a millionaire; who was a
martinet in Austinfriars, and who liked to see old heads set on young
shoulders.

In this respect Brian suited him to a nicety. Brian might have been a
thousand years of age.

“A most discreet young man,” he remarked to Mr. Munday; and Mr. Munday
agreed that he was, but ventured to observe that it was a pity he
chanced to be born on the wrong side of the channel.

“As for that, sir,” answered Mr. Gyton, drawing himself up, and taking a
pinch of snuff out of his box, without offering any to Mr. Munday, “as
for that, sir, the Gytons have some Irish blood in their veins. My
grandfather married the niece of an Irish Earl, and my father-in-law was
Irish to the back-bone. Well trained, sir, and well bred, the Irish can
run as well as the best of us.”

“Then I suppose it is quite true what I hear about your son?” said Mr.
Munday, a little maliciously.

“What about my son, sir?—what—what about Frank?”

“It is generally said that Mr. Drewitt has a very charming sister——”

“And if he have a dozen sisters, Mr. Munday——”

“Why, in that case, your son could have choice enough.”

“Of course he could, and he can choose whom he pleases, and he can marry
whom he likes, without consulting every idiot in London on the subject,
I suppose.”

“Certainly, Mr. Gyton, his own father not excepted, we may conclude.”

And then the partners had quite a quarrel; they tried who could hit
hardest, who could say the sharpest things, and went on wrangling and
snarling, until Mr. Gyton, looking as red as a turkey-cock and as
pompous as a beadle, remarked, that he supposed his private affairs
might be regarded as separate from those of the firm; and that he wished
Mr. Munday would recollect for the future that he had expressed such a
wish; after which observation, which was delivered in the tone of a
royal command, Mr. Gyton summoned Brian into his private office, and
began catechizing him.

“How long have you been with us, Drewitt?”

“Six months, sir.”
