“And you like the business?”

“I like anything out of which money can be made,” was the reply.

“Anything honest, I suppose, you mean.”

“Anything honest and honourable,” agreed Brian. “Anything that a
gentleman may do, and still remain one.”

“Humph! You call yourself a gentleman, then, do you?”

Brian flushed scarlet, but had sense enough to make no reply.

“There—there—don’t be annoyed. I only asked for information. I wanted to
know more of you. An employer ought to understand clearly the position
in life of a person holding your responsible office. Let me see—how did
you happen to come here?—who recommended you?—with whom were you last?”

Brian told him.

“And wasn’t there something about your father cutting you out?—what was
all that?”

Once again, Brian gave him the information he wanted.

“And which of your family have you with you in London?” was the next
question.

“My mother and only sister,” answered the young man.

“Dependent upon you, of course?”

“No, sir; my mother has her settlement, which is quite enough for all
her wants,” Brian said, stiffly.

“Hah! so much the better. Now just begin and tell me your story right
through—don’t keep anything back—be frank with me, and I will be fair
with you. I am not asking out of idle curiosity—I am not, upon my
honour.”

Thus exhorted, Brian told all—told everything, even to Harold’s mishap;
and during the course of the recital, Mr. Gyton senior indulged himself
in many pinches of snuff—in many hems, and haws, and God bless me’s, and
upon my soul’s.

It was early summer, and Mr. Gyton was arrayed in those wonderfully
light trousers for which most City magnates seem to have some kind of
mania; he had on a white waistcoat, displayed an imposing amount of
shirt-collar, wore a lightblue silk cravat, and was shaved as clean as
it was in the art of man to shave a human chin.

He always carried an immense white silk pocket-handkerchief and an
equally enormous bandana.

The bandana was now spread out over his knee, like a flag laid over a
railing, and he contemplated the way in which his boots were blacked;
while with his plump legs well apart, and his stomach swelling out under
his white waistcoat, and his heavy guard-chain strolling about over his
chest, he sat and listened to Brian, interrupting him only with the
remarks I have mentioned.

“Hah! poor lady—very sad—very sad indeed: has Mrs. Gyton been to see her
yet—eh?”

Mr. Gyton was always fancying his wife was in league with his son, and
he put the question fully expecting to hear that Mrs. Gyton and Mrs.
Drewitt were bosom friends.

No such thing however.

“My brother took that affair I mentioned so much to heart,” answered
Brian, “and his health was so bad from the time we left Ireland, that he
could not bear the sight of a stranger at all, and since his death my
mother has never recovered her spirits sufficiently to care for
visiting.”

“Wonderful—bless me—extraordinary!”

“We are so differently situated now,” began Brian, who really did not
know what else to say; but Mr. Gyton interrupted him with the remark
that he should have thought—but of course everybody knew their own
business best (the millionaire was apt at times to be ungrammatical)—a
little friendly visiting would have done Mrs. Drewitt good. “It’s not
well to sit moping,” went on the great man; “brooding over sorrow only
makes it worse; and we none of us can live alone—at least, that is my
experience. I am sure when I was so ill, this time two years—let me see,
is it two years? yes, it must be pretty nearly two years—the doctors
told me I must speak to no one, see no one, and think of nothing, for at
least three months.”

“They considered your case so serious?” suggested Brian, imagining some
observation was expected from him.

“Serious? yes, indeed, it was serious; but their prescription would have
killed me. I might as well have been put in my coffin at once, and so I
told them. I went abroad, certainly, but what did I do there? I amused
myself as much as possible. I saw everything there was to be seen. I
drove about, I ate foreign kickshaws, I talked French (Brian thought he
would have given something to hear the French to which Dives had treated
the Parisians), and what was the consequence?”

His clerk faintly hoped that he had come back better.

“Better! why you see me now. I have been better since than I ever was in
my life; and that’s what your mother ought to have, change and variety,
instead of mouching in the house all day. Mrs. Gyton shall call upon
her; yes, that she shall;” and Mr. Gyton having uttered the last words
as if in answer to an internal protest against any such erratic
proceedings, took a pinch of snuff, blew his nose violently and looked
straight at Brian, who said—

“I hope, sir, you will not think me rude and ungrateful, but——”

“But what—what is it you have got to say?”

“I think my mother would rather—that is, we are so completely removed
from the circle we——”

“Bless my soul! what ails the boy? he cannot finish a single sentence!”
exclaimed Mr. Gyton, pettishly. “That is a shocking habit for any one to
get into. Out with it!” he finished, working himself up to a pitch of
desperation; “out with whatever you have got to say.”

“I am quite certain,” said Brian, steadily—so steadily that Mr. Gyton
never guessed what the effort cost him—“that you would rather our house
and your house were kept completely separate, when I tell you Mr.
Francis Gyton once thought my sister very pretty; as she is,” added
Brian. “All things considered, my mother decided it was best he should
not see so much of her, nor she of him. I hope I am not doing wrong in
telling you this, sir. We know exactly where we stand, and we do not
wish to push ourselves into any house under false pretences;” and Mr.
Brian drew himself up to his full height, and looked the very
incarnation of pride and determination.

“Do you mean to say my son was ever fond of your sister?” asked Mr.
Gyton, facing slowly round.

“I believe he was. I do not think my mother would ever have said it was
best for him not to see Geraldine, had there not been something of the
kind—and something definite, too.”

“The young dog!” remarked the parent.

“I should be sorry to say anything calculated to annoy you, or to
prejudice you against Mr. Francis. But the matter is all settled now;
and you will understand, from what I have said, why I think my mother
would rather Mrs. Gyton did not call.”

“Thank you. It is always best to speak out fairly and frankly, is not
it? Now there is something else I want to talk to you about. You have
done your best, I think, since you came to us?”

“I have tried to do my best,” answered Brian, who was in horror lest he
was going to be dismissed.

“Yes; and those who try generally succeed, don’t they?” said Mrs. Gyton,
whose face seemed to Brian to get redder every moment, while his
shirt-collar became more imposing. “I have been thinking of sending you
abroad, very shortly.”

“Do you mean to any of the colonies?” asked Brian, hurriedly.

“No, no—only to France; it is rather a delicate piece of business, and I
want it managed cleverly. If you conducted it to our satisfaction, I
have no doubt we should be able to raise your salary next year.”

“You are very kind to me, sir,” Brian said.

“Not at all, not at all. I know what I am about. Take care of what you
do, and you may make your way in the world yet. Now you can go; that is
all I wanted.”

But still Brian hesitated.

“I can have no objection to your repeating to Mr. Francis what has
passed between us to-day; but at the same time, sir, I almost wish you
would not mention the matter to him. It is all settled now; and——”

“Yes—yes—yes—yes,” exclaimed Mr. Gyton, impatiently waving his hand; “I
understand perfectly; be satisfied, I shall not make any mischief;” and
Mr. Gyton rose from his chair as a hint that the interview must be
considered ended, while Brian went back to his desk wondering greatly
whither all his principal’s questions had tended.

As for Mr. Gyton, he took an early opportunity of seeing Mr. Pryor and
asking him what kind of girl that sister of Drewitt’s might be; which
question Mr. Pryor answered by inquiring why his brother-in-law wanted
to know.

“Curiosity,” was the reply.

“Nonsense,” exclaimed Mr. Pryor; “you have heard some of that chatter of
Munday’s about Frank being fond of her, as Frank was, for that matter;
but do not let it influence you against Brian; for though Munday does
not like him, you will never get a more faithful nor trustworthy clerk.”

“But I want to hear about this girl. Have you seen her?”

“Have I seen her?” repeated Mr. Pryor, with a nervous laugh. “Have I
seen you? Did not I see them every day in Connemara before they ever
left Kincorth? Did not I tell you I was in the house when her father
died, and that I heard the will read over too? Seen her! I should think
I had.”

“Do you think Frank really was fond of her?”

“How is it possible for me to answer such a question? He was at one time
never out of the house, and he proposed for her. There, now the cat is
out of the bag. But he had not courage to ask your consent, and Mrs.
Drewitt would not allow the affair to go on without your knowledge.
Frank asked me about it, and said he feared there was no chance of your
approval; in which opinion, knowing there was no money nor prospect of
any money, I agreed with him.” Mr. Pryor was never weary of having a
fling at his brother-in-law’s money-bags.

“And so Frank gave her up?”

“I don’t think he did; I think he is waiting for an opportunity to speak
to you.”

Thereupon Mr. Gyton grew rampant: was he a bear, was he a brute, was he
a beast, that his own son should be afraid to speak to him on such a
subject? “Hang it! sir,” he said, “was I rolling in money when I married
your sister?”

“No,” was the reply; “but then neither were you dependent on your father
for your support; and Frank knows, as I know, that there are probably
many young ladies you would prefer to a girl without fortune, and she an
Irishwoman.”

“Now I’ll tell you what it is, Geoffry,” and at this point Mr. Gyton
became almost purple in the face with indignation, “I won’t have you or
anybody settling my affairs for me, and saying what I should like, or
what I should not. If you must know what I should not like, it would be
one of those Munday girls. I wonder,” he went on, “that you, having just
come into such a handsome property, should not have offered to make
things square for Frank, and he your only near male relative. Not that I
want the money; only I wonder, when these Drewitts seem to be such
_protégés_ of yours.”

“You need not wonder,” was the reply. “I may marry yet, myself.”

“The deuce you may!” and Mr. Gyton surveyed him with perplexed
amazement. “Ho, ho! I am beginning to see daylight, I think. Perhaps you
are in love with the girl yourself?”

“Perhaps I am;” and Geoffry Pryor, seeing that his last chance of
winning Geraldine was slipping through his fingers, stood at bay.
“Indeed,” he proceeded, “there is no use in my making any pretence about
the matter. I did hope—I have hoped year after year since I first knew
her, that some day I might be able to persuade her to become my wife. It
may seem to you a ridiculous rivalry,” added Mr. Pryor, hastily; “but I
think I should have been far the honester lover of the two. Had I
approved of Frank’s conduct in the affair—had I thought it likely he
would make her happy—I love her so well, that—”

“You would have taken his part against me. I am obliged to you,
Geoffry.”

“—That I would have done what I could to help him and her, though I do
not pretend to much Quixotism either,” finished Mr. Pryor.

“I must see this young lady, who seems to have turned all your heads,”
remarked Mr. Gyton.

“Well, then, listen to one word first. Frank has treated her very
badly.”

“How dare you say such a thing of my son, sir?”

“I shall say what I please about my nephew,” retorted Mr. Pryor, coolly.
“And I repeat, Frank has treated her very badly. Now, do not you go and
treat her badly also. Make up your mind what your intentions are in the
matter, and see that Frank has not changed his, and then do what you
like; only don’t let him keep beating about the bush as he has been
doing, ever since he first saw her.”

“What relative is she to that Lady Emmeline woman who stayed with us one
spring?”

“Lady Emmeline’s husband and Miss Drewitt are first cousins,” Mr. Pryor
replied.

“Was that the fellow who got the estate?”

“The same.”

“Any chance at all of our clerk having it back again?”

“Not the slightest.”

“Are you going over to Ireland this year?”

“I do not know. No, I think not. I have some intention of selling
Durrow, and buying a place in England.”

“How long have you taken that idea into your head?”

“Not very long;” and Mr. Pryor returned his brother-in-law’s stare
unflinchingly.

“Don’t take it to heart,” said Mr. Gyton, with a certain kindness and
sympathy in his manner; “don’t, there’s a good fellow.”

“If you don’t take it to heart, I am sure I need not,” answered the
barrister, bitterly; “I thought the very mention of such a _mésalliance_
would have driven you out of your mind. The longer I live the less I see
one need expect any person to act as it might have been supposed he
would.”

“Then you are sorry I do not altogether disapprove of the business.”

“Do you think I am made of iron, that I should be glad?” was the reply;
but Mr. Pryor never afterwards repeated this remark; on the contrary,
when his sister told him how Frank’s wandering habits and unsettled mode
of life had been a source of grief and anxiety to both her and his
father, how anxious they were for their only son to be “settled,” and
how thankful for her part she felt that he had chosen that “dear, sweet,
pretty Geraldine, who was so fond of him,” the barrister resigned
himself to the inevitable, and began marvelling whether he could ever
look on Geraldine as his niece.

He did not much think his nephew would make any woman very happy; but
still, they were both young, and both in love; and, who knew but that it
was best as it was?

Geoffry Pryor could not say it was not best, though it ended his dream,
though it crushed out of his heart the hope he had been cherishing for
years.

“I thought you would have liked Frank to marry either rank or fortune,”
he remarked to his sister.

“So we should, but Frank has never shown the slightest predilection for
either one or the other, and really young men now-a-days do make such
matches that I am thankful he has chosen a lady by birth, and one whom
we need not be ashamed to present to any person or in any place. There
is young Munday—married his sister’s maid! Mr. Lark has just had to send
his son abroad to keep him out of the way of some most disreputable
connexion. There is Lady Ackington’s step-son run off with the
governess—and in short, my dear Geoffry—”

“In short, my dear Maria, you think, with me, that Miss Drewitt will
make almost too good a wife for Frank.”

“I think no such thing,” retorted Mrs. Gyton, indignantly; “for a young
lady in Miss Drewitt’s destitute position to make such a capital match
is remarkable; and had Mr. Gyton not taken such a fancy to her brother
it never could have been brought about. Of course we shall be glad to
see Frank settled. He has promised his father that he will really settle
down to business now, and take that position to which, as the son of the
head of the firm, he is entitled; and if he does that I shall say
meeting with Miss Drewitt was the greatest blessing which could have
befallen him. But, at the same time, I must add, that had he fancied
Miss Ackington, or even one of the Miss Larks, I should infinitely have
preferred a connexion which might have advanced his worldly interests as
well as insured his domestic happiness.”

“You really are getting eloquent, Maria.”

“It is enough to make any one indignant to hear the way you talk. A
stranger would really go away with the impression that Miss Drewitt had
ten thousand a year, and that she was conferring some honour on Frank by
accepting him, instead of being only too glad to secure such a catch.
Not but what,” continued Mrs. Gyton, noticing an angry look in her
brother’s face; “not but what I will say, they behaved admirably in the
business, which proves that, though they are poor, they have been
educated in all the ideas of the highest ranks.”

“What are the ideas of the highest ranks, Maria?” inquired Mr. Pryor.

“Not to take advantage—to act honourably—to do as you would be done by,
and so forth.”

“Oh! indeed!” remarked her brother; “after that, I think I may go home
and propose to my housekeeper.” But he did not go home, neither did he
propose to his housekeeper: he drove to the house in Islington which
Brian had taken for his mother after Harold’s death, and found Geraldine
alone in the drawing-room.

“I have come to wish you joy, dear,” he said; but there was an
unsteadiness in his voice as he spoke, and the hand which held hers
trembled a little. “I hope you will be happy, and wealthy, and wise;”
and he tried to smile; which proved so futile an attempt, that the tears
came into Geraldine’s eyes as she looked at him.

“I was foolish once,” he went on, “and thought—Ah! well, you know what I
thought. But that is all gone and past now, my child, and we will be
good friends for ever, won’t we?”

She put her other hand on his shoulder and said, “yes,” with a sob in
her voice.

“And if ever you are in any trouble or perplexity, you will come to me
as you would to—I am going to be your uncle,” he added abruptly; “so
what need I say more?”

“I will come,” she replied.

“And we will forget all the past. You will forget I was ever silly
enough to overlook the fact that I was old enough to be your father;
that the time when I might have found favour in a young girl’s eyes was
in the summers before you were born.”

“Don’t,” she pleaded; “don’t; I can’t bear it.”

He put the hair back from her forehead, he held her away from him at
arms’ length, he looked at her sweet sorrowful face—looked at it as he
knew he might never look in her face, or in the face of any other woman
again, and then he took her to him and kissed her, as he had longed to
do that day when they stood together on the hill above Kincorth.

“Is not there a nursery rhyme, Geraldine, which declares that some go
up, and some go down? I feel that I have been among the latter class;
and yet, if you are happy, my dear, I will make myself happy too. It was
a mistake; that is all; and in this world it has often seemed to me we
pay as dearly for our mistakes as we do for our sins. You may think it a
strange fancy, child. I am not going away from London; at least, I am
only going to Durrow for a month, and I hope often to see you when I
come back; constantly, indeed, after a time: yet still, I want to say
good-bye; that was the chief word I came to speak to you to-day.”

She knew what he meant; knew that it was not to her he was bidding
farewell, but to the dream that had been so sweet and so exceeding
beautiful. She knew that from thenceforth he wanted her to forget, as he
intended to forget—that from that day forward, she was to seem to him
his nephew’s affianced wife, nothing more.

Nothing more. When he was standing on the cliffs above Durrow it seemed
to him that the sea kept repeating that refrain eternally:

Nothing more—nothing—so long as the rain fell, and the wind blew,
nothing for ever!

But Geraldine was happy, and talked with her mother of brighter days to
come, when she should have no anxiety about ways and means; no need to
pinch and save; no trouble, no sorrow.

“And they will push Brian on, mamma, and he will get, oh! so rich, and
Frank wants him also to live with us till he marries; so that you need
have no hesitation now about coming to us; and we will make you so
comfortable, mamma, and take every care from you, and you shall be so
happy—so happy!”

Should she? After a talk like this Mrs. Drewitt would go to her own
room, and fall on her knees, and pray that her son and daughter might
somehow learn the truth which she had not courage enough to tell
them—that the days were not very far off when she should indeed have no
more care, when she should be very happy, but not in this world—ah! no,
not here.

 CHAPTER XIII.
 Country Gossip.


Mr. Pryor did not make a very long stay in Ireland. He merely remained a
sufficient length of time to receive Mr. Connor’s numerous reports; to
ride round his property; to point out a few improvements he wished made;
to distribute much charity; to be blessed with blessings that were
beyond reason as they were without stint; to drive out a few times; to
renew his acquaintance with the Durrow fishermen, and to hear all the
local news.

Considering that Mr. Connor had a whole year’s budget to unfold, the
amount of information was not great, although it might be interesting.
The gaugers had been over several times, and the constabulary, too,
about the stills and the smugglers; “And one gauger was not afraid to
say,” went on Mr. Connor, “that if you, sir, did your duty, there would
soon not be a still in Durrow; and about the smugglers, the coastguard
people declared I got share of the cargoes.”

“We can both bear such remarks, I suppose, Mr. Connor,” said Mr. Pryor,
smiling.

“They are wild about it, that is the thing, you know, sir. Why, they do
say that even Mr. Drewitt has turned against the stills, and is hunting
them off his property there in the Joyce county. You would not know Mr.
Maxwell, sir, if you were to see him now; he is all for law and order,
and is making himself perfectly detested by everybody, high and low. He
is abroad now, sir; travelling on the Continent with a Mr. Campnell from
Oxford, who they do say is going to be his heir, and who, many people
think, is something nearer even than that; and Lady Emmeline has left
him—did you never hear of it, sir? She has left him and is gone to live
with Mr. Vervensoe and his wife up at Cragantlet. The famine made a poor
man of him, rich though he was once; and they are glad enough to have
Lady Emmeline with them for the sake of what she pays. They do say the
life he led that misguided woman, sir, lately was shocking—shocking. I
could not tell you half the stories that are in everybody’s mouth about
his wickedness, and his cruelty; and it all began, that is, the cruelty,
because she refused to have this young man come to the house as her
nephew, or come to the house while she was in it, in fact, at all.”

“One cannot wonder at her doing so,” remarked Mr. Pryor.

“No; but I am told he did—that he said, as she had no children, the
least she could do was to let him have one of his own in the house; and
then she threatened him—I could never rightly make out with what; and
then he struck her; and then she got worse, and said she would leave
him, and expose him; and after that he begged her pardon, and talked to
her for more than an hour, and got somehow to the soft side of her; so
that she said, at last, if he would let her go, and take what she
brought with her, there might be peace between them.”

“That could only have come through the servants,” remarked Mr. Pryor,
thoughtfully.

“It came to me, most of it, from my brother-in-law; but where he got it
I cannot say.”

“And so Lady Emmeline is really gone?”

“Yes, sir. I met her the other day out driving with Mr. and Mrs.
Vervensoe; and she looked ninety. And, indeed, I was sorry to see her,”
finished Mr. Connor.

“I wonder if Maxwell Drewitt was married before he met with Lady
Emmeline—secretly, I mean,” said Mr. Pryor.

“Not a bit of it, sir; there never was a man less in the marrying notion
than that same Mr. Drewitt, if all I have heard from Ryan about his
young days is true. And if you are thinking that he was married first,
and then married Lady Emmeline afterwards, you may put that notion away
altogether. He was always far too wide-awake to get himself into any
mess of that kind. Have you been round by Kincorth, sir? He is putting
up the handsomest entrance there is in this part of the country at all;
and they do tell me the house is like a palace, and that the gardens are
just a show. He has made a second avenue, leading out on Calgillan Road;
so that the drive now goes right through the property. I heard say—but I
don’t know whether it was true or not—that some young lady in England
took a fancy to him, not knowing he was married; and that when he came
home, he cursed and swore at Lady Emmeline for living too long, and
standing in his way.”

“I think that story must be an invention,” answered Mr. Pryor; “at
least, I give my countrywomen credit for more taste than that would
imply they were possessed of.”

“I don’t know,” said Mr. Connor; “I fancy there is no way Mr. Drewitt
cannot put on him when he likes. I am sure he came over here one day
wanting gravel out of Durrow River, for his new avenue, and you would
have thought I was Prince Albert, at the very least.”

“Who is living at the Headlands, now?” asked Mr. Pryor.

“A Captain Crabbe, his name is; and awful work he and Mr. Drewitt have
about the place not being kept in good order; and about the cows getting
among the plantations. I don’t wonder, myself, at Mr. Drewitt’s being
vexed to see the grounds—you remember how he used to have them; and now
they are as bad as Cragantlet. He says he will never let it to anybody
again, unless an Englishman; and not to him, unless he signs an
agreement to keep the place as it ought to be kept. He goes on
dreadfully, though, when anything puts him out. When you are going along
the Calgillan Road you can hear him cursing his men half a mile off.”

“What do you suppose is the cause of his ill-temper?”

“Goodness knows, sir! He never had much of a temper, and now it is worse
than ever. Ryan says, he thinks it must be that the devil he has inside
him wears away his patience, and that he is glad to break out on
anybody. There is one thing sure, he has been worse since Mr. Harold
died. My brother-in-law told me he shut himself up for a whole day when
the news came over, and that no one durst go near him; and I believe he
sent to Galway, and telegraphed over to Mr. Brian, would he like to bury
him in Eversbeg. But Mr. Brian wrote back, he would not be indebted to
him for enough ground even to lay a dog in, let alone a brother.”

“What an unforgiving set they are!”

“Yes, sir; and little though you might think it, Mr. Archibald Drewitt
was just the same in some things. There was a man talking to me about
him only the other day, a poor fellow of the name of Byrne, who was
turned off one of Mr. Drewitt’s lots the time you stood for Duranmore.
He would vote for you, and so Mr. Drewitt said he should go, and he got
a farm under Mr. Waller; but someway, nothing went well with him, and
then he took a place as labourer, with Mr. Maxwell. He had always liked
Mr. Drewitt, for he had a kind, affable way with him, and a friendly
look and a pleasant word for tenant and labourer just the same; and of
course whenever he met Mr. Drewitt, for a while he touched his hat to
him, expecting that Mr. Drewitt would give him time of day,[1] or say it
was fine weather for the crops; or perhaps, how are the pigs? or is the
wife getting better, Barney? But Mr. Drewitt never took the least notice
of him, no more than if he hadn’t been speaking to him every week for
the previous ten years. As you may suppose, Barney quit touching his
hat; but he said that about two months before Mr. Drewitt’s death, he
saw him creeping along the road, and that his mind misgave him the
master was not long for this world. So he thought he would like to speak
to him once before he went, (the old fellow cried when he told me about
it), and he stood up close to the roadside of the footpath, so that Mr.
Drewitt could not help seeing him; and he touched his hat, after twenty
years, to his old landlord again, and says: “I was sorry to hear you had
been bad, sir; I hope your honour’s better.” Mr. Drewitt looked him all
over from head to foot, and the man was ragged enough; so perhaps, he
thought his politics had not done much for him; but he never answered
him, no more than if he had been dirt under his feet. Byrne never saw
him after that, but he followed his coffin and saw it put in Eversbeg
Abbey. I call a thing like that carrying hatred too far; but of course I
am no judge.”

Footnote 1:

  Meaning good-morning, or good-evening.

Mr. Pryor walked on for a few steps in silence, slowly, thinking of all
his agent had told him while he walked.

Then he began showing Mr. Connor the alterations he desired to have
made, remarking at the same time—

“It is very likely my nephew and Miss Geraldine may pay you a visit next
summer, and I should like the place to look its best in honour of them.”

“Are they going,” began Connor, considering in what suitable form of
words to clothe his question, “are they going to make a match of it?”

“They are talking of doing so,” answered Mr. Pryor, with a smile which
he could not suppress.

“I am right glad to hear it, sir, if I may make so free as to say so. If
Miss Geraldine is half as well as every one here wishes her, she will
make him a good wife, and he will be a good husband to her!”

“Thank you, Connor.” Mr. Pryor said this simply, and as though he was
thanking Connor for some compliment paid to himself.

“And you think they will come next summer, sir?”

“I hope so,” answered Mr. Pryor: but many things happened before that
next summer which the barrister then had no idea the future could bring
to pass.

He went to call on Lady Emmeline ere he returned to London, and found
her what Connor had described—an old withered woman, bent and aged, but
still rouged to the eyes, and dressed in the extreme of the fashion.

“I am so glad to see you, Mr. Pryor,” she said, entering the room with
skirts so voluminous and crinoline so extensive that it was some time
before the barrister could see which was woman and which steel and silk
and lace. “It was so kind of you to come, considering all things, so
very kind.”

Mr. Pryor ventured to think it was very kind of her ladyship to say so.

“It is the truth, it is nothing but the truth; there are not many who
would come running after an old woman like me, though there was a time—”
and Lady Emmeline stopped, and would have coloured had not the rouge on
her face rendered such a performance as impossible as it was
unnecessary. “Do you remember the election at Duranmore, Mr. Pryor? Ah!
who that saw me then would ever have thought I could come to this?”

When a woman’s case is beyond flattery it is beyond consolation. Mr.
Pryor felt this and held his tongue.

“You have heard, I suppose,” resumed Lady Emmeline, after leaving a
decent space for compliment had he chosen to fill it up, “that
unfortunate circumstances have compelled me to accept an asylum here.
Ah! dear; to think my husband should ever have treated me as he has
done: and I made him, Mr. Pryor; it was my money made him; and I did
once think he was fond of me, I did indeed.” Poor Lady Emmeline! there
was no falsehood nor affectation about those words; and although her
tears might wear strange channels down her cheeks, giving her a
generally wrung-out and draggled appearance about the face, Mr. Pryor
did not feel inclined even to smile, but listened sympathetically while
the neglected poetess proceeded.

“I often think of those sweet lines by Moore,” and she repeated—

 “‘When first I met thee warm and true,
 There shone such truth about thee,
 And on thy lips such promise hung
 I thought ’twere sin to doubt thee.’

“That was just my case, Mr. Pryor. I believed in him implicitly. I
thought he could say nothing but what was good and true; that he was
brave and honest. I have found how much mistaken I was since those days.
But it is too late now for repentance, almost for regret;” and she went
on whispering, as was her wont, the remainder of the lines—

 “‘Go, deceiver, go—
 The heart whose hopes could make it
 Trust one so false, so low,
 Deserved that thou shouldst break it.’

“So false, so low,” she said, shaking her head sadly; “aye, and so base
and cruel, too.”

Thinking to divert her attention, Mr. Pryor asked her when she had been
last in London, and when she intended paying it another visit, and in
return got more than he bargained for.

In the spring she hoped to go to London again, but not till then,
unless, indeed, dear Geraldine’s marriage took place first, in which
case she should make a point of being present.

“I was so truly delighted to hear of the probability of her settling so
well,” went on Lady Emmeline. “At one time, do you know, Mr. Pryor, we
all thought you were to be the fortunate man. Shows how wrongly people
may judge sometimes—even the cleverest people.” Which was a back-handed
blow at her husband.

Mr. Pryor winced—the wound was still green—but the lady proceeded. “I
was always very fond of Geraldine—fonder than of either of her
brothers—though Brian was a gentlemanlike lad, and promised to grow up
handsome. I thought Geraldine took most after her mother. I suppose she
is greatly improved in her appearance since she went to London?”

The barrister paused for a moment before he answered. He was thinking of
the Geraldine who had stood talking with him on the way to Durrow, and
wondering in what way Lady Emmeline could imagine it possible for such a
vision of loveliness to be improved.

“She is thinner,” he said; that seemed the truest and most unconcerned
statement he could make, under the circumstances; “but otherwise I do
not think her much altered.”

“Ah! Well, that would be an improvement,” said Lady Emmeline. “She was
always a little too plump for perfect elegance.”

“Opinions may differ on that point,” Mr. Pryor would have answered, but
he fortunately remembered that Lady Emmeline, who had always prided
herself on her graceful figure and perfect elegance, was as thin as a
thread-paper.

He therefore contented himself with saying, that when she came to London
she would be able to judge for herself; and shortly afterwards took his
leave, though earnestly entreated by Mrs. Vervensoe, _née_ Munks, to
stop for dinner.

“I had five hundred and sixty-one things I wanted to ask you about,”
said that energetic lady; “and I had twice as many, I know, to tell you;
but, however, if you must go, why you must. It is treating us very
shabbily, though; and I do not know what Mr. Vervensoe will say to me
when he comes in, about letting you go.”

“You should tell him, ‘a wilful man must have his way,’ and then he can
say nothing,” answered Mr. Pryor.

“But, really now, is not it mean and shabby?”

“It would be, perhaps, if I could remain; but as I cannot, you must
exercise your charity, and think as well of me as you can.”

“There is no fear of our not thinking well of you. Good-bye, then, if it
must be good-bye. Give my love to Geraldine, and tell her I shall expect
a piece of the wedding-cake; she had a bit of mine to dream on, I know,
when she was not three years old.”

With which pleasing reminiscence Mrs. Vervensoe allowed their visitor to
depart; and accordingly Mr. Pryor rode away from Cragantlet, which,
though twenty miles from Durrow, supposing Duranmore were taken by the
way, was not more than fifteen, if the traveller turned up the lane
leading past Regan’s, crossed the Calgillan coach-road, and then kept
straight on for the ruins of Castle Cronach.

Under any circumstances it was a long ride, and a lonely; and Mr. Pryor
had got very weary of his own thoughts before he reached the highway,
with its many turnings and windings, along which they had trotted all
four abreast the first day he ever beheld Durrow.

He had not gone a mile, however, past Castle Cronach, when a new subject
for thought presented itself, in the shape of a jaunting-car, on which
were seated two gentlemen, one on each side, with the driver occupying
the position that of right belongs to him, but which in Ireland he seems
never to assume, unless dire necessity compels his doing so.

One of the gentlemen was Maxwell Drewitt; the other, a dark moustached
youth, apparently not more than two and twenty, though he had a beard
that might have been growing for a dozen years.

“Mr. Campnell, I presume,” thought Mr. Pryor, as the car pursued its way
to Duranmore and he to Durrow. “I wonder if he wears that mass of hair
to prevent any one tracing a likeness to his father; if it be his
father,” corrected the barrister.

“Where could they be coming from, Connor?” he asked during the course of
the evening.

“Most likely from Castlebar. It is nearer from here to Castlebar than it
is from Galway to here, and a nicer country to pass through too. And so
you saw Mr. Campnell; and what kind of a looking young gentleman is he,
sir, if I may inquire?”

“He is so covered with hair, it is almost impossible to say what he is
like. He has dark eyes, as well as I could observe, and that is all I
can tell you about him.”

“And he is only twenty-one, I hear.”

There was the rustling of a woman’s dress through the hall, and Mr.
Pryor, turning round sharply, guessed that Mrs. Connor had been
listening at the open door.

There was nothing in their conversation that he cared if all Ireland
were cognizant of, yet the circumstance gave him an uncomfortable
impression, and made him feel a little doubtful about Mrs. Connor’s
entire trustworthiness.

While she, poor Jenny! she stole out to a field at the back of the
house, where her children were playing in the summer’s evening, and
sitting down on the grass, took the youngest to her heart, while she
thought about the boy she had borne in the times gone by; whom she had
never nursed on her knees; whom she had never folded in her arms; whom
she had been forced to send away; whom she had never through the years
set eyes on; who would never be told the name of his mother, but whom,
she knew, was coming to Kincorth that day, to be, if he lived,
Kincorth’s owner, when perhaps she and his father were both dead.

 CHAPTER XIV.
 What met Brian.


A winter’s day in London, more especially when that winter’s day happens
to be raw, damp, drizzling and foggy, is one, I think, of the most
depressing ordeals through which a human being can be called to pass—a
day when the streets are sloppy; when the pavements are greasy; when the
passing omnibuses are full of wet men, and wetter women; when people
poke you in the eyes with their umbrellas; when more people poke you in
the ribs, and jostle you into the middle of another set of people, who
shove you back again and probably anathematize you into the bargain;
when there is no business doing in shops, where the gas is lighted, nor
in offices, where the gas is lighted likewise; when if you are out
collecting money, nobody will pay you; when if you owe any person any
money, everybody makes a point of boring you (in the gaslight), and
saying they felt sure of finding you within; when the apples in the
costermongers’ carts look damp and dirty; when there is a dull yellow,
leaden sky above, and an atmosphere you can taste around you; when
London Bridge looks as much like water as the Thames; when you do not
feel as if there ever had been any fine weather on earth; when you are
quite certain there will never be any fine weather again;—this, I say,
is the kind of day to try a man’s equanimity, and to put his
cheerfulness to the test.

There was one man however who, having been set down by an omnibus coming
from the Borough at the corner of Mansion House and Princes Streets,
walked along, careless of the weather, careless of the passers-by,
careless of the dull leaden sky, of the greasy pavements and the sloppy
horse-roads. He looked neither to right nor left as he strode away after
his business; he was evidently thinking of something pleasant, carrying
his sunshine along with him, as some country-people carry lanterns: that
man was Brian Drewitt; he had just come from Paris, and he was bound for
Austinfriars.

He wanted to see Mr. Gyton before he left the City, and as it was
getting on for three o’clock he hurried his pace and dashed on towards
Austinfriars as though he were walking for a wager.

If you do not know Austinfriars, reader, you are not acquainted with one
of the queerest nooks that the march of modern improvement and the
heartlessness of railway companies has left us. Before some new
extension gets its bill passed, I should recommend you to go and see
that curious spot, where, in 1253, says Pennant, “was erected by
Humphrey Bohun, Earl of Hereford, the house of the Augustins, for friars
eremites of that order.” Until very recently, one of the oldest churches
in London (built before 1361) stood about the centre of Austinfriars;
the external walls still, I believe, remain, but the interior was,
unhappily, destroyed by fire some few years since.

In it were laid great people: Edmund Guy de Meric, who, it is stated,
incited Richard II. to the murder of the Duke of Gloucester; Lucie,
daughter of Barnaby, Lord of Milan; the great Earl of Arundel, beheaded
on Tower Hill, in 1397; the Earl of Oxford, who suffered a similar fate
in 1463; Edward Stafford, Duke of Buckingham, in 1521; while,
twenty-five years afterwards, this church was filled with herrings, by
order of Henry VIII., who used Grey Friars, at the same period, as a
warehouse for wine.

There are fewer historical records of Austinfriars than of almost any
other spot about the City. In a map of London, A. D. 1563, the old house
of the Augustines is not even marked; yet some gleams of the olden glory
still lie here and there—in its out-of-the-way courts—in its
old-fashioned houses—under the stones of its ancient church—for the
curious in such matters to note, as they pass by.

Some time, it may be, that I shall ask you, reader who have so often
before this, strayed with me into world-forgotten corners—into silent
and deserted nooks—to turn aside from the bustle and din of the City
streets, and spend a few minutes with me in Austinfriars; but that day
must not be now, when Brian Drewitt is hurrying along to catch the head
of the great house of Gyton, Lark, Munday, Hatfield, and Company, before
he takes his portly person westward.

Very cheerfully, Brian Drewitt pursued his way. He knew that he had
accomplished the business on which he was sent abroad satisfactorily.

He felt certain he should receive the praise of his employers for the
manner in which he had performed his mission. It had been intimated to
him more than once, that in being sent abroad he was also being put on
his trial, and that if he stood the test of the negotiation entrusted to
him, his salary would be at once increased, and every facility afforded
him for pushing his way up to the very top of the tree.

When he reached the top, he had then his vague designs of vengeance to
gratify; but upon the lower branches were hanging sweet home fruits,
that Brian longed to gather. He wanted to prevail on his mother not to
live with Geraldine; to live near, but not with her; to have her all to
himself, to provide with the little luxuries that her delicate health
required—that it would he a pleasure and a pride to him to place around
her.

If she went with Geraldine, it would be Frank Gyton, and not Brian
Drewitt, whose money might anticipate her wants, and outspeed her very
wishes. No one knew better than Brian how both he and she had saved, and
pinched, to pay the expenses attendant on Harold’s trial—on their
removal to London—on his long sickness—on his early death. Even
Geraldine never fully understood all her mother had denied herself, so
that Harold might not suffer for lack of anything he desired. And now,
hungrily and greedily, the son who did comprehend everything, longed to
have his mother, who had suffered so much, all to himself, that he might
make her life a happy one at last, and render the evening of her days
the calmest, the brightest, the peacefulest portion of her existence.

There are some men who, from their youth upwards, need to have something
to love—something upon which to pour out all their affection, all their
devotion. It may be father, brother, sister, mother, child, that they
gather into their heart, and keep there: whatever the object may be, it
is love they give, rather than love they receive. It is necessary to
their very existence to be planning, thinking, working, for some one;
and during the entire of his short life, Brian Drewitt had been
planning, thinking, working, for others rather than himself.

He could scarcely remember the time when the love he felt for his mother
was not a species of idolatry. Instinctively, perhaps, even from
childhood, the stronger nature knew that the weaker had need of it; and
as years went by, and the child became a boy, and the boy a man, this
feeling grew stronger and stronger, till at last Brian came to
understand that whatsoever his mother stood in need of, if it were in
the power of a human being to procure it, she should have her wish
gratified.

And at last he felt that it would soon be in his power to make his
mother’s life comfortable and happy, and to free it from care.
Comfortable and happy—did I not say once before, that was not to be in
this world for ever?

Onwards he sped; eager, sanguine, triumphant. Mr. Gyton, senior, was not
gone; and with his snuffbox on the table, with his bandana ready for
use, with his coat thrown well back, and his thumbs stuck in the
arm-holes of his superfine black cloth waistcoat, he listened to Brian’s
narration with every sign of approval visible in his face.

When Brian had quite finished he said—

“We did not expect you home until to-morrow; but now that you are here I
believe my son has something to say to you. Meantime, I may add that you
can draw your salary at the rate of two hundred and fifty pounds per
annum from to-morrow.”

Mr. Gyton had a way of uttering those two words “per annum” which always
seemed to give money its true value in the listener’s ears; and
accordingly, Brian Drewitt thanked him very heartily for the advance,
and had just reached the middle of a neatly-turned sentence when Mr.
Gyton, junior, entered.

“Don’t stand wasting your time here, Brian,” he said; “but go home at
once. There’s something up there. Geraldine sent here this morning to
know your address, and telegraphed for you.”

“What is it?” Brian asked: he stretched out his one hand and seized the
table tightly as he spoke.

“Your mother is very ill, I believe; don’t turn so white, Brian; it may
be nothing to signify. I have told them to get you a cab. Should you
like me to go with you?”

“No, thank you,” Brian answered; “no—no.” Even then he wanted to have
her all to himself, and he blindly made his way out of Mr. Gyton’s
presence-chamber, and across the clerks’ office, and down the stairs,
and so out into the court, at the entrance to which a hansom was waiting
for him.

“Drive fast!” that was all he could say; but the man obeyed his
instructions literally. Up the City Road, round by Duncan Terrace, out
across the Lower Road, and then up Upper Street, till they were close to
Canonbury Tower.

“Set me down here,” Brian said, pushing up the little trap-door, and the
driver pulled up instantly.

“Your fare?” was the next question; and Brian counted him out a sum,
which even the man’s conscience pricked him for demanding, without a
remonstrance, but stupidly, and as though he were drunk.

He never could tell what feeling possessed him to stop the cab and walk
quietly up the street where was his home. He never knew how he got from
the cab to the door opposite his own door, where he paused and looked
across the way.

Every blind in the house was down.

He saw a gentleman coming out; a gentleman whose carriage stood waiting
for him to enter. Brian crossed the street and asked him what was the
matter.

“A poor lady has done with all suffering,” was the reply.

“Mrs. Drewitt?”

“Yes; are you her son?”

It was too much, the reaction was too great; and Brian would have fallen
on the pavement, but that he staggered by chance against the doctor’s
brougham.

“Come in,” said the doctor, “and I will go in with you.”

“I won’t,” answered Brian; and he turned and walked back down the
street, like one in a dream.

For hours and hours he walked about before he could face the house
again. With his clothes soaked through, with his hair damp and wet,
shivering with the cold, heartbroken and miserable, Brian Drewitt at
last made up his mind to face the inevitable, and retraced his steps
towards home.

It was late in the evening when he approached the door; the street-lamps
had long been lighted; the shops in Upper Street were all brilliantly
illuminated as he passed by; the pavements were crowded with people,
well-dressed, lively, happy, thoughtless; with women going to make their
purchases; with women only curious to look through the windows at the
goods displayed within; with men on their way from the City to Holloway
and Highbury; with the husbands, and the brothers, and the lovers of the
females, who were going shopping, or window-gazing, with idlers and
loungers of all descriptions—amongst whom Brian made his way, with a
dull, stupid sorrow tearing at his heart, with a conviction that he was
going home to meet something very dreadful—something that he still
doubted whether he should have strength of mind to meet.

He lifted his hand to the knocker, and rapped hesitatingly for
admittance. When the servant opened the door she put her apron to her
eyes, and said, “Oh! sir——”

“I have heard,” Brian interrupted, sharp and stern, and walked straight
into the parlour.

There sat Geraldine—her eyes red with weeping, her cheeks pale with
watching. Mrs. Gyton was holding one hand, and Frank the other, while a
man whom Brian had never seen before was standing with his back to the
fire, talking vehemently and positively.

At sight of Brian, he stopped short, and while Geraldine, with a cry,
ran towards her brother, he stooped down and inquired of Mr. Gyton who
the new comer might be.

“Her brother,” was the answer; and when Brian came forward into the
room, he found his hand clasped in that of the stranger, who said—

“It is a poor welcome home for you, this—but we did all for her we
could.”

“May I ask your name?” Brian’s tone was as quiet as though nothing
unusual were lying in the house.

“Murphy—at your service. It is many a year since I saw you; and it is
just the same time since I saw your poor mother. God help her! she must
have gone through an awful lot of trouble. I can remember her as
young-looking as your sister is now, with her hair as soft and dark
every bit as yours, Miss; but there is one comfort, she can feel no more
sorrow where she is gone: women’s heads are not turned grey with grief
there. Just let him be,” he whispered to Francis Gyton. “You know he
must feel it, so do not try to get him to put on a face before you.”

Brian had sat down at the table and rested his head on his hand. All at
once he looked up, with no dimness in his eye, with no hesitation in his
voice—

“I should like to see her,” he said; “may I go up stairs?”

Geraldine rose to go with him, but Mr. Murphy gently pushed her aside.

“With your permission,” he observed, taking one of the candles off the
table, and he and Brian left the room.

“Did you attend her?” Brian asked.

“No, I was only called in last night. She had a very good doctor,
though—very good. The whole College of Surgeons could not have saved
her, nor all the physicians in London to the back of that.”

“It was sudden, I suppose?” Brian scarcely spoke above a whisper, and he
drew Mr. Murphy nervously into the drawing-room, still evidently
unwilling to look upon the dead.

“Towards the last, very; but these cases always are sudden. She must
have known how it would end for a considerable time past.”

“Can you tell me if she had been long under treatment?”

“Twelve months, her doctor says; but your sister never knew a syllable
of what was the matter with her. She does not appear to have told a
living soul belonging to her a word about her disease.”

“And was it incurable from the first?”

“Yes.”

“Could money have kept her with us a little longer? had anxiety any
share in hastening her death?”

“I do not know; I cannot tell. Anxiety always aggravates sickness, as a
rule; and money also, as a rule, alleviates it; but in this particular
case I cannot say. I do not know how she has lived; I can only guess how
she must have suffered.”

There was a moment’s silence; then Mr. Murphy said, “Are you ready?” and
Brian bent his head in answer.

The bed-chamber opened off the drawing-room with folding-doors: as Mr.
Murphy turned the handle of one of these, Brian put his hand on the
surgeon’s and held it so tight that he could not shoot back the lock.

“Shall we wait a while?” he asked.

“No—no;” and Brian relaxed his grasp and put up his right hand to his
head, as though, fancying he had on his hat, he was going to remove it.

The bed was made, and unoccupied; there was a fearful formality in the
arrangement of the furniture; something terrible in the prim, frigid
neatness which pervaded the apartment.

There had been a space cleared in the centre, and there on trestles
stood the shell.

Mr. Murphy laid his hand on the lid, and looked inquiringly at his
companion.

Brian nodded, and the surgeon pushed the lid aside.

There she lay; there she slept the sleep that on this side eternity
knows no waking. She had been tossed by the billows; she had been amid
the waves; she had been buffeted by the winds; she had been beaten
hither and thither by the world’s storms; she had crossed the ocean of
suffering; she had been out in seas of trouble; she had felt the deeps
go over her head, and passed through torrents of tribulation—but it was
all over now—the voyage, the fear, the struggle, the misery—for the Lord
had taken her, after much weariness, unto Himself to rest, and she was
at last brought by Him “unto the haven where she would be.”

There she lay, with the wrinkles smoothed out of her face; with the
lines care had written erased from her forehead; with the beauty of her
youth almost restored to her—so unlike what she had been in her later
life, so different to what he could remember her, that Brian Drewitt
looked in Mr. Murphy’s face incredulously.

“That is not my mother?” he said; and he drew back a step.

“It is all you will ever again see for your mother here,” was the reply,
uttered slowly and thoughtfully.

Brian stooped over the coffin. Mr. Murphy always believed he was
listening to hear if she breathed; but he was only touching her lips
with his lips—only silently whispering words of tenderness—of passionate
sorrow—that he had a vague feeling she might understand, though she
could never answer.

“Would you mind leaving me for a minute?” he said, at last. “Would you
go into the next room and wait for me?—if you go down stairs some of
them will be coming up.”

Without uttering a syllable, Mr. Murphy placed the candle on the
chimneypiece, and left Brian, closing the folding-doors after him.

The surgeon thought that very likely Brian wanted to have a good cry
unnoticed—unpitied. He had seen as strong natures weeping in their agony
and despair. He knew the blow must have been heavy—the wound sharp; and
he knew, likewise, that at such times sorrow must have its way in some
form or other: for all of which reasons, he left Brian willingly, and,
walking to one of the windows in the drawing-room, lifted a corner of
the blind and looked out.

Had he been able to see what was passing in the inner chamber, he would
scarcely have remained contemplating the wet street and the flickering
gas-lamps for so long.

At the head of his mother’s coffin Brian stood erect; one arm was
stretched out over the dead, and he was swearing an oath, to himself,
full of vengeance and of hate. Beside her coffin he was pouring out
imprecations on all who had ever caused her misery—just as Maxwell had
said the peasants sat beside their desolate hearths, cursing the men who
had taken the roofs off their heads, and left them without a shelter
from the storm.

There were no tears in Brian Drewitt’s eyes—there was no softness in
Brian Drewitt’s heart. We had best come out and close the door, and
leaving him alone; for the days were nigh at hand when he repented him
of his blasphemy—when he covered his face, and acknowledged his sin, and
bowed his head, while that angel of the Lord,—which teaches unto men all
manner of good things—suffering—showed him how God’s ways are not as
man’s ways—how they are eternally wise and eternally just, although, to
our short-sightedness, they may oftentimes seem indeed inscrutable and
past finding out.

 CHAPTER XV.
 Madcap’s last Leap.


There was a great meet of the Duranmore foxhounds at Kincorth, and on
the lawn and about the drive were hunters in abundance: gentlemen in
scarlet coats and buckskins by the dozen, grooms _ad libitum_, and
hangers-on past counting.

It was a clear, bright morning. Every man’s spirits were of the highest;
every man’s girths of the tightest; everybody was expecting prime sport;
and the breakfast of which they had just partaken was one certainly not
calculated to damp their courage or to retard their pace.

Wine had been drunk freely—conversation had circulated more freely
still. Mr. Maxwell Drewitt was a good host, and knew how to make his
guests comfortable; and the absence of a lady of the house tended, in
the opinion of most present, to make the repast pleasanter still. Mr.
Drewitt was perhaps the gayest of all present. He laughed, he joked, he
talked, he drank—not to excess—but certainly beyond his wont; and he
backed his mare, Madcap, to leap Durrow River—if, as was most likely,
the fox took that course—better than the youngest hunter on the lawn.
“She is nine years old,” he said; “but I would not change her for any
other animal in my stable.”

“It is a shame for you to be hunting her still,” remarked Mr. Frost, who
was Lady Marsden’s nephew.

“It would be a shame for you to hunt her,” retorted Maxwell; “for you
ride, I suppose, thirteen stone every pound of it; but I, who am a light
weight, cannot possibly distress her; and then she loves the tally-ho!
and the long swinging gallop after the hounds as much as either of us.”

They had all risen from table by this time, and were standing, some on
the lawn, some in the hall, some on the drive, making altogether a
bright and animated picture.

Nothing prettier indeed in its way can be imagined than the scene at
Kincorth that morning. Far below lay Duranmore Bay, with its green
islands shining like emeralds in the beams of the midday sun. Although
it was winter, the morning had broken up clear and bright and pleasant;
the mountains were not enveloped in mists; you could see over the
country for miles and miles; in the distance the Twelve Pins showed blue
and distinct against the sky, while for foreground the looker-on had the
lawn and trees and gardens of Kincorth; steeds plunging and pawing;
grooms running hither and thither; the dogs kept in order by the
whipper-in, and red coats and buckskins and top-boots appearing at every
turn.

The fulness of life seemed to be in that place at that moment; it was
impossible to imagine sickness prostrating one of those men, sorrow
touching one of their hearts, anguish wringing one of their souls.

The winter’s sun looked down upon the scene, brightening and beautifying
all it touched.

“One moment, Mr. Drewitt, if you please.” It was Ryan, who spoke to his
employer when Maxwell came out to mount.

“Make haste, then.”

“I should like to see you alone.”

“What a fool you must be, Ryan, coming at this time! I cannot leave my
guests. Say what you have got to say here, or else leave it to a more
convenient opportunity.”

“I must speak to you in private. Lead your horse up to that privet
hedge, if you do not wish to come into the house, for speak to you I
must and will.”

Maxwell looked at the man who uttered these words, looked at him from
head to foot, but Ryan never flinched.

“I am in earnest,” he said; “and I am sober. Let me speak to you; I will
not keep you five minutes.”

Mr. Drewitt flung the reins to a groom.

“Hold her a second,” he said, and turned aside with his lawyer.

“Now, what excuse have you to make for intruding upon me?” he fiercely
demanded: “what do you mean by coming up here at such a time?”

“I have a letter from London this morning,” said Ryan. “Mrs. Drewitt is
dead.”

“How the devil does her death concern me?” asked Maxwell.

“It concerns me,” answered Ryan. “There is her son dead—here is she
dead. Mr. Drewitt, I can’t stand it. I won’t stand it any longer.”

“What do you mean? Do you think I killed them?”

“Yes, I do,” returned Ryan; “you and I between us. I wish to God I had
let old Neill do his worst! I wish to the Lord I had been hung, drawn,
and quartered, before I ever was indebted to you for sixpence! But I
won’t hold my tongue any longer. You promised me to do well for Mrs.
Drewitt and her children, and this is the end of it—this!”

“Ryan, you are drunk,” said his employer, putting a curb upon his temper
as he spoke.

“If I am, it is on grief then,” was the reply. “Oh! why did I ever put
it in your power to beggar them—to send them away from home, and
friends, and all, to die!”

“That is a question you ought to be able to answer for yourself without
any difficulty,” sneered Maxwell Drewitt; “and as for holding your
tongue, or not holding it, you can exercise your own discretion. I am
not in your power. You may try to ruin me if you like, but you will only
ruin yourself. You may say you gave the later will to me, and added the
codicil to the former will yourself, at my dictation; but you have only
your own word; you have no proof, no corroborative evidence. And look
here, Ryan,” he added; “so sure as you begin a game of that kind, I will
have you shut up in a mad-house for life. Poor fool! you thought to
frighten me, I suppose. Go home, man, go home; I am not such a born
idiot as to let any created being have the whip-hand of me.”

“But I have the whip-hand of you,” answered Ryan; “and if you don’t do
the thing that is right by that woman’s children, by—I’ll use it. I am
no fool either, Maxwell Drewitt. Yes, for all your properties, you are
no more than Maxwell Drewitt to me now. And I knew that that Campnell
you are so taken on with is your own son, and that it was he, not Harold
Drewitt, who should have stood in the dock. Ha! have I touched you at
last? Who is the fool now? Can nobody put two and two together, do you
think, but yourself? I won’t keep silence,” he went on passionately; “it
was only last night I was dreaming about Mrs. Drewitt, and I thought she
came to the foot of my bed and said: ‘Ryan, you know all about that
robbery; clear my poor boy’s memory.’ And I believe she was there, that
is, her spirit. I am sure she came and parted the curtains, and spoke
just those very words to me; and I will clear his memory, unless you
give back Kincorth, or Kincorth’s value, to him who has the best right
to it.”

“They are waiting for me, and I must go,” said Maxwell Drewitt, and his
voice sounded so strange and unnatural, that Ryan’s rage was quieted for
the moment. “What do you mean to do?” he asked.

“What do you mean to do?” and Maxwell faced round on him like a maniac.

“I mean to go to London and tell Brian Drewitt all,” was the reply.

“You can tell nothing without criminating yourself,” said Maxwell.

“I know that.”

“And you will tell him—about—tell him of what you suspect, in short?”

“Yes, everything. I swore long ago I would be even with you one day; and
I will be even with you now.”

“Are you sure?”

“Yes, I am;” and the pair drew back a step or two from one another, and
looked each in his enemy’s face as they had done years before—years and
years previously, in that little cottage down by the sea.

“I don’t think you will,” began Maxwell, after he had stared for a
moment full into Ryan’s eyes; “I don’t think you will. You say Arthur
Campnell is my son, and you say right; but you have not said who Arthur
Campnell’s mother is. Can you guess?”

“No, and do not want to guess. What can it signify to me who she was?”

“Will you guess?”

“I have told you I will not.”

“Shall I tell you her name?”

“If you like.”

“Do you ask me to tell you?”

“I do not.”

“Then I will tell you without your asking. Arthur Campnell’s mother
lives over at Durrow, and her husband’s name is Connor. Now do your
worst. The man must get up very early in the morning who means to play
card against card with me.”

“You are the damndest villain, and the most cursed liar!”

“Am I? Go and ask your sister if I lie.”

“I will; and if I find you are lying, as I feel sure you are—if I make
sure you have thrown dirt on her name to shield your son from disgrace,
then, Maxwell Drewitt, then the dogs pity your cries, for I won’t. I
swear I won’t!”

But he spoke the last words to the air, for Mr. Drewitt had already left
him, and was walking rapidly across the lawn to the spot where his horse
stood.

In his heart he was cursing the hunt and the guests, the sunshine and
the breeze; and as he rode to the piece of plantation on the side of
Calgillan Hill, where the underwood was thick and the fox hidden away,
he wished he had sent Ryan out of the country long before, and that, let
come or go what would, he had not told him anything about Jenny, whom he
had betrayed and ruined and deserted in the years that were now so very
far behind him—in the years that could return no more.

“You were right—he has made for Durrow,” said Mr. Frost; and the words
were scarcely out of his lips before the hounds were off in full cry,
and the horses off after them.

On and away; galloping across the fields; leaping the eternal stone
fences; plunging through bogs; dashing over the moorland—in the
excitement of that mad ride Maxwell forgot everything save the wild
pleasure which is caused by passing at the full top of a horse’s speed
through the air—forgot everything save the pace at which Madcap was
going, and the ease with which she took her leaps.

“Now for the river!” he said, and as though Madcap had heard his words,
she laid back her ears, and bounded on like a thing possessed, towards
the bank.

Age had not stiffened her limbs, nor crippled her pace. There was not a
horse in the field could keep up with her save one, and that was a
hunter belonging to Captain Crabbe, and ridden by that gentleman’s son,
who, having been trying all the way to pass Madcap, now shot by, almost
touching the chesnut as he did so, and eliciting a volley of oaths from
Maxwell for his awkwardness.

Boring on the bit, Madcap tore along. All her spirit was up, and she
flew after Captain Crabbe’s fleabitten old screw, as Maxwell had often
contemptuously called the horse, like the wind.

Maxwell had his work cut out to hold her. It was a beautiful piece of
turf, smooth and level as a bowling-green. There was no break, no pause,
till they came to the river, which ran broad and deep between two steep
and awkward banks away to the sea. Mr. Crabbe was already close on the
brink; but at that very moment, when he thought he was going to beat the
chesnut for ever—when with a blow of his heel and cut of his whip he was
urging his horse to the leap, the brute swerved suddenly round, and
sticking its plain ugly neck out straight before it, galloped back as
hard as it could go along the way it had come.

Mr. Crabbe, taken completely unawares by this sudden manœuvre, had
scarcely recovered himself from the shock of the sudden baulk before
there came a tremendous crash.

Maxwell, tearing on for the leap himself, could not get out of the way.
He saw the collision coming, but was powerless to avert it. He felt a
shock just as though a battering ram had come up against him; a shock
which lifted him right out of the saddle—lifted him so high, that he
could remember the reins being jerked out of his hand; and then he came
down flat, with his back on the ground and his face to the sky; in which
position he lay while the whole field jumped over him.

“Drewitt’s down—Drewitt’s dead!” was the shout; but not a man could stop
his horse to look after him until Mr. Frost, who had not been going at
quite so furious a pace as the others, managed to pull up, and dismount.

“I’m not dead,” Maxwell managed to say, “but I’m badly hurt; don’t touch
me. I didn’t try to move because I knew the horses would leap me, and
that if I stirred I should likely have my brains knocked out. Let me lie
quiet for a while; don’t stop for me, go on.”

But there were about half a dozen gathered about him by this time who
would not go on; who sent to the nearest cabin and had a door taken off
its hinges, on which Maxwell, groaning with pain, was laid gently and
tenderly.

“The mare?” he said; but nobody answered him.

“The mare?” he repeated, and Mr. Frost asked—

“Where is the chesnut? he wants to know.”

“She is all right,” answered some one.

“You are not telling me the truth?” Maxwell said, turning his eyes on
the speaker, for he was too much shaken to move his head. “I want to
know about the mare.”

“Well, then, if you must know, poor old Madcap has taken her last leap,
and she died in taking it.”

The injured man made no answer; he closed his eyes; whilst all the
people around looked at one another.

There was not a person present who did not feel grieved at Madcap’s end.

She had been to each of them almost as a friend—so gallant, so fleet, so
daring, so proud of herself, and her small head, and her arched neck,
and her flowing mane, and her shining coat—so proud, so petted, so
dainty, so conceited, and so beautiful, that Lord Marsden once said, “He
thought there must be a woman’s soul inside her, for that he had never
seen an animal give herself such airs and graces before.”

Poor Chessy—poor Madcap! She had made on like the rest for her leap, and
taken it—taken it at the worst point she could have chosen. Missing her
footing on the other side, she fought desperately for a moment to keep
herself from slipping while she tried to scramble up the bank. One man
made a clutch at her bridle, but missed it, fortunately, perhaps, for
him; for, with a scream almost such as a human being might have uttered,
she fell backwards into the river.

Flynn had always said she was as sensible as a Christian, and those who
saw that mad struggle for life, who heard that sound which is never
uttered by a horse, save under the influence of some terrible pain, or
some frightful fear, thought that Madcap must have had a kind of human
comprehension of what the river’s bed held for her—great blocks of
granite, which cut her all to bits; huge stones, against which she was
knocked all to pieces; and just enough water to float her, lying on her
side and kicking convulsively, down the stream, which was dyed red with
her blood.

Poor Madcap—poor beauty! She kept her head up as long as she could, but
soon her strength failed, her neck sank below the surface, the water
filled her nostrils, and before many minutes it was all over.

“She had,” as Maxwell’s informant truly said, “taken her last leap, and
died in taking it.”

“And there’s not her like left in Ireland,” remarked Flynn, who in the
course of the afternoon dragged her, stiff and stark, out of the river,
at a curve half a mile below where the accident happened, and then he
fell to sobbing over her like a child.

“I’ll hamstring that brute of Crabbe’s the first time I get the chance,”
he said to the grooms who came with him; “a score of such ill-looking
divils wouldn’t make one of her. Not but what we must all die some day,”
he concluded; “and I don’t know that I wouldn’t as soon she had died
that way as any other.”

As for Maxwell, in the midst of his own pain, in the midst of his own
agony, he could not get the thought of that dead animal out of his mind.

It was not because he had ever cared for her, as some men do care for
their horses; it was not that her wise, winning ways, her playful
tricks, her never-failing spirit had made him feel for her any real
affection; but he had been proud of her, vain of her. She had been his,
and she was the best mare in the county; and above and beyond all this,
there seemed to Maxwell something more than mere accident about the
whole affair.

Like many men who are utterly irreligious, he was superstitious; and,
lying there with his eyes shut, his mind was busy, piecing, in a kind of
delirium, past, present, and future into a connected whole.

He saw Brian bidding his farewell to that mare the night he left
Kincorth and went out into the world. He saw Harold taking that
desperate leap at the Headlands; and he remembered, at the same time,
that Harold was dead. He had heard that morning of Mrs. Drewitt’s death;
he had been asked by Ryan to act justly at last by his uncle’s living
children—and he refused. Ryan had threatened him; and he in return
stabbed the lawyer in his tenderest point.

He had ridden out on Harold’s mare; and now Madcap was dead, too; while
he was being carried, though he did not know it, straight as the mare
who bore him could go to Durrow.

The same thing, or worse, might have happened, no matter on what horse
he had been mounted; but, as the “wicked flee when no man pursueth,” so
the wicked likewise are apt to be superstitious about circumstances to
which better men would attach no importance; and the fever, which was
already upon him, made Maxwell look at all things with a fearful and
troubled mind.

He thought of what Ryan had said about Mrs. Drewitt; and he wondered if,
when he was alone, she would come and stand at the foot of his bed, too.

How many more times was Harold going to take that hedge?—how often was
his uncle’s head to drop forward on Brian’s breast? He knew they were
carrying him to Kincorth; for he could see Kathleen standing at the
hall-door; and Jenny, too—Jenny, with a child in her arms; he and she
were wandering through the glen. They could not get the cover of the
will opened—the seals would not break. They need not mind—he could tell
them what was in the codicil—he had got Ryan to add it. Yes, and he
could let them into a secret about what would make ink look faded! What
was that? Was the house falling? He was going up in the air—up—up—up;
and then his mind ceased to wander—for he fainted.

 CHAPTER XVI.
 After long Years.


Ryan stood in the spot where Maxwell had left him until the riders were
all gone—until the last red coat, the last groom, the last whipper-in,
had disappeared from view; then, like a man rousing him from a dream, he
moved slowly away, and took the hill-path towards Durrow.

Most men have some sense of honour—some point where, if you cut down
deep enough, blood will spout out; and a woman’s virtue was that
sensitive place in Timothy Ryan.

To him it seemed that a woman should be pure as snow—that for her fame
to be breathed on was for it to be sullied. He had his own ideal of what
the sex should be: and that ideal was very lofty and very beautiful.
Pretty well he knew what men were; he knew more of their bad than of
their good, perhaps; but women!

The angels were not purer or more holy than the generality of women, in
Ryan’s estimation; and one of the women whom he had loved, honoured, and
adored, for many a year past, was his own sister, of whom Maxwell had
asserted such things as made his very heart stand still.

But he did not believe it—he would not believe it unless her own lips
condemned her. While he walked across the hills he kept saying this to
himself, and then he would hurry on for a few steps, only again to stop
and linger—linger by the way.

He sat down on blocks of granite, and pulled the grass and heather up by
the roots, while he thought the matter over, till he went almost mad.

His own life had not been peculiarly honest or honourable. He had done
many a dirty trick; he had helped Maxwell Drewitt to serve many a man a
mean turn; he had not been scrupulous in the way of business; he had
lent a hand at forging the codicil; he had given, or, rather, allowed
Maxwell to take the second will.

He had not been at all nice as to what filth he put his hand into, and
he had not felt many qualms of conscience in his life; but now, behold,
there was a spot on his sister’s robe, and he could not bear it; could
not endure to think she had deceived him and her husband, and—and
everybody, thought the poor fellow.

“I will not believe it,” he ended. “I will go on; I will ask her about
the matter, and then I will return to Kincorth and cram his words down
his lying throat if there were a hundred people with him; for I will not
believe Jenny could be bad—I will not—I will not.”

It was easy enough to say this—easy enough to repeat the words over and
over, over and over again—but, alas! the more he repeated them the less
he believed them; the more surely conviction stole over his heart that
Jenny had once fallen from her high estate; that Jenny was not pure, not
innocent, not what he had fondly imagined his sister, and Connor’s wife,
and the mother of her children, to be—a woman as much above fear as
beyond reproach.

Onwards towards Durrow, with the mountains to his right hand and the
Atlantic to his left. But Ryan never looked either to right or left;
never looked, that is, noticing the hills or the sea. His heart was full
to bursting. He had been a bad man, and an unscrupulous, and now the
hour of reckoning was at hand. His health was ruined by drinking, his
nerves were shattered, he had been unhinged by hearing of Mrs. Drewitt’s
death, and now it seemed to him as though sorrows were multiplying upon
him, while he paused at intervals, and asked himself—

“What if it be true—what if he did not speak falsely after all?”

Mrs. Connor was alone, and sitting at work in one of the upper rooms,
when her brother entered. He was a frequent visitor, so Jenny did not
attach any importance to his coming till he had been seated for a few
minutes, when, noticing the anxious, troubled expression of his face,
she asked him what was the matter, whether anything was amiss.

“There is a good deal amiss, Jenny, that I want to talk with you about.
Is Dennis out?”

“Yes, he has gone to Duranmore, I think. What is wrong, Timothy? Can I
be of any use to you? Is it money? Sure Dennis is always willing to lend
to you when he has it. What is wrong? Tell me.”

She had laid down her work, and was close beside him. She was a woman
who never could grow old—who had such gentle, clinging ways, such
loving, winning manners, that she was always to Ryan the young sister,
the sweet Jenny whom he had once trembled might go off with Maxwell.

Once! what was that dread to this?

“It is not money, Jenny,” he answered. “I wish to God it were! I have
got many a thing to say to you. Go back to your chair, dear, and sit
down. Let me see, where was I?”

“Going to tell me about this trouble that has come to you,” she said. It
never struck her it could be any trouble relating to her; she had been
safe for so long. There were none alive that knew her secret who would
reveal it; through the years it had never oozed out, and she did not
imagine that after long years her sin could be brought to her
remembrance by any human being.

“I have bad news, Jenny,” he began, after a moment’s hesitation. She sat
with her hands folded together in her lap, her eyes fixed on his face,
her lips a little parted, and he felt he could not plunge into _that_
question without leading up to it in some indirect manner. “I have bad
news. Mrs. Drewitt is dead!”

“Dead!” Mrs. Connor repeated, and the slight colour faded out of her
cheeks, and her eyes brimmed full of tears. “I am very sorry to hear it.
She was a nice lady.”

“And hardly done by.”

“I am afraid she was. My heart was sore for her that night she came here
after Mr. Drewitt’s funeral. How did you hear about it, Timothy? did Mr.
