Brian write to Mr. Maxwell?”

“Mr. Brian will never write to Mr. Maxwell, and small blame to him for
it. No, it was Mr. Murphy wrote to me, whatever possessed him—Murphy
that used to be with Sheen, I mean, who was so sweet on you. He’s a
great man now, I believe. Somebody told me he was making two or three
thousand a-year; but maybe it is not true.”

“She did not stay long after Mr. Harold. I wonder if it was the fretting
killed her.”

“I don’t know, I am sure—likely as not, for she worshipped the ground he
walked on. He was a fine lad.”

“He was,” answered Jenny, simply; “but I was fondest of Mr. Brian.”

“Did you ever see anybody Harold favoured?” asked Ryan, suddenly.

“Never. Mr. Pryor always said he was the image of his cousin. I never
could see it, though.”

“Mr. Pryor was right; but there is another person who is more like
Harold than his cousin ever was; that is, another person who must have
been like Harold when he was Harold’s age.”

“Who is that?”

“Mr. Campnell, the young gentleman who was at Kincorth last autumn.”

“I never saw him.” But the colour came back into her cheek, and her eyes
drooped under the look of eager, anxious inquiry her brother fixed upon
her.

“Say that again, Jenny; say you never saw him.”

“I never saw him,” she repeated.

“Is it the truth you are telling me, or is it a lie?” He rose in his
suspense and misery, and stood directly facing her.

“Why should I tell you a lie about the matter?” she asked. “What can Mr.
Campnell be to me that I should fear to speak the truth? I never set
eyes on him.”

“And yet Maxwell Drewitt told me, not two hours ago, you were his
mother?”

The work she had taken up again dropped from her fingers; she lifted her
face to her brother’s with a cry of helpless despair. Against any other
testimony she might have maintained her innocence; but it was useless to
attempt any further denial. Against him—false first and last—false as
cruel—deceitful as relentless—there was no appeal.

“You need not speak, Jenny,” he said; “you need not rack your brain to
think what other falsehood you can coin to try and deceive me. I see it
is all true. Oh! Jenny, Jenny.”

She did not say a word—she could not say a word—she was so stunned, so
confused, so wretched, that she could do nothing but sit and wonder if
the misery of the past would all have to be endured over again; if she
had not suffered enough then, that this fresh disgrace was come to
her—that this blow had been dealt to her by _him_.

“I suppose this was the reason you would not marry for so long. I
suppose this was what made you refuse offer after offer, and live on
single till I’m sure I thought you never were going to be a wife at all.
But though you deceived me, Jenny, I hope you did not deceive him. I
hope you never let any honest man take you as you were, ignorantly.”

She put out her hands, and there came such a look of horror into her
white face as made Ryan turn round to see what could have caused it.

Behind him stood Connor, who, having heard the last part of his
brother-in-law’s sentence, asked hoarsely what they were talking
about—what it all meant.

“It means, I ought never to have married you, Dennis,” she broke out
passionately. “It means, I am all he says, and worse. It means, that
years, and years, and years ago I sinned, and that now my sin has found
me out.”

“What is it?” he repeated. “I do not understand it at all. Are you both
mad? What is it, Tim? Tell me what she says.”

“She says she is not what we both took her to be,” answered Ryan,
bitterly. “She confesses—that—that boy Maxwell Drewitt has ‘adopted’ is
her son, and his son, and their son.”

“And God knows, Dennis, that many a time I would have given anything to
have been able to tell you this—that when you have been telling me how
happy—” the poor creature’s voice was choked with sobs—“how happy you
were, I felt my heart turn cold, thinking about my fault. I did not want
to marry you, Dennis; you know I didn’t. And I have been true and
faithful to you since; and I was so young, and I am the mother of your
children, and you have had every thought of my heart, and every bit of
my love, since ever you took me home. Won’t you forgive, Dennis?—sure
you couldn’t cast me off?”

She stretched out her arms towards him, but he made no step forward to
take her to him. He averted his face that he might not look at the woman
who had deceived him; who had lain in his bosom, and lived with him
through the years and borne him children, knowing what she knew about
herself all the time.

“Dennis!” and she was going to fling herself at his feet, but her
brother prevented her.

“Connor,” he said, holding her back, and standing resolutely between
them, “I don’t ask you—I can’t ask you to keep her. She has brought
shame and disgrace to both of us. She is no more to you now than if she
had never been your wife; but she is my own sister just the same as
ever; so I will e’en take her with me;” and suiting the action to the
word, Ryan drew her arm within his and led her, spite of her struggles
and remonstrances towards the door.

It was stern justice, stern and sharp, and Jenny rebelled against it.

“Let me go back to him,” she said; “don’t try to take me from him, Tim.
He is not that hard—he will listen to me, and forgive me.”

They had reached the door by this time, and one of her children, her
youngest, came running along the passage, calling out “Mammy—Mammy.”

At the words she fought to free herself, and succeeding, caught the
little fellow to her heart and covered his face with kisses.

Very resolutely, Ryan took the boy from her and setting him down, said—

“Run away, Johnny, and play yourself; run away, you have no mother now;”
and he drew his sister’s arm in his once more and walked on towards the
head of the staircase.

This time Jenny made no effort to free herself; no struggle to return.
With the full sense of her dishonour brought home by that one solitary
sentence, she staggered forward, weeping bitter tears for her shame and
their disgrace.

Weeping for a stain that the floods of Niagara could not have washed
away; for hearts broken; for sin discovered; for confidence abused; for
love betrayed!

So they descended the stairs together; she crying audibly; he weeping
tears not to be noticed of men, but seen of God—tears not of brine from
his eyes, but of blood from his heart.

So, clasping her hand closer and tighter, he guided her down step by
step: he who had never forsaken her in joy or in sorrow—who was true to
her in bad report as in good—who, reckless and unscrupulous and
unprincipled though he might be in most things—who, fallen though he
knew her to be, was her faithful brother still.

Step by step they descended that broad staircase; but when they reached
the bottom, a hoarse voice called after them to stop; and Connor,
running down the flight, laid his hand on Jenny’s shoulder.

“Give me my wife!” he said; “she has been a good wife to me, and you
shall not take her from me. Jenny, haven’t you loved me? haven’t you
been true to me since we came together? Only say that, and I won’t mind
the rest. I’ll try to forget it, Jenny—I’ll think it was a bad dream.
There—there—darling; don’t fret. I was stunned like for the minute; but
I could not have let you go; no, I couldn’t, dear, I couldn’t.”

Her arms were about his neck; her head was resting on his shoulder; her
tears were falling like rain; and she was striving to articulate some
words of thankfulness and blessing, when there was a trampling over the
ground outside of many horses’ hoofs, and of many men’s feet; then a
loud knocking; and then followed a scene of indescribable confusion.

“It was nearer to bring him here than take him to Kincorth. Shall we
carry him upstairs? He is not dead, though he is badly hurt. Who is it,
d’ye say? It is Mr. Drewitt; who else? He was thrown just on the other
side Durrow River; and the chesnut mare is killed.”

“God help us!” exclaimed Connor.

“And we have sent for the doctor; and there’s a heap of gentlemen
outside there; and shall we take him upstairs, or will you make him up a
bed down here? That would be best, if you could do it.”

“Where is he hurt?”

“Sorra a one of me knows,” answered the man addressed; “he groaned that
bad at first, I am sure you might have heard him up here; but he’s
fainted now, I think. Likely as not he won’t get over it.”

By this time several of the gentlemen had alighted and entered the
house. From them Connor learned the particulars of the accident.

“The rest are gone after the fox,” said Mr. Frost; “but I have have had
enough hunting for to-day; and so I think have my friends here. I never
was more upset in my life.”

Whereupon Mr. Connor suggested that a little whiskey might not be
unacceptable; or “some wine,” he added. “Mr. Pryor’s Madeira is very
good, I’m told.”

“Let us have two or three bottles up, then. I should like to hear the
doctor’s opinion of Mr. Drewitt. I have been thinking since, that we
ought perhaps to have taken him to Kincorth; for I suspect he won’t be
able to move from here for many and many a day—unless, indeed, he is
moved away in his coffin.”

“Well, sir, we must only do our best for him, if he does stay,” answered
Connor, who could not, at such a time, remember his own injuries, or
nurse his own anger.

Sickness, like death, is a great soother of human passions; and Maxwell
Drewitt’s state was for a long time sufficiently critical to render it
doubtful whether he should ever leave Durrow again, except for his
grave.

Day followed day—week succeeded to week—and month to month—but still
Maxwell was unable to leave his bed. What he thought of, as he lay
there, weak and faint and helpless, who can tell?—not even Jenny could
imagine—though, judging by the words he let fall in his delirium, his
reveries could scarcely, during convalescence, have been pleasant.

All through that illness he was nursed and tended by the people he had
injured—by Ryan and Connor and Jenny; and that he ever recovered
sufficiently to leave Durrow and return to Kincorth, was owing more to
their care and attention than to any other cause.

But Maxwell was not grateful for their kindness. I think he hated them
all bitterly for thus heaping coals of fire upon his head. I believe he
could have struck Ryan when he told him how he had kept forwarding money
to Mr. Campnell, at that gentleman’s request, without waiting for Mr.
Drewitt’s recovery, or troubling him on the subject till he should
regain his strength.

“And in a letter I had from him the other day,” proceeded the lawyer,
“he says he wishes you would reconsider your decision and purchase him a
commission, as he should prefer a soldier’s life to any other.”

“If he choose a soldier’s life he must go as a private, and never look
for a shilling from me,” retorted Mr. Drewitt. “The boy is a fool, and
he must be brought to his senses.”

 CHAPTER XVII.
 The Beginning of the End.


Once again the summer sun was shining over Kincorth—brightly as on the
day this story opened—and under the shadow of the overarching trees
Maxwell Drewitt stood gazing up at the house just as he had done
twenty-five years previously.

He was then three-and-twenty; he was now eight-and-forty: he was then
full of youth and health and strength; he was now older than his age, a
man stricken and feeble, who might never have a vigorous body or a sound
constitution again. That fall, though it had failed to kill, had yet
injured him permanently. He could know no more what it was to be free
from ache and pain, to care nothing about the weather, to be insensible
to fatigue, to have an iron frame, an indomitable spirit.

A minute—less than a minute—had sufficed to make him a perfect wreck; a
worn, sickly man, who, having formed no sweet home-ties for himself in
the days of his strength, was now, in the time of his weakness, lonely
and desolate.

Upon him the sunlight that streamed through the branches of the trees
fell brightly, as they had fallen brightly, if you remember,
five-and-twenty years before. He was starting on life’s journey then;
that journey was nearly over now.

He had planned then, he had hoped. The plans were now realities, the
hopes certainties. He had longed to possess, and Kincorth was his. He
had determined to be wealthy, and behold he had gold at his command. He
had resolved to make the name of Drewitt notable, and the name of
Drewitt had become remarkable. He had declared he would make the estate
valuable, the property something to talk about, and in the length and
breadth of Connemara there were not two lovelier places than Kincorth
and Headlands. He had made up his mind to have his revenge, and uncle
and aunt and youngest-born were all gone to their graves, poor, and
stricken, and sorrowful, while Brian was a clerk in London—Brian, who
ought to have had Kincorth.

If success be happiness, Maxwell Drewitt should have been a man without
a care; but the best-mounted horseman has as often that imp sitting
behind him on the crupper as the poorest man riding on the shaggiest
pony. Care, as a rule, indeed, loves high places, and care never left
Maxwell Drewitt’s side now by day or night.

Care for his own life; care for the man who was to come after him; care
lest the winds of heaven might blow upon his own cheek too roughly; lest
Arthur, the only thing he had on earth belonging to him, should go
hopelessly astray.

He was doing no good at college; he was spending money as though he had
an income of a thousand pounds a week; he was wild, he was reckless, he
was intractable; and yet Maxwell, in his own fashion, loved him, perhaps
on the same principle that a prisoner loves a rat; namely, because he
had nothing else to care for.

And the youth knew this, and traded on it; winding the man who had never
been weak before round his finger as easily as Maxwell had wound others
in the days gone by.

Of his son and of his health, Maxwell Drewitt was thinking while he
stood under the trees, looking up at Kincorth. He felt that for him,
life was over—life with its purposes, its hopes, its successes; and
occasionally he could not help asking himself how it would be when not
only the life of work was over, but the bare life—the living and
breathing that would, he knew, continue after every capacity for
exertion, every power of action had been taken from him.

House to house, acre to acre, property to property! for this end—to be
standing with the best part of his existence—health—taken away from him;
thinking in solitude of that unknown world concerning which the clergy
preached continually; the secrets of which not one of the departed had
ever returned to reveal.

The next world! There is a something very terrible to a man like Maxwell
Drewitt in the idea of leaving all he has most enjoyed, most coveted,
most valued, and going away to the cold and silent grave. He could not
bear it; he shrank from the reality of death; he turned away from the
grim spectre; he fled from the unwelcome thoughts that would intrude
themselves upon him. He could not bear to be alone; he would have
anybody to sit with him rather than remain in solitude. He wished he had
not quarrelled with his wife, and made overtures of reconciliation to
her; but Lady Emmeline, now resident in London, refused his offers, and
treated his advances with indifference and coldness.

He could not get Ryan to come and chat with him, and the evenings were
frightful. He had no strength to endure the racket of company, and it
was not likely that the men he knew would take pity on an invalid, and
bear with his temper in sickness as they had borne with it in health.

The rector would have come; but Maxwell, after one or two trials,
abandoned that experiment.

He thought of going away, but he did not know where to go, and, besides,
it was in his nature to cling to his home and his country—to remain in
the midst of the scenery he knew so well rather than take up his abode
in the midst of a great city. He wished he had some one to stay always
with him, always to speak to, always to have at his elbow, whom he could
tell to drive away that demon who always would persist in reminding him
that the days were passing—passing; that there must come an hour when
soul and body would have to be parted—when time would be the shadow, and
eternity the substance.

If he had even Wilhelmina; but Wilhelmina and her husband and her
children had sailed a year before for America. Susan was in the
country—that is, in England; but then he had always hated Susan, and he
had quarrelled fearfully with Captain, now Colonel, Ellenham about her
fortune. When that individual, who had long been a widower, discovered
that under Mr. Drewitt’s will Susan was entitled to a small fortune, he
married her forthwith; and over the matter of that fortune he and
Maxwell had exchanged such letters as few people have inclination to
send, or leisure to pen.

No, he had not a soul excepting his son; and his son would not consent
to take up his abode permanently in Connemara—not, at any rate, until he
had seen the world. He wanted to be an officer—he would be an officer;
his father must purchase him a commission.

In his very last letter he repeated this statement, and expressed a wish
that a remittance might be forwarded to him immediately.

“I must stop the supplies,” Mr. Drewitt decided, as he walked away from
under the trees, and retraced his steps towards the house slowly and
painfully. “Yes, I must stop the supplies; that will bring him to his
senses.”

The next letter from his son announced that he had gone to London, and
begged again for money. In reply, Maxwell referred him to his former
letter, and desired him to return home.

During the week, Arthur wrote once more, and intimated that if “the
needful,” as he called it, were not forthcoming from Connemara, he must
get it elsewhere, and in some mode which might be disagreeable to his
father.

The tone of the correspondence was, by this time, waxing warm, and
accordingly Mr. Drewitt answered—

“That for Arthur’s own sake, he hoped he would get it honestly.”

After that there came a lull; for a month there was not a line from this
unrepentant prodigal, and Maxwell was getting most seriously anxious
about his black sheep, when one morning the post brought two pleasant
pieces of intelligence to him at the same time: news that his son had
enlisted and that the mutiny in India had broken out.

Arthur was very penitent, very submissive; he had seen quite enough of
soldiering to satisfy him. He wanted to be bought off. If his father
would but buy him off, he would do everything he required. He would
settle in Connemara, and never ask to stir outside the gates of
Kincorth, unless his father permitted him to do so. He would make it the
study of his life to obey and please his parent; he would thankfully
accept any conditions his father chose to impose. He confessed he had
been a bad son; that considering his birth, he had not estimated Mr.
Drewitt’s kindness at its proper value, and he wound up by imploring his
father to get him out of the service somehow.

“I have got into, perhaps, the very worst regiment I could have done,”
he finished, “and our colonel is one of the most detestable men, they
tell me, in the whole of the British army. His name is Ellenham.

 “Your affectionate son.”


Maxwell dropped the letter on the carpet. Were his troubles to have no
end? Had he been prosperous for so long only that the tide might turn at
last, and bring nothing but sorrow to him on its waves?

What had he done, that in middle life he should be stricken down in the
pride of his health and strength?

Wherein did he now differ from the Maxwell of former years—that
everything he touched in those days should have gone well with him—while
nothing that he now desired would come to pass—nothing?

In a moment he had grasped all the facts of the case—in a moment he
realized the complicated difficulties of his position. He knew, if
Colonel Ellenham guessed Arthur to be his son, that he would throw such
obstacles in the way of purchasing his discharge as might make that
discharge impossible to obtain. He was well aware how much the war in
India would increase his embarrassment; and he sat on, pondering and
pondering what he should do—how it would be best for him to proceed,
while his son’s letter lay on the carpet and the newspaper was spread
out before him.

Look at him now, reader. Would you know him? Remembering what he was
when this story opened, would you have imagined it within the bounds of
probability that—long before old age had laid its hand upon him—long
before winter’s withering frosts had come to pick the green leaves off
the goodly tree—to dry up its sap, to strip it of its beauty, and to
leave it bare and destitute of foliage, almost in the summer of his
life—he could have changed to this?

Sitting alone there, the terrible ravages sickness and pain had made
were plainly to be traced; and, with Maxwell Drewitt, when once his
physical beauty was destroyed, everything was destroyed; and he who had
once been so haughty—so insolent—so handsome—was now but as any other
bowed and beaten man.

And in that hour he was humbling himself mentally—he was bending his
proud spirit to ask a favour from a man whom he detested. He only knew
one person in London who would, he felt sure, work for his son’s release
heart and soul—and that person was Geoffry Pryor.

Yet Geoffry Pryor had read him through in the days gone by; and, reading
him, had refused his proffered hand, and never grasped it since.

Nevertheless, it was to him Maxwell Drewitt made up his mind to apply.

“I will send Ryan to Dublin by this night’s mail,” he decided; “and he
can cross to Holyhead to-morrow morning.”

Sickness had not dulled the keen brain, though it had wasted and worn
his body. He could think rapidly, though it was pain and misery to him
to move swiftly. He could still chalk out a plan, and make others act
upon it, though the days were gone for ever when he could execute his
designs himself and be dependent on no other man living to see that they
were carried out to his satisfaction.

“Do you think I had better follow you?” he inquired; but Ryan answered,
“No. I am certain, if any person can get him off, Mr. Pryor will find
that man; and you will only knock yourself up by going travelling in
such hot weather as this. No; you keep yourself quiet, and rely on us. I
will do my best—if only for _her_ sake—be sure of that.”

Yes, they were all rowing in the same boat now. Ryan could never
disassociate his interests from Maxwell, nor Maxwell his from Ryan in
the future for ever.

The comparison may not seem complimentary to the married state; but in
one respect guilt is very like matrimony. Whether people like or hate,
they must endure each his fellow’s company when once they have bound
themselves together in the bonds of sin.

It is as difficult for rogues to get a divorce as for husband and wife;
and accordingly what Maxwell and Ryan had been in the beginning, so they
continued to the end.

“I had better ride to Galway, I suppose,” Ryan observed; “there is no
dependence to be placed on those post-horses.”

“Nonsense,” answered his employer. “The mail-car can take you as safely
as the letters, I should imagine.”

“I won’t risk it,” returned Ryan; “there might not he a vacant seat.”

“And how about your luggage?”

“The car can bring a carpet-bag after me; there is sure to be room for
that.”

“One word more, Ryan. Don’t let him out of your sight; bring him
straight back with you here, till we try if we cannot make something of
him. I wish to heaven he was as wise as he is handsome!”

“He’ll never be wise, I’m right sure of that,” was the reply; “but I
will do what you tell me. I will bring him back to you.” And with that
Ryan left the room, while Maxwell remained fuming over his last words.

“What right had any man to say his son would never be wise? Was not he
clever enough? was not he young? If he had gone wrong once, what of
that? He should be a credit and a comfort to him yet. Once let him
(Maxwell) have him back again in Connemara, and he would look him out a
wife who should keep him in order, and give him an interest in the
county.

“Many a girl would snap at such a chance, and he, Maxwell, could take
the opportunity of making a proviso that his heir should assume the name
of Drewitt.

“It would be pleasant for him to have his son and his son’s wife living
in the house, and the chatter of children’s tongues and the patter of
children’s feet might yet break the funeral silence of the empty rooms,
by the doors of which he dreaded to pass.”

Always this man had longed for children, and now, with a more terrible
longing than ever, he desired to hear young voices ringing in his
ears—to have something lively and bright to chase away the gloom that
was enveloping him.

With all his heart and with all his soul he wished he had brought Arthur
home sooner; that he had insisted years and years before on having him
at the Headlands, instead of leaving him among strangers.

He recollected why he had not done so. How he had calculated on Lady
Emmeline’s death, and on his second marriage, and on the chances of
legitimate issue.

The scroll of his life could not have been pleasant reading in the days
of which I am now writing, and it was little marvel that Maxwell Drewitt
opened that manuscript as seldom as possible.

Rather he preferred to dream dreams, and see visions; to picture to
himself the young wife who would make the old house seem home-like and
cheerful, the children who might gambol on the lawn.

Dreams—dreams—sad and pitiful to think that they could never be
realized! visions so bright that when the disillusion came the man whose
life was almost over could not bear the blow!

It was on himself he had built before; it was on another he was building
now: that made all the difference. He had been always able to tell what
course he should pursue; but the course his son might adopt was darkness
to him.

One of the children of this world, he had been wise in his generation;
one of the fools of this world, there was no counting upon what Arthur
might do from day to day.

And what he finally did was this:—

While Mr. Pryor, after vainly trying to come to some arrangement with
Colonel Ellenham, was running after one great man after another, to try
to procure Arthur’s discharge, that young gentleman, wearied of the
ill-success attendant on his friend’s efforts, coolly did the most
foolish thing possible under the circumstances, namely, took the matter
into his own hands and deserted.

“That I ever should have been father to such an idiot!” exclaimed
Maxwell Drewitt when the news reached him. “There is no hope for him
now, unless we can discover where he is and get him out of the country.”

And even, in fancy, he never again heard the chatter of children’s
tongues or the patter of children’s feet; the lonely rooms he could fill
no more, even in dreams, with light and life and merriment.

Deeper and darker grew the night, lonelier and more desolate grew the
man—sad and pitiful! sinner that he had been—for the days were come at
last when he said he had no pleasure in them. He had thought to live
long, and to have much enjoyment, and behold he found out in a moment
that what Solomon said was true: “Man knoweth not his time: as the
fishes are taken in an evil net, and as the birds that are caught in the
snare, so are the sons of men snared in an evil time when it falleth
suddenly upon them.”

 CHAPTER XVIII.
 Man’s Vengeance.


The world had prospered with Brian Drewitt since his mother’s death; his
salary was still indeed not excessive, but he was promised an increase
very shortly, and Mr. Francis Gyton had further thrown out many vague
hints to the effect that in his, Frank Gyton’s opinion, it would be far
jollier if they (he and Brian) were to cut the old connection, and start
in business on their own account.

“I know I could not stand Munday, so there is no use in my father
talking of taking me into partnership,” he said one evening when he and
Brian were walking up westward together from the City. “I think such a
lot of partners are a damned nuisance, and I should like to get clear of
them. So, to cut the matter short, Brian, whenever I am married I should
like to commence with you. I can find the tin, and you the brains—is it
a bargain?”

“Your father?” hesitated Brian.

“My father will be a little restive at first, but when he is tired of
opposition he will give in, and it is of no use mincing the matter,
Brian; Mr. Munday and I cannot remain in the same business together.
What do you say—will you be my man, or will you not?”

“I will not, without speaking first to your father in the matter. He has
been very kind to me, and—”

“And you will do the respectful and all the rest of it, and I will do
the dutiful; so that, between us, the old gentleman will be as easily
managed as possible. I should not wonder if he came over to us himself,
for he likes Munday no better than I do.”

Mrs. Drewitt’s death had of course postponed Geraldine’s marriage, and
as it had also crippled their means, brother and sister were now living
in a cottage at Highgate, which Mr. Gyton entertained some idea of
purchasing for his son.

“Throw out a drawing-room there, and build a conservatory here, and put
the kitchens at the back, and it would really make a very charming
little residence. Love in a cottage, eh, Geraldine?”

Whereupon Geraldine blushed and laughed, while the evening sunshine fell
upon her.

“And you will require coach-house and stabling; in fact, the whole place
will need alteration; but it is a lovely situation—lovely!” and the
great man stood back to admire the view. “I tell you what it is, Miss,”
he went on; “I shall get so fond of this cottage one of these days, that
you will have me taking up my abode here,” and he pinched the girl’s
cheek, who, passing her hand through his arm, said coaxingly—

“Something has taken up its abode here that would astonish you. I wonder
if I might tell you, or whether you would be shocked. It is a deserter,
a countryman of my own.”

“A deserter?—you are jesting, Geraldine.”

“I am not jesting at all,” she answered. “Our servant found him in the
wood-shed, this morning, and he is half-starved, and badly clad, and ill
and miserable.”

“Good bless me!” exclaimed Mr. Gyton.

“And I want your help to send him over to America, or Newfoundland, or
some place,” added Geraldine, with that charming ambiguity of expression
which is so pleasing a characteristic of her sex.

“But—but—but all this is very illegal; extremely improper,” began Mr.
Gyton.

“We don’t care for law at all in Ireland,” answered Geraldine, demurely;
“and in such a case as this, not much for propriety.”

“You incorrigible little girl, I don’t know what I must say to you.”

“Say you will pay his passage out to America,” she suggested. “If I had
the money, I would do it in a minute.”

“But it is against law: the proper course for you to pursue, would be to
go down to Scotland Yard, and give information that such and such a
person was concealed on your premises.”

Geraldine broke out into a peal of laughter. “Should you really like me
to go to Scotland Yard, Mr. Gyton?” she suggested. “Would a note not do
as well, or could Frank not call round in the morning? Is there any
reward? I think I should like to get a reward.”

“What are you talking about?” demanded Lady Emmeline, who was likewise
of the party. “I declare, any person might think it was you and Mr.
Gyton who were engaged, instead of you and Frank.”

“So we are engaged for life, as father and daughter,” replied the
millionaire; “we were talking about a deserter. Geraldine, it appears,
keeps one among her coals and wood.”

“You are not serious?”

“Perfectly serious. I dare say the young man is on view at the present
moment.”

“Dear me! how romantic!”

“It is romantic,” added Geraldine; “for he is evidently a gentleman; he
has been to Oxford, at the University, I mean—a student there, or
whatever you call it.”

“I think, Mr. Gyton, that this affair ought to be investigated,” said
Lady Emmeline.

“I think so, too,” was the reply. “Suppose we send for a policeman?”

“If a policeman come here I will smash his skull for him!” remarked
Brian, at this juncture.

“Dear! dear! what a set of people you Irish are!” observed Mr. Gyton. “A
man has only to shoot his landlord—or be a rebel—or a deserter—or
something of that kind, to engage all your sympathies!”

“You would not have us give him up?” exclaimed Brian, his voice sounding
loud and angry, as he spoke.

“Certainly not, sir. To an Englishman I might suggest such a course; but
to an Irishman—never. The proper thing for you to do, is to feed him,
clothe him, love him, and then send him over to America, to hate the
English, and say we are the cause of all Ireland’s misfortunes.”

For the life of him, Brian could not help laughing. The sketch was so
true—so real and so ready.

“Could not we get a peep at him?” asked Lady Emmeline.

“Certainly,” answered Brian; “at five pounds a head.”

“Is he worth the money, Lady Emmeline, do you consider?” inquired Mr.
Gyton.

“Now, how can one possibly tell that unless one sees him?” demanded Lady
Emmeline. “You really must allow us some discretion, Brian.”

“As much as you please,” Brian answered, “if you give me five pounds.”

“Is it as a charity?” inquired her ladyship.

“As a charity,” Brian replied, bowing low.

“Then there is my five pounds,” said Lady Emmeline, putting five
sovereigns into Brian’s hand.

“And five pounds for me and five for my wife,” added Mr. Gyton.

“You won’t charge me anything, Brian? I am one of the family,” said Mr.
Francis Gyton.

“And well able to afford to pay for the sight,” added Brian.

“What a leech you are! Won’t my I O U do as well? Won’t you trust me?
’Pon my honour, I have not five pounds about me.”

“I will pay for him,” said Geraldine, drawing out her purse; “but
remember, Frank, it is out of my housekeeping money; so you must give it
back to me honestly.”

And thus, amidst much laughing, they collected twenty pounds, on the
strength of which the deserter was brought by Brian into the little
parlour of Spring Lodge.

“Well, but really now, you know—joking apart, excuse me, but, my good
fellow, this is really very irregular,” remarked Mr. Gyton, senior.

“I suppose no one can know that better than he,” said Brian, looking at
the thin pale face, the hollow cheeks, the unshaved chin, the grisly
moustache, with an interest he could neither have explained nor defined.

“And where do you come from?” asked Frank Gyton.

“From Yorkshire,” was the reply; “that young lady (indicating Geraldine)
will have it that I am from Ireland, but she is mistaken.”

“No, I am not,” answered Geraldine. “No Englishman ever pronounced ‘have
it’—‘haf’t’—as you do.”

“What is your name?” inquired Mr. Gyton.

“Ward,” he said; “Charles Ward.”

“It is not,” exclaimed Lady Emmeline; “it is Campnell, and you are Mr.
Maxwell Drewitt’s illegitimate son.”

“Do you know him?” asked Brian, hurriedly.

“Know him? To be sure I do. Didn’t I see him in Connemara last year?
Have not you noticed the likeness between him and Harold?”

In a moment Brian comprehended what had attracted him about the
deserter. In an instant he formed his plans. The hour of vengeance was
striking at last, and it would be his own fault if he faltered in his
design.

Through Maxwell’s son he must hurt Maxwell; and soon after he rose, and
left the room.

While they were all talking together, speaking to Arthur Campnell,
asking him how he chanced to enlist, how he came to desert, Geraldine
left the room likewise. She searched all over the house, she made
inquiries of the servants, their own, and Mr. Gyton, and Lady
Emmeline’s. None knew where Brian had gone; but one man had seen him go
out of the gate, and walk down the hill.

“Tell Mr. Gyton I want him,” Geraldine said; “not Mr. Frank, but Mr.
Gyton.” And Mr. Gyton came out. “Brian is gone to give information,” she
said; “we must get him away, and at once.”

“Geraldine, you are dreaming,” exclaimed Mr. Gyton.

“Am I? You don’t know Brian as well as I do. So long as he thought that
man was simply a deserter, he would have died before letting a hair of
his head be touched; but now he knows him to be Maxwell’s son, he would
put a rope round his neck, and hang him.”

“God bless my soul! you don’t say no!”

“I do say so,” she answered; “and I say you know nothing of the Irish,
whose hate is as strong as their love. I noticed his face change, the
moment Lady Emmeline said who Arthur was, and I know he must be got away
from here without a moment’s delay.”

“But really, that is, you know——”

“Take him to Mr. Pryor; he will see to him—he will get him off,” said
Geraldine. And accordingly, greatly to his disgust, Mr. Gyton was
persuaded to drive off, with a deserter in his carriage personating
Frank.

“And now, Geoffry,” added Mr. Gyton, when he had finished his narrative,
“let me entreat of you to get him away as soon as possible. He is no
credit to the family; and one really cannot get through life
respectably, if deserters, and so forth, are to be hidden in our
coal-cellars and larders. Really, Mr. Maxwell Drewitt ought to have more
consideration,” he continued; but Mr. Pryor smiled.

“Maxwell Drewitt does not know what to do with this boy, whom he means
to make his heir. It is a lamentable business altogether; but, as you
say, there is nothing for it except to get him to Ireland, and out of
the country as soon as possible.”

“I did not think Brian would really have informed,” remarked Mr. Gyton.

“It would be hard to say what he might, or what he might not do, where
his cousin is concerned,” was the reply.

“And with regard to this youth?”

“Leave him to me.”

Meanwhile, there were high words waging at Highgate between Geraldine
and her brother.

She was accusing him of meanness, cowardice, vindictiveness.

He retorted by saying she was cold, insensible, heartless.

“Indifferent to our mother’s sufferings—to Harold’s disgrace.”

“Our mother, on her death-bed, said, that you were to forgive, as you
hoped to be forgiven,” answered Geraldine.

“You should not talk about what you do not understand, Geraldine,” he
said.

“I understood that well enough,” she replied; “and I know that long
letter she wrote to you was full of nothing else but regrets for ever
having encouraged your dislike of Maxwell, and not having checked your
desire for revenge.”

“She was an angel.”

“She was.”

“Who never could carry a feeling of resentment; and therefore it behoves
me—I tell you, Geraldine, it behoves me to remember her wrongs, and have
no mercy.”

“That is not what she would tell you, Brian, if she were alive. But
there is no use in our arguing this point out now, for Maxwell’s son is
now beyond the reach of your vindictiveness.”

“Are you quite satisfied of that?”

“Yes, I am. Oh, Brian! do not push this matter further. Think if it had
been Harold!—and he is so like Harold.”

“So was the lad who stole those notes.”

“Do you think it was he that took them?”

“Think!” he repeated, contemptuously; “I am sure of it. What is your
opinion of your protégé, now?”

There was a long pause, during the continuance of which Geraldine sat
weeping silently. At last, she said—

“Brian, do you imagine it would comfort Harold to know you were pursuing
that young man for his sake?”

“It comforts me,” he replied.

“Now, when he is gone where his innocence is known, what can it matter
to him who goes free on earth?”

“It matters to his memory, Geraldine.”

“And who cares for his memory? who but a few that, believing him
innocent in life, cannot doubt him in death?”

“We need not talk any further about the matter,” said Brian, angrily. “I
am doing what I consider right; and no one shall convince me to the
contrary.”

“I think I could,” said Mr. Pryor, who, late though the hour was,
entered at the moment. “Brian, you have done very wrong!”

“I have not!” was the answer.

“I told you yourself, years ago, you were preparing a bitter cup; but
you would not listen to my warning. That youth was safe here. No one
would have suspected his being concealed in such a house as this; and we
could easily have shipped him off from here to some place of safety.
Now, I candidly say, I do not know what we are to do. Every plan seems
beset with danger and difficulty!”

“I am glad to hear it,” remarked Brian, sullenly.

“No, Brian, you are not. Vindictive though you may be, you could not in
cool blood—you could not, I am sure—give that boy over to be flogged and
branded!”

“I could! believing, as I do, that his father will feel every lash and
every touch of the iron through him.”

“And his mother, Brian?”

“The mother of such a son must be as bad as his father.”

“She is not! I know her well; and I know she is not! Shall I tell you
her name, Brian?”

“It can have no interest for me,” he answered; “but you may tell me, if
you like.”

“Mrs. Connor!”

“No!” Brian started to his feet. “No! I tell you it is not—it shall not
be!”

“How can you prevent it? the fact is so. Mrs. Connor, when she was Jenny
Bourke—young, pretty, guileless—brought that boy into the world, whom
you have tried your best this night, Brian, to give up to trial and
punishment.”

“Mr. Pryor, God is my witness! I thought I was only doing my duty!”

“Duty toward whom?”

“To my mother’s memory—to Harold’s! For myself and Geraldine, I do not
care—I never have cared; but no soul can give _them_ any comfort or
happiness here. And I vowed—I swore—if ever I could pay Maxwell back any
portion of the suffering he had caused them, I would do so.”

“Did not your mother leave some parting message for you—some words that
must have sounded as though uttered from her coffin?” asked Mr. Pryor.

“She did,” was the reply; “but though she might forgive for herself, I
did not think myself bound to comply with her request and forgive for
her. So long as we are in the flesh we must act like men; and it is not
like a man to forgive and forget such injuries as Maxwell heaped on us.”

“Brian, do you think it is our affair to manage the world—to avenge
wrongs, to requite the virtuous and punish the wicked?”

“How can I tell?” he answered. “I suppose we are instruments; and when
one sees a man like Maxwell prosperous, happy, respected, what is one to
do—how is one to act?”

“Not as you have done, at any rate; we are all so mixed up here, that if
we strike a blow we never can tell whose heart may feel the wound most
severely. As in this case, for instance, you never guessed Mrs. Connor
could be mixed up in such a matter.”

“Well, the young fellow is gone,” answered Brian, impatiently; “he is
not caught, I suppose; and wherever Geraldine sent him, I conclude he is
safe enough.”

“Is he? there were inquiries at my house not half an hour ago, and if I
had not been quicker than they suspected, the boy would have been now on
his way back to barracks.”

Brian made no answer; he sat for a long time silent, for many minutes he
never spoke one word; then he rose and left the room in silence.

“He is sorry,” Geraldine remarked; and Geraldine was right. Brian was
gone away, to mourn in solitude; not that he had tried to punish
Maxwell’s son, but that Maxwell’s son was also Jenny’s boy—poor Jenny,
to whom he had once vowed to be grateful for life.

Next morning Ryan and his nephew started for Ireland; proceeding
thither, not by the Dublin and Holyhead route, but running down by the
Great Western to Milford Haven, whence they crossed to Waterford; from
which place Mr. Ryan meant to work their way by all manner of curious
routes to Connemara.

“Once there we shall be safe,” thought Mr. Ryan; but he reckoned without
his host.

“I wonder if they have reached Kincorth?” Mr. Pryor remarked, the day
but one after their departure, to Brian, who had never opened his lips
on the subject from the night he discovered who the deserter really was
until that present moment.

“If who have reached Kincorth?” asked the younger man.

“Ryan and his nephew.”

“They have not gone to Ireland, surely—they have not been so mad as
that?” exclaimed Brian, all in a hurry and tremble.

“Why not? it seemed to me a much safer place than London.”

“But I told them whose son he was—they are certain to be on the
look-out. Why did you say nothing to me—oh! why did not you?”

They were walking down the Holloway Road at the moment, and Mr. Pryor,
without answering Brian by a word, called a passing Hansom, and jumped
in.

“Where are you going?” asked Brian.

“I am going to Lothbury, to the Telegraph Office, to stop your work, if
I can.”

“Let me go with you.”

“No;” and Mr. Pryor banged the door of the Hansom and was off.

Brian had never seen him angry before, never out of temper; and as he
stood there alone on the edge of the broad pavement, between the Seven
Sisters Road and the railway bridge, he began to feel that he had indeed
done more evil than he could readily undo, that he had let his revenge
get the better not merely of his charity but of his prudence, and that
unless he could give some assistance in the matter he should feel
wretched for life.

Wretched for life! Did he know the meaning of the words he was
whispering to his own soul? had he other than the vaguest comprehension
of the misery he had wrought both for others and himself?

Flogged—branded! Somehow he derived no sort of satisfaction from the
idea of his enemy being punished now.

He had kept his pebble and turned it, taken it out and slung it, and
struck his foe in the very place where it had delighted him to think the
blow would take effect. But now that it had taken effect—now that his
enemy was worsted, that his pebble was gone out of his own keeping, that
he had taken his own way, and revenged himself at once on the man who
had cast them out of Kincorth, and the boy who had damaged Harold’s
character past repair—he could not help asking himself what good there
was likely to come of it all to her, or him, or them.

To him or her? Should he go up to the churchyard sloping towards the
west and tell the earth how he had taken one step forward to the end he
had laid out for himself? Should he whisper to the dead that he had made
the living wretched for their sakes? Should he tell the wind that
stirred the grass growing green on Harold’s grave how the delinquent was
at last discovered, how he had set the officers of justice on his track?
Would not the dead be gratified; could not he make them hear that; would
not the words bring them back to life? Had not he well obeyed his
mother’s dying injunction, that he should forgive as he hoped to be
forgiven; and from the heavens would she not look approvingly at the
base act of vindictive hatred of which he had been guilty?

Dimly as he walked along all this passed through his mind; vaguely he
began to see he had chosen the wrong path, that others had been right,
that he had been wrong. Somewhere he had heard of a better way of
avenging oneself upon an enemy than by branding and scourging, of a way
which did not raise every feeling of a man’s heart into ten times worse
hatred and antagonism, but which instead “heaped coals of fire upon his
head.” Nothing he could do—nothing—nothing for ever might comfort or
soothe the departed: the “dull cold ear of death” could hearken to no
words that it was in his power to frame: if he killed Maxwell and laid
his body on their graves, what could it profit them? Could he bring them
back again? and if he could, oh God! if he could, would it not be awful
to see them rise up and rejoice because their enemy was brought low?

Very speedily Brian made up his mind he would follow Mr. Pryor, hear
what he had done, and then, if need were, start that night for Ireland.

He might be of some use, he might be able to render some assistance; and
“I declare to you, Mr. Pryor,” he said, “that if I could retrace my
steps I would; if I could take that night back and act my part in it
over again, I would not do one thing I have done, not one.”

“Even a savage would not have been so treacherous, so inhospitable,”
answered Mr. Pryor.

“I know it,” was the reply; “but are we always able to argue out such
questions coolly? Does the man live who has never believed he has seen
clearly walking through the darkness?”

 CHAPTER XIX.
 Through Connemara.


Maxwell Drewitt sat alone in the dining-room at Kincorth, waiting for
his son; he had waited many days and yet the fugitive came not, neither
had any tidings of Arthur reached him.

He had watched for the post, he had strained his ears to catch the sound
of horses’ feet, the noise of coming wheels. He had risen early, and
gone to bed late; he had lain through the night wakeful and restless,
and still there arrived no tidings; he had no data to go upon except Mr.
Pryor’s telegram which he held in his hand.

Sorrow had told upon him; the suspense, the agony, the alternations of
hope and fear had broken him down more and more. He was old—old and
stricken; and as he crouched over the fire, which a damp autumnal
evening had rendered acceptable, I think a more hopeless picture of the
vanity of human wishes, of the end of human desires, could not have been
presented for consideration.

Where was the erect carriage, the bold, defiant glance, the indolent
grace of movement, the insolent overbearing manner? Gone, friends,
gone—like the raven hue of his own glossy hair; like the cold brightness
of his eyes; like the beauty he had been proud of, and that women had
been won by; gone—gone for ever.

The passionless sneer had left his lips and given place to a sort of
irrepressible convulsion. He could not walk now as he had walked in
former years along the shore of Duranmore Bay, kicking the pebbles
before him; but when at last he arose from his chair, in order to make
his way to one of the windows, he had to support himself with a stick
and to catch at chairs and tables as he passed by.

It was a wild night; the equinoctial gales were blowing their fiercest,
and Maxwell, standing leaning against the window-frame, thought of how
the waves must be lashing up against Eversbeg Head; how the billows must
be raging and tossing outside the bay.

There was a something so dreary in the idea of that limitless ocean;
those thousands of miles of tempestuous waters; that great expanse of
sea without an island, without a shore, without a rock; those great
deeps, shouting under the night to one another, while the floods clapped
their hands—that Maxwell could not bear it; he had to turn from the
window, and make his way feebly to the hearth, and stir up the fire, and
try to think of a more cheerful subject.

But what cheerful subject had he to think of—this man, who had lived for
the midday, and not for the night?

He could think of his boy, but of nothing else—of his boy, who, all
unknown to him, was fleeing through Connemara towards Kincorth; who was
trying to outstrip his pursuers, whom Ryan had promised to mislead, if
possible, at Oughterard.

Through the night he was riding as if for his life; while the wind
howled down among the mountains and through the valleys, and washed the
waters of the lakes over the low stone walls that are in some places all
that separates them from the highway—he rode on.

He had lost his hat about six miles on the Duranmore side of Oughterard,
and now, as he galloped through the darkness, the wind tossed his hair
hither and thither, now leaving his forehead bare, and again blinding
him with locks that were dead-black as Harold’s had been. On—on—without
a pause, except when the steepness of the ground necessitated pulling up
his horse and walking—on past the giant mountains; past the eternal
lakes; past the long low line of hills that lay between him and the sea;
past bogs and morasses; over mountain-streams, over brawling
torrents—still on through the darkness towards Kincorth.

Having gained the Calgillan road, he dismounted, unfastened his girths,
took the saddle off his good steed’s back, and the bridle off his head,
and then turned the animal adrift. Saddle and bridle he carried till he
reached the plantations of Kincorth, when he left the main road, and
hiding his belongings deep in the underwood, made his way across the
fields towards the house.

“They are after me, father,” were his first words; “but I have got the
start. Mr. Ryan said you were not to keep me here, but forward me on to
Durrow. I don’t know my way there.”

He stood in the centre of the room while he spoke, with the firelight
flickering over his pale face, with his straight hair tangled and
matted, with a weary, anxious look on his countenance, with a hunted
expression in his dark eyes.

“Who let you in?” Maxwell asked. It was no time for the interchange of
much ceremony—for many words to be wasted.

“Dickson; and I have warned him.”

“Go into the next room for a minute while I ring the bell. Bring wine,”
Maxwell ordered, “and, Dickson, come here for a moment.”

“Yes, sir.”

“Is Flynn gone?”

“He leaves at six, you know, sir, usually, and it is now eleven.”

“Could you put the roan horse in the car?”

“Yes, sir.”

“The other servants are gone to-bed, I suppose?”

“All, except the cook, sir.”

“Take the horse and car round to the back avenue, and then sit up for
me. If anybody comes here asking where I am, say I have gone to Lord
Marsden’s to dinner, and will not be back till late—perhaps not till
morning, and—and—you know the rest, Dickson.”

“Yes, sir. I am to say Mr. Arthur has not been here.”

“Now go. Of course you will sit up for me. Should there be danger, light
a candle in the blue room, and I will be on my guard; you understand?”

“Perfectly, sir. But surely you are not going out to-night; excuse me,
sir, but——”

“Necessity knows no law,” finished Maxwell Drewitt, with something of
his old decision of manner. “Now, Arthur, drink some wine, to put life
into you; for it may be many a night before you are able to put your
head under a roof again.” And while his son followed his advice in
silence, the wretched father sat and looked at the only child he ever
had.

“Surely you are not coming with me?” said Arthur, as his father arose
and intimated it was time for them to depart. “It is an awful night,
cold, and bleak, and threatening for rain. Let me try to find my way
alone, if you would rather not trust Dickson.”

“I am not afraid of trusting Dickson,” was the reply; “but I must leave
some one to answer their inquiries should they come.” Arthur shuddered;
too well he knew who _they_ meant. “And as for the night, I shall be
better out; the cold air will not hurt me.”

Would not it? He knew better; but he knew, also, that anything would be
preferable to remaining at home in suspense; and, besides, after all,
whom could he trust so well as himself—who could keep the secret like he
and Arthur?

Through the night they pressed on together—father and son.

As they drove along that interminable road towards Durrow, Maxwell could
not help thinking, that at some previous time he had traversed it under
circumstances similar to the present; and all at once, as they drew near
Castle Cronach, he remembered what Mr. Gyton had said about riding for
one’s life through Connemara; and about how a man who was doing so would
curse those bends and twists and curves.

There are some things which impress us with such a sense of reality—with
such a feeling that at a future day we shall pass through what we fancy
then—as gives us the idea, when the future day arrives, that we have not
prefigured the event, but that it has actually happened at some former
period.

This was Maxwell’s state of mind, at all events; and as the keen wind
swept by—as the rain began pattering in his face—as he felt the nipping
night air blowing through and through him, he could not help
occasionally marvelling which was the dream, and which the reality—which
was the shadow, and which the substance. Had he ever ridden along that
road with three companions? Was he driving it in very truth then? Had he
ever been strong enough to ride? Was he only imagining he was driving
now? Were there people following them? Did he hear the sound of horses’
hoofs behind?

“It is only the wind,” Arthur kept saying; but Maxwell lashed the roan
on for all that.

How long the road was! How he did curse those curves! how he did
anathematize the engineer who planned, and the men who made it! How
every winding seemed to double itself in length! how every mile appeared
to quadruple itself in distance! But there, at last, thank God! at last,
was Durrow!

They soon knocked Mr. Connor up, and explained to him the state of the
case.

“You are on good terms with the fishermen, Connor,” said Mr. Drewitt;
“get Skerritt to stow Arthur away till he can be taken off in some
smuggling craft. Make it all right with the men; and say, I don’t mind
five hundred pounds when I hear he is safe in France. It is more than
you are worth, boy,” he added. “Don’t you think, now, you have been more
trouble than profit to me, all your life long?”

His voice shook and trembled as he spoke; and Mr. Connor drew back,
knowing they were about to say farewell to one another.

“It will be safest for us to leave you in their hands entirely,”
explained Mr. Drewitt; “I am not afraid of any informers among them.
Write to me, when you get clear off. You will have money and clothes
provided for you on board the vessel. If I can, I will join you in
France; but do not attempt to come back to these countries. And now
good-bye, for the present. Good-bye!”

He held out his hand—he had never been much given to caressing, and it
was the only mode of leave-taking he knew; but Arthur fell on his neck
and wept; and Maxwell felt those tears dropping down into his heart, one
by one.

It had been a cold heart and a dead—it had lain numb through life, only
to waken up with pain and grief towards the last; and now he felt not
only his son’s tears softening him, but the memory of other tears he had
seen flow—of other sobs he had heard in the night—of other anguish he
had let go away unsoothed—came up before him then—the ghosts of dead
realities, the shadows of things that had all once been.

A few seconds and it was over; that was no time at which to waste
precious moments in grief or in adieux; and while Connor took Arthur
with him down to the shore, Maxwell drove back to Kincorth through the
night alone.

That seemed to be his doom—alone—alone for ever—in adversity as in
prosperity—at the end as in the beginning. But in the beginning solitude
had not seemed terrible as it did towards the end; and that fact made
all the difference.

Through that desolate wilderness he drove back, as I have said, alone;
past great stretches of country, across which the wind went howling and
howling as though, with shrieks and laughter, the storm were running
races over the flat tracts of moorland. For a little distance the road
wound along by the side of Durrow River, where the chesnut had died
taking her leap so gallantly, and then great mountains hemmed him in for
a mile or so, when they swept grandly off to right and left, leaving the
traveller breathing space once more.

It is a grand country, this Connemara, but desolate beyond all power of
description; the ends of the world seem to be reached when you are
journeying through it; and even when a person has no pressing care, no
special cause for unhappiness, when he is strong and free from anxiety
of mind, there is a weirdness and a solemnity about a night-ride through
his part of that land of the Wild West which must be felt to be
comprehended.

What, then, must that solitary drive have proved to Maxwell Drewitt,
whose strength was turned into weakness, whose powers of endurance were
not equal to such an undertaking—who, racked with mental and bodily
pain, prostrated by physical suffering, fancied, now that the mountains
were advancing towards him—now that the road was slipping away—now that
the air was full of strange sounds—and again that through the valleys
and up the defiles were coming hundreds and hundreds of armed men
seeking after his son?

It was fortunate that the roan knew his way to his stable, knew every
turn in the road, every point of danger, every awkward curve, the
position almost of every block of granite, or else Maxwell could
scarcely have guided him home.

As it was, between two and three o’clock in the morning, when the wind
was a little lulled, and the rain coming down in torrents, the unhappy
man reached Kincorth, and lifting his eyes instinctively to the windows,
saw no light in them.

“You must be just drowned, sir,” said Dickson, as he helped him off the
car and took the reins out of his trembling hand and assisted him into
the house; “I have a good fire in your own room, sir, and you had better
get off your wet things and go straight away to bed.”

“Who is that, Dickson, who is that?” Maxwell whispered, seeing that a
man had taken the horse’s head and was leading him round towards the
stables. “There was no light burning—who is it?”

“No one, sir, but Mr. Brian Drewitt, who came in not ten minutes after
you left. He said he would put up the roan, and set everything right for
the morning, so that I could stay with you. He seems dreadfully
downhearted, and he hasn’t put bite nor sup in his lips since he entered
the doors.”

“Go to him, Dickson, and tell him not to lay a finger on any horse of
mine; tell him that he shall not stay in my house, nor meddle in my
concerns. It is owing to him that my son is a fugitive this night,
without a place to lay his head in. He has come to gloat over me, to
exult in my defeat; but he shall not stay here; he shall not, by——!”

Very unceremoniously, Dickson marched his master off to bed.

“I don’t think, sir,” he answered, “that you rightly know what you are
talking about. I never saw a young gentleman seem in less notion of
exulting, as you call it, than that same Mr. Brian was to-night; and I
am certain sure if Mr. Arthur had been his own brother he could not have
appeared more cut up than he was. When you’re warm in your bed, just see
him, and hear what he has got to say; and if you don’t think then he
means fair, why tell me to put him out, and if I don’t do it my name is
not Michael Dickson. And sure, is not he making himself of use at this
very minute, washing the car and rubbing down the horse, and doing a
stable-boy’s work, only better than any stable-boy ever did it? I know
what Mr. Brian can do. Many a time when I used to be coming up here, I
saw him at work just like a labourer. Let him stay, sir, let him stay;
we’ll want him yet.”

“It was he gave the information,” muttered Maxwell.

“So he told me; and he told me why he did it, and how sorry he had been
since. He talked to me for an hour in the dining-room there—for an hour
at the least. And he spoke about his mother, and Mr. Harold, so that it
would have melted the heart of a stone.”

With a shudder, Maxwell Drewitt turned his head away, and laid it on the
furthermost pillow.

“Let him come,” he said, faintly; “it may be that we have cross accounts
to settle; let him come;” and Brian came accordingly.

“Maxwell,” he began, “I am here to help you, if I can. I am here to make
what poor reparation is in my power. There was a time,” he went on,
“when I swore I never would re-enter your doors at peace with you: over
my dead mother’s body, I renewed that oath; and I swore then that if
ever I had a chance of vengeance offered me, I would avail myself of
that chance to the uttermost. Maxwell, I have recalled those oaths. I
would undo that which I have done if I could. I want to help you, if in
my power. Had I known who Arthur’s mother really was, I would have cut
out my tongue before bringing sorrow upon her. Do you believe me,
Maxwell? I never spoke falsely to you in my life; and you may believe it
is the truth I am telling you now.”

There ensued a long silence, broken only by the sick man’s sobs. “I am
so ill,” he said, “I am so weak—Brian, I thank you—I will take your
offer, I will trust you. If you will, there shall be peace between us at
last. Do you forgive me?”

“You are pressing me hard, Maxwell,” answered the other. “Hearing what I
hear, guessing what I guess; with my dear ones where no reparation can
be made to them, I cannot say I forgive. But I may try to forgive; will
that content you?”

“And you will stay with me, Brian, you will stay. I am so sick, and I am
so lonely. It was a fall off the chesnut did it; but I suppose you heard
all about that?”

“Yes, poor Madcap!”

“It was not her fault, it was that fool of a Crabbe—it has made wild
work with me, Brian, has not it? I shall never be a man again while I
live—never!”

While he lived. Brian, keeping watch by the sick man’s bedside, knew
that his existence could not be prolonged much longer, and looking at
that poor wreck of mortality—at that old worn man—he found it hard to
identify him with the Maxwell he had last seen standing just within the
hall door of that house in which he was at length master.

Then he had been handsome, strong, resolute; now he was a stricken and
hesitating creature, dying by inches, while there was not a human being
related to him, by any close ties of blood, to take his hand and bid him
be of good cheer, as he glided onward to that valley where is the
blackness of the shadow of death.

 CHAPTER XX.
 Brian’s Repentance.


Before many days Mr. Pryor appeared at Durrow, anxious to know what had
become of Arthur, and how Brian and his cousin were settling their old
scores.

As for Arthur, Mr. Connor informed him the lad was still lying concealed
in one of the caves in Durrow cliff—“the secretest of all their
hiding-places, Skerritt informs me,” he proceeded—“a cave that no gauger
or coast-guardsman could find out unless he were the devil.”

“Then as I never heard of the devil taking the form of an exciseman, and
going about the country looking after private stills and cargoes of
smuggled goods, I suppose we may conclude there is every chance of the
young gentleman getting off safe.”

“He’ll get off right enough, the first time the schooner comes round
this way again. The reward Mr. Drewitt has offered ought to put the men
on their mettle—not, to do them justice, that they need such an
inducement—it’s enough for them to know that a man is flying from the
constituted authorities, and they will strain every nerve to assist him
to escape.”

“That characteristic of your country-people it is which makes Ireland so
much pleasanter a residence for rogues than for honest men,” remarked
Mr. Pryor.

“Ah, well, sir; sure you see by this that even very respectable fathers
may have sons in trouble.”

“I hope you do not call Mr. Drewitt a respectable father, because, in my
opinion, he is nothing of the kind,” answered Mr. Pryor.

“He is fond of the boy, at any rate,” said Connor, simply; and his
thoughts flew back to that night when through the storm Maxwell drove
his son over to Durrow, and had stood silent while Arthur wept tears of
penitence upon his neck.

“He’ll never get the better of it, Mr. Brian tells me,” went on the
agent, pursuing his own train of reflection. “He was wet to the skin,
and has not been able to be out of bed since. Well, let him have been
what he will, Mr. Pryor, you would be sorry for him if you were to see
him now—just a shadow of himself, and with the grey hairs more plentiful
than the black, and crow’s feet about his eyes, and a drop of the mouth,
and a twitching of the face that you would wonder what ailed him.”

“I think I’ll go over and see him,” observed Mr. Pryor.

“Indeed, sir, and I just would,” agreed Connor; “maybe the day’s not so
very far off when you’ll be glad to remember you did it.”

“Do you mean, Connor, you really think he is going to die so soon as all
that comes to?”

“Think, sir! I am sure I believe in my heart he’ll never rise from his
bed again; that he did his last piece of work in this world, the night
he came over here with Mr. Arthur. I did all I could to get him to stop,
and so did my wife, too; but he was so mortally afraid of any of the
Duranmore people guessing where his son was hid away, that nothing would
serve him but round with the horse’s head and back to Kincorth, though
the wind was coming down from among those hills, and blowing off the
Atlantic, fit to tear the house down. And then it began pelting with
rain. I don’t know how he ever got home, and him in the state of health
he was—I do not.”

Next morning Mr. Pryor walked over the hills to Kincorth, to pay his
visit to Maxwell Drewitt.

“He will see you if you come upstairs,” Brian said, in answer to the
Englishman’s inquiries; “but I must warn you that he is very
ill—dangerously ill, in my opinion. I have thought it right to tell Lady
Emmeline what I believe about him; and it is very probable she may be
here to-morrow afternoon.”

“Are you glad you came, Brian?” asked Mr. Pryor.

“I would not have come for all the property Maxwell owns;” and Brian,
who was head nurse in the sick-room, led his friend gently to the
darkened chamber, which Maxwell was never again to leave till he was
carried out in his coffin.

“You were right about me, Mr. Pryor,” said Maxwell. “I was not an honest
man; and I did not come into Kincorth fairly; but Brian shall get it
back again when I am gone; and I hope he will have more enjoyment of it
than ever I had.”

“I trust,” answered Mr. Pryor, “you will live for many a long year to
keep him out of it. Brian will be better and happier working for his
living in London than he ever could be dawdling about Kincorth.”

He spoke these words cheerfully and heartily, but Maxwell shook his
head.

“My race is run,” he said, “my day almost finished; and if I could but
once know the boy was safe, I am so tired that I think I would lay my
head down and never raise it again.”

“You will think differently when once he is clear off,” answered Mr.
Pryor.

“Shall I?” and Maxwell’s hand strayed idly and listlessly over the
coverlet, picking bits of cotton out of it while he spoke.

“Why of course you will; anxiety is the chief thing that is the matter
with you now.”

“Is it?” and the poor wasted fingers still kept picking, picking.

“I don’t know what I should have done without him,” he said, after a
short pause, turning his eyes towards Brian. “I could not have lived so
long had I been alone. He has been gentler to me than any woman I ever
had about me; and when I look at him I think of his mother, and believe
she has forgiven me——”

“You can clear that matter up, about Harold,” suggested Mr. Pryor.

“Yes, when I am gone, and when Arthur is off, you will find a parcel and
letter in that safe over there. You can make what use you like of both;
of course they would not take any proceedings at this distance of time,
but you will settle that with them, won’t you?”

“And I mean to settle that you shall not talk any more at present, and
that if Mr. Pryor comes here to discuss unpleasant topics he must be
forbidden the house,” broke in Brian.

“Will you come to my house, then, to-morrow, to dinner?” asked Mr.
Pryor; and Brian promised that if his patient were no worse, he would;
having received which assurance, Mr. Pryor walked back over the hills as
he had come, and noticed as he walked a wicked-looking little schooner
tacking and tacking between Duranmore Point and Durrow. “That is our
friend,” he thought, “and twenty-four hours more will see Arthur out of
the country.”

“Yes, sir, that’s her,” remarked Skerritt with a knowing wink. “She is
only making believe tacking about there; she is really bound for Mayo,
and then she will be back here about this time to-morrow. We have sent
word to the captain, and it will be all correct.”

Mr. Pryor was truly rejoiced to hear it, and said so. It was wretched to
think of that lad lying in the cave night after night while he slept
comfortably in a good house close at hand; and his sympathy was strongly
excited for the unhappy father, whose first question when he awoke in
the mornings was always, “Has Arthur got off yet?” and whose last sigh
at night sounded like a moan for the son who had made such grievous
shipwreck of his life.

“You will bring me back a report of how things are going on, Brian,” he
said as his cousin was leaving for Durrow; “a true report,
remember,—promise me that;” and Brian promised ere he departed.

It was a lovely afternoon in the early part of October; the trees about
Kincorth were getting very bare of foliage, but there were still enough
leaves left to make the woods look very beautiful in their autumnal
deckings of brown and orange and gold. The sky was clear and bright, and
though there was a stiff breeze blowing, Brian after long watching in
his cousin’s sick room found the wind only make his walk the pleasanter.

He did not feel in bad spirits as he climbed the hill and pursued the
short cut towards Durrow; rather, on the contrary, his heart was lighter
than it had been for many a day past, for he began to be certain Arthur
would effect his escape safely, and he knew if the youth were once
landed in France that a load of anxiety would be lifted from his
shoulders, a burden of self-reproach taken off his soul.

It was a delicious afternoon; there was just that feeling of chilliness
in the air which is pleasant when a man who can walk fast has likewise
to walk far; and Brian hurried over the ground feeling that there are
some days in the year when living is a pleasure, and that this was one
of them.

But yet, as he drew near Durrow, he fancied he saw on the side of the
hill, looking seaward, something which alarmed him—a man with a
telescope. Brian immediately charged down the incline, and came upon a
commonplace-looking individual apparently engaged in digging up bait.

It seemed a strange place to select for such a purpose; but of course
the man had a right to choose an inconvenient position if he chose, and
Brian remained talking with him for a few minutes while he looked about
to see if he could discover any trace of the telescope or discover what
part of Durrow was best commanded from the spot.

The whole of Durrow village was concealed by Durrow cliff, and there was
nothing to be discerned except the south side of the principal rock,
which rose sheer out of the sea which now washed its base.

Any trace of the telescope he failed to detect, though he could have
sworn he saw the brass tube glitter for a moment in the afternoon sun.

“He was there, was he, the divil!” remarked Skerritt, whom Brian at once
informed of the circumstance. “I wish I had caught him at his spying.
You’re not sure he was spying, arn’t ye? Well, I am, and that’s enough.
Digging earth-worms, too, for a make-believe! Pity he wasn’t feedin’
them, that’s all I have got to say.”

“Will it hinder Arthur getting away?” asked Brian, anxiously.

“Will it hinder the say comin’ in to-morrow morning?” inquired Mr.
Skerritt, which, being decidedly an unlikely result, Brian took heart
again.

“Are you going out fishing to-night, Skerritt?” inquired Brian, noticing
that the man was preparing his nets and getting his boat ready for sea.

“I go out fishing most nights,” was the answer; “and now, sir, if you
are as tired of asking questions as I am of answerin’ them, I wish ye
just would go up till the house, and stay in your proper place, which is
up there with the quality,[2] instead of down here with me.”

Footnote 2:

  Gentlefolks.

“Your best friends can’t say much for your civility, Skerritt,” remarked
Brian as he moved away.

“If I am not very civil I am very wise,” was the significant answer; and
Brian departed on the instant.

“I wish to-night was over—and well over,” said Mr. Pryor, as they stood
together on the lawn, while the afternoon light faded away, and the
twilight deepened, and the darkness drew on.

“And so do I. Have you any notion at what hour they mean to make their
venture?” returned Brian.

“Not the least. Skerritt would give me no information on the subject,
and I have not seen a sign of the schooner to-day. The first
intelligence I expect to hear is that he is off safe and sound, and
half-way to France.”

“Heaven grant it!” Brian replied.

The words were scarcely out of his lips before there arose such a shout
from the shore, as made Mr. Pryor and Brian exclaim, at the same moment—

“There is something wrong: let us go down;” and they ran round to the
shelving side of the cliff, and hurried down the path leading to the
sea.

“They have found the cave,” explained Skerritt; “and they are all here,
police, and gaugers, and soldiers, and all.”

“And where is Arthur?”

“You’ll see in a minit,” was the reply. “Now Patsey, my man. Run down
for your life, Mr. Arthur, and out into the sea!” he cried in a voice
like thunder, and, at the same instant, there flared out from the shore
a flame of light that just flashed across the waters and then was
extinguished.

Quick as thought, another light glanced out from the little schooner,
which had been quietly stealing round a line of rocks that stretched far
away into the ocean; and, next moment, while his eyes were still dazzled
by the sudden glare, Brian heard the plash of oars, and knew that Arthur
was safe in the boat and making for the vessel that alone could ever
bear him to freedom.

Hoarsely the command for the boat to stop rung out through the night,
and, as a mocking laugh came back across the waters, the corporal gave
the order to fire.

Three balls went instantly whizzing over the sea; and then there was a
shriek such as Brian never had heard in all his life before—the shriek
of a strong man in his death agony.

But the boat went on.

“Fire!” was again the command, and again the balls sought their mark: no
shriek followed this discharge, and, for a minute or two, the corporal
seemed to think his men must have made some mistake, but it was only for
a moment.

The next, cries for help were borne by the wind to the shore, and there
were frantic entreaties among the women that a boat might be launched on
the instant.

“Lord help you, mother,” said Skerritt’s son; “don’t you remember every
boat was sent to Duranmore, that they might not be able to get the
advantage over us that way? but then if he is not hit he can swim. There
it is again! That’s not my father’s shout, it is the young fellow’s, and
the boat is settling.”

The boat was settling. Every one of the balls, except that which struck
Skerritt, had gone through her side. There can be no doubt but that the
men must have happened to fire as she rose on the waves; and now, with
their hands loosely holding their muskets, they stood listening to the
screams of the drowning men.

“I can’t bear this,” cried Brian; “I must do something;” and he made a
rush towards the sea, but half a dozen people were before him in an
instant, and kept him back from his design. “Have you not got a boat?”
he gasped.

“Not one. Do you think if we had, it’s myself would be talking to you
now, and my own father and brother out there in the middle of it all?”

“How many were there altogether?”

“Just three; and, out of the three, there were two of us.”

“God pity you,” said Brian, as he sat down on the shore. “And God pity
me, too,” he added to himself, “for this is my doing.”

There was silence on the sea—silence only broken by the whistling of the
wind—by the plashing of the waves against the rocks.

Each moment it was growing darker and darker, and though the people
under the cliffs were running hither and thither with lighted torches in
their hands, still nothing could be seen of the tragedy that had just
been enacted.

Sullenly the sea came rolling up on the shore—sullenly it swept along,
wave succeeding to wave, and billow to billow: though they strained
their eyes out into the night they could see nothing but a waste of
water, but an eternity of ocean.

Till morning dawned they would not be able to distinguish any object,
